\mathrm{exp}_{\varepsilon}(\xi,X):=(\xi,\mathrm{exp}((1-P_{\varepsilon}(\xi))X)\mathrm{exp}(P_{\varepsilon}(\xi)X)).

The fact that the exponential is a diffeomorphism implies that
\mathrm{exp}_{\varepsilon}
is a diffeomorphism. We denote its inverse by
\log_{\varepsilon}:\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathsf{G}\to\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathfrak{g}
.

We define the map
\rho_{\varepsilon}
by the property that

\log_{\varepsilon}\circ\rho_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathrm{exp}_{\varepsilon}=P_{\varepsilon},

as mappings
\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathfrak{g}
. It is readily verified that this mapping has the required properties.
∎

Proposition 0.5.4
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be a Jordan-Hölder basis, take an
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
and define

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}:=\{(\xi,f):\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon},\;f\in\mathcal{H}_{F_{V,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O},\xi),\xi}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi})\}.

Then there is a unique topology on
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
such that

(1)

The projection mapping
p_{1}:\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\to\Xi_{\varepsilon}
defines a trivializable bundle of Hilbert spaces with fibre

p_{1}^{-1}(\xi)=\mathcal{H}_{F_{V,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O},\xi),\xi}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi})\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
d=\#\varepsilon_{n}/2
.

(2)

For all compact
K\subseteq\Xi_{\varepsilon}
, the space of sections
C(K,\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})
densely contains the space of sections of the form
\xi\mapsto(\xi,V_{\xi}f)
for
f\in C^{\infty}(K\times\mathsf{G})
such that
f(\xi,gh)=\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(h)f(\xi,g)
for all
\xi\in K
,
g\in\mathsf{G}
,
h\in\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
, and that
f
is of Schwarz class modulo
\mathsf{H}_{V}
.

(3)

The fibrewise
\mathsf{G}
-action on
\mathcal{H}_{F_{V,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O},\xi),\xi}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi})
extends to a strongly continuous,
C(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-linear
\mathsf{G}
-action on the space of continuous section
C(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

By
[
123
, Theorem 5.1.1]
there exists rational functions
q_{1}^{\varepsilon},\ldots q_{d}^{\varepsilon}
on
\mathfrak{g^{*}}
whose restrictions to
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
are smooth such that the mappings

q_{j,\varepsilon}:\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathsf{G}\to\mathbb{C},\quad q_{j,\varepsilon}(\xi,g)=q_{j}^{\varepsilon}(g\xi),

are smooth and satisfy that
q_{j,\varepsilon}(\xi,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}(\mathsf{G})
are
\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
-invariant. For a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we let
h_{\alpha}
denote the associated Hermite function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define the smooth functions
\tilde{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}:\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathsf{G}\to\mathbb{C}
by

{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\xi,g)=h_{\alpha}(q_{1,\varepsilon}(\xi,g),\ldots,q_{d,\varepsilon}(\xi,g))\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(\rho_{\varepsilon}(g)),

where
\rho_{\varepsilon}
is the function constructed in Proposition
0.5.3
.

By Proposition
0.5.3
, we have that

\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(\rho_{\varepsilon}(gh))=\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(\rho_{\varepsilon}(g))\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(h),

for all
\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}
,
g\in\mathsf{G}
,
h\in\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
. It now follows from the properties of
q_{1}^{\varepsilon},\ldots q_{d}^{\varepsilon}
that for each compact
K\subseteq\Xi_{\varepsilon}
, the function
q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(K\times\mathsf{G})
satisfies that

{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\xi,gh)=\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(h){q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\xi,g),

for all
\xi\in K
,
g\in\mathsf{G}
,
h\in\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
, and that
\tilde{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}
is of Schwarz class modulo
\mathsf{H}_{V}
. It follows from the construction, and that
q_{1}^{\varepsilon},\ldots q_{d}^{\varepsilon}
define canonical coordinates on each orbit, that for each
\xi
, the sequence
(V_{\xi}\tilde{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\xi,\cdot))_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
is an ON-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{F_{V,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O},\xi),\xi}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi})
.

We define a topology on
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
by for each compact
K\subseteq\Xi_{\varepsilon}
declaring a section
f:K\to\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
to be continuous if and only if the function

\tilde{f}:K\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\tilde{f}(\xi)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\left\langle V_{\xi}{q}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\xi,\cdot),f(\xi)\right\rangle h_{\alpha},

is continuous. By construction, Item 1 and 2 of the theorem holds. We note that the map
f\mapsto\tilde{f}
defines a global trivialization
T_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Item 3 follows from the next lemma.
∎

We let
C^{\infty}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{U}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
denote the topological group of mappings
\Xi_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{U}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
that are smooth for the strong operator topology.

Lemma 0.5.5
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be a Jordan-Hölder basis, take an
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
and consider the trivialization

T_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\to\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

constructed in the proof of Proposition
0.5.4
, where
d=\#\varepsilon_{n}/2
. Let
\pi_{\varepsilon,0}=(\pi_{\xi})_{\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}}
denote the family of unitary representations of
\mathsf{G}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
induced from the fibrewise
\mathsf{G}
-action on
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
and
T_{\varepsilon}
.

There exists smooth functions
a_{\varepsilon,j}\in C^{\infty}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathfrak{g})
, for
j=0,\ldots,d
, that are rational in
\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}
, polynomial in
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and linear in
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, such that ,

(D\pi_{\xi}(X)f)(t)=ia_{\varepsilon,0}(\xi,t,X)f(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{\varepsilon,j}(\xi,t,X)\frac{\partial f}{\partial t_{j}}(t),

for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}
and
X\in\mathfrak{g}
. Moreover, there exists
h_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{G})
and
k_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{G},\mathbb{R}^{d})
that are rational in
\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}
, polynomial in
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and polynomial in
g\in\mathsf{G}
such that

(\pi_{\xi}(g)f)(t)=\mathrm{e}^{ih_{\varepsilon}(\xi,t,g)}f(t+k_{\varepsilon}(\xi,t,g)).

In particular,
\pi_{\varepsilon,0}
defines a continuous homomorphism

\pi_{\varepsilon,0}:\mathsf{G}\to C^{\infty}(\Xi_{k},\mathcal{U}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))).

We omit the proof of this lemma and refer the reader to
[
105
, Lemma 2.8 and Proposition 2.11]
. See also
[
123
, Theorem 2.7.2]
.

To place this construction in a more
C^{*}
-algebraic context, we will define a
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module that will capture the bundle
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
.

We define the space
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
to consist of all
f\in C^{\infty}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}\times\mathsf{G})
such that
f(\xi,g)=\chi_{\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi),\xi}(h)f(g)
for all
\xi\in\Xi_{\varepsilon}
,
g\in\mathsf{G}
,
h\in\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
, and that
f
is compactly supported in the
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
-direction and of Schwarz class modulo
\mathsf{H}_{V}
in the
\mathsf{G}
-direction. On
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
, we define a
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-valued inner product as

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle(\xi):=\int_{\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)}\overline{f_{1}(\xi,g)}f_{2}(\xi,g)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi},\quad\mbox{for $f_{1},f_{2}\in\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}$},

where
\mu_{\xi}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{H}_{V}(\xi)
defined from exponentiating the Lebesgue measure on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)
defined from the Jordan-Hölder basis. In fact,
\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathpzc{H}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
. We let
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
denote the completion of
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
as a
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert module.

We note that any
f\in\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
canonically defines a smooth compactly supported section of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
via the map
V_{\xi}
from Equation (
5
). We denote the associated injection by

\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}).

(8)

Proposition 0.5.6
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
. The map
\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}
extends to a unitary isomorphism of
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules

\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\to C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}).

Proof.

Recall the construction of
(q_{\alpha,\varepsilon})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
and the global trivialization
T_{\varepsilon}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
from the proof of Proposition
0.5.4
. The functions
q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}
defines elements
\langle q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}|\in\mathcal{L}_{C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})}(\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon},C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}))
. We set
|q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}\rangle:=\langle q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}|^{*}\in\mathcal{L}_{C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})}(C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon}),\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})
and note that

\langle q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}|q_{\beta,\varepsilon}\rangle=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\in C_{b}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{M}(C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})).

We can write the identity of
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
as the strictly convergent sum

\mathrm{id}_{\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}|q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}\rangle\langle q_{\alpha,\varepsilon}|.

(9)

It is readily verified that the composition
T_{\varepsilon}\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}
takes the form

T_{\varepsilon}\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}f=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\left\langle q_{\alpha,\varepsilon},f\right\rangle h_{\alpha},

for
f\in\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
. Using Equation (
9
), we conclude that
T_{\varepsilon}\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\to C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a unitary isomorphism of
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules. The proposition now follows from the fact that
T_{\varepsilon}
is a global (unitary) trivialization.
∎

Proposition 0.5.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
. Left translation of
\mathsf{G}
on functions in
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}
defines an action of
\mathsf{G}
on
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}
as unitary, adjointable
C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})
-linear mappings. This action integrates to a
*
-homomorphism

\tilde{\pi}_{V,\varepsilon}:C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathrm{End}^{*}_{C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})}(\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}),

and the unitary isomorphism
\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\to C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})
from Proposition
0.5.6
is
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant. The unitary
\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}
in particular induces a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant
*
-isomorphism

\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})}(\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})\cong C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{K}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})).

Proof.

By construction,
\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}
is
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant as a mapping
\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}^{\infty}_{V,\varepsilon,c}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\Xi_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon})
. The proposition follows from density.
∎

The constructions above take place over
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
, but it can readily be seen from Kirillov’s orbit method that the unitary equivalence class of the
\mathsf{G}
-representation on each fibre remains constant along each orbit. We now restrict our constructions to bundles and modules over the stratas
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
. As explained in Proposition
0.5.1
, the Jordan-Hölder basis defines a homeomorphism
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\cong\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
and we can thusly consider
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
as a subset of
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
. We define the locally trivial bundle of Hilbert spaces

\mathcal{H}_{V,\varepsilon}:=\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}|_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}\to\Gamma_{\varepsilon},

and the
C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module

\mathpzc{H}_{V,\varepsilon}:=\tilde{\mathpzc{H}}_{V,\varepsilon}\otimes_{C_{0}(\Xi_{\varepsilon})}C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon}).

We note that Proposition
0.5.6
implies that
\tilde{V}_{\varepsilon}
from Equation (
8
) induces a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant unitary isomorphism of
C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon})
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules

V_{\varepsilon}:\mathpzc{H}_{V,\varepsilon}\to C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{H}_{V,\varepsilon}).

(10)

The unitary
V_{\varepsilon}
in particular induces a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant
*
-isomorphism

\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,\varepsilon})\cong C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\varepsilon})).

As above, we order the strata
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\ldots
in the fine stratification as above. We use the same notation for
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},\ldots
,
\mathcal{H}_{V,1},\mathcal{H}_{V,2},\ldots
and
\mathpzc{H}_{V,1},\mathpzc{H}_{V,2},\ldots
. We define the ideals
J_{k}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
as

J_{k}:=\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\forall\pi\in\widehat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\cup_{j\leq k}\Gamma_{j}\},

where we identify each
\Gamma_{j}
with a subset of representations under the Kirillov correspondence. We note that these ideals fit into a sequence of ideal inclusions

0\vartriangleleft J_{1}\vartriangleleft J_{2}\vartriangleleft\cdots\vartriangleleft C^{*}(\mathsf{G}).

(11)

We also define the
C^{*}
-algebras
I_{k}:=J_{k}/J_{k-1}
.

Proposition 0.5.8
.

The spectrum of the ideals
J_{k}\vartriangleleft C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
are given by

\widehat{J_{k}}=\cup_{j\leq k}\Gamma_{j}\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}},

and the map
\widehat{J_{k}}\to\widehat{J_{k+1}}
induced from the ideal inclusion
J_{k}\to J_{k+1}
is given by the inclusion

\cup_{j\leq k}\Gamma_{j}\to\cup_{j\leq k+1}\Gamma_{j}.

Proof.

This follows from
[
57
, Proposition 3.2.1]
.
∎

Proposition 0.5.9
.

The
*
-homomorphism

\pi_{V,k}:C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathrm{End}^{*}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k}),

induced from the
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{V,k}
of Proposition
0.5.7
satisfies the following:

(1)

\ker\pi_{V,k}=J_{k-1}

(2)

\pi_{V,k}
restricted to
J_{k}
defines a surjection

\pi_{V,k}:J_{k}\to\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k}).

(3)

\pi_{V,k}
induces an isomorphism

I_{k}\cong\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k}).

In particular, any global trivialization
\mathcal{H}_{V,k}\cong\Gamma_{k}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
together with the unitary from Equation (
10
) together with
\pi_{V,k}
produces canonical
*
-homomorphisms fitting into the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(\mathsf{G})/J_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ C_{0}(\Gamma_{k},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{b}(\Gamma_{k},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

(12)

where the left vertical map is a
*
-isomorphism.

Proof.

We note that item 2 follows from item 1 and 3. To prove item 1, we note that by construction,

\displaystyle\ker\pi_{V,k}

\displaystyle=\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\ \forall\pi\in\Gamma_{k}\}=

\displaystyle=\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\ \forall\pi\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\cup_{j<k}\Gamma_{j}\}=J_{k-1}.

In the middle step used that
\Gamma_{k}\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\cup_{j<k}\Gamma_{j}
is dense (since
\Xi_{k}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}\setminus\cup_{j<k}\Xi_{j}
is Zariski open).

Let us prove item 3. It follows from item 1 and the construction that
\pi_{V,k}
induces a monomorphism
I_{k}\to\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k})
. Proposition
0.5.6
and Proposition
0.5.4
implies that there exists an isomorphism
\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k})\cong C_{0}(\Gamma_{k},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, so the spectrum of
\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k})
is the locally compact Hausdorff space
\Gamma_{k}
. The spectrum of
I_{k}
is
\Gamma_{k}
by Proposition
0.5.8
and
[
57
, Proposition 3.2.1]
. The inclusion
I_{k}\to\mathbb{K}_{C_{0}(\Gamma_{k})}(\mathpzc{H}_{V,k})
induces the identity at the level of spectrum by construction, and item 3 follows.
∎

Remark 0.5.10
.

We note that since the top strata
\Gamma_{1}
is dense, the map
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to C_{b}(\Gamma_{1},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is a
*
-monomorphism.

The results we have discussed were stated in a slightly less explicit form in
[
17
]
. In
[
17
]
it was proven that for any
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
there is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
and for every
\xi\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
a representation
\pi_{\xi}:\mathsf{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon})
such that
\xi=[\pi_{\xi}]
and the map
\xi\mapsto\pi_{\xi}(a)
is norm continuous for every
a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
. The following map is a noncommutative Fourier transform of an element
f\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
:

\mathsf{F}:C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\rightarrow l^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathsf{G}},\mathbb{K}(\oplus_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon})),\ \mathsf{F}(f)(\xi)=\pi_{\xi}(f)\in\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}).

In
[
17
]
it was shown that
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
can be identified with a certain subalgebra of
l^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathsf{G}},\mathbb{K})
. In particular, they constructed the ideals from Equation (
11
), proved that the ideal inclusions came from the ordering on
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
defined in
[
124
]
and proved that there was an isomorphism

I_{\varepsilon}\cong C_{0}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon})).

0.6.
Flat orbits

We will be particularly interested in a special class of nilpotent groups, possessing so called flat orbits. The existence of flat orbits ensure that each orbit in
\Gamma_{1}
is flat. The set of flat orbits, when non-empty, is in practice readily computed and poses a natural candidate for a Zariski open subset of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
that we later on will use in index theory.

Theorem 0.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a coadjoint orbit of a simply connected nilpotent Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Denote the center of the Lie algebra by
\mathfrak{z}
and its annihilator in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by
\mathfrak{z}^{\bot}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a
\xi\in\mathcal{O}
such that
\mathcal{O}=\xi+\mathfrak{z}^{\bot}
.

(2)

The representation
\pi\in\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
corresponding to
\mathcal{O}
is square integrable modulo
\mathfrak{z}
.

(3)

There is a
\xi\in\mathcal{O}
such that
\omega_{\xi}
is non-degenerate on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
.

We refer the proof of this theorem to
[
114
]
,
[
40
]
.

Definition 0.6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a coadjoint orbit of a simply connected nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
. If
\mathcal{O}
satisfies any of the equivalent conditions of Theorem
0.6.1
, we say that
\mathcal{O}
is a flat orbit. We write
\Gamma
for the set of flat orbits.

If there exists a flat orbit, i.e.
\Gamma\neq\emptyset
, we say that
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits.

We remark that the notion of a flat orbit in some places in the literature refers to the orbit being an affine space of the annihilator of the stabilizer Lie algebra. See for instance in
[
40
,
105
]
. We exclusively use the term flat orbit for the case when the stabilizer Lie algebra is the center.

Theorem 0.6.3
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. Then
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits if and only if any of the following equivalent conditions holds:

Geometric:

There exists
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
is flat, i.e.
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}=\xi+\mathfrak{z}^{\bot}
.

Analytic:

There exists
\pi\in\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
which is square integrable modulo
\mathfrak{z}
.

Algebraic:

The center of the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{z}
.

We refer the proof of this theorem to
[
114
]
,
[
40
]
.

A flat orbit is therefore, as a subset of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, an affine space modelled on
\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}
. We remark that if
\mathcal{O}
is a flat orbit, then
\dim(\mathcal{O})=\dim(\mathfrak{g})-\dim(\mathfrak{z})
. In particular, the center having even codimension is a necessary condition for admitting flat orbits, it is by Example
0.9.23
not sufficient.

Proposition 0.6.4
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. Choose the Jordan-Hölder basis
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
used in the fine stratification to satisfy that
\mathfrak{z}
is spanned by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n-2d}
where
2d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
. Then
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits if and only there exists an
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
with
\#\varepsilon_{n}=2d
. In this case,

\Gamma=\bigcup_{\varepsilon:\#\varepsilon_{n}=2d}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{g}_{n-2d}=\mathfrak{z}
, we have for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
k=1,\ldots,n
that

\mathfrak{z}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{k}=\mathfrak{g}_{\min(k,n-2d)}\subseteq\mathsf{stab}(\xi|_{\mathfrak{g}_{k}}).

Therefore
J^{k}_{\xi}\cap\{1,\ldots,n-2d\}=\emptyset
for any
\xi
. The proposition is therefore equivalent to the statement that for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
is flat if and only if
J^{n}_{\xi}=\{n-2d+1,\ldots,n\}
. Clearly, for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
it holds that
J^{n}_{\xi}=\{n-2d+1,\ldots,n\}
if and only if
\mathsf{stab}(\xi)=\mathfrak{g}_{n-2d}=\mathfrak{z}
. The proposition follows from the fact the annihilator of
\omega_{\xi}
is
\mathsf{stab}(\xi)
.
∎

Remark 0.6.5
.

Whenever non-empty, the set
\Gamma
is the top strata in the coarse stratification of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
(see more in
[
17
]
). There are several examples where
\Gamma
only consists of a single fine strata (see examples in Section
0.9
below). A notable example where
\Gamma
consists of multiple fine stratas is the complexification of the Heisenberg group, see Example
0.9.15
.

For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, the antisymmetric form
\omega_{\xi}
descends to an antisymmetric form on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
. It is readily verified that
\xi
defines a flat orbit if and only if
\omega_{\xi}
is a symplectic form on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
. Since the center is an ideal, it is also readily verified that the Pfaffian

\mathsf{Pf}(\xi):=\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}}),\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*},

is constant along orbits. So
\mathsf{Pf}
defines a function
\mathsf{Pf}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}/\mathsf{Ad}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}
. We conclude the following proposition.

Proposition 0.6.6
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. Then
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits if and only if

\mathsf{Pf}(\mathcal{O})\neq 0,

for some orbit
\mathcal{O}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}/\mathsf{Ad}^{*}
. If
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits, the set of flat orbits
\Gamma\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
can be described as

\Gamma=\{\mathcal{O}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}/\mathsf{Ad}^{*}:\mathsf{Pf}(\mathcal{O})\neq 0\}.

If we identify
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
with a subspace of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
via the Jordan-Hölder basis, and
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits we can identify
\Gamma
with the Zariski open dense subset
\Lambda\subseteq\mathfrak{z}^{*}
defined by

\Lambda:=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})\neq 0\}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon:\#\varepsilon_{n}=2d}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}.

This proposition shows that the flat orbits form a Hausdorff subset of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
which is stratified by the stratas in the fine stratification for which
\#\varepsilon_{n}=2d
.

Remark 0.6.7
.

Let us describe the Pfaffian function in slightly more detail on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
in terms of the Jordan-Hölder basis
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
fixed throughout our constructions. We assumed that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{\dim\mathfrak{z}}
span
\mathfrak{z}
and we will further assume that for some
p\leq\dim(\mathfrak{z})
,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
span
\mathfrak{z}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
. Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}
denote the dual basis for
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. We obtain a collection of
2
-forms
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{p}:(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\wedge(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\to\mathbb{R}
by setting
\omega_{j}(X,Y):=\xi_{j}[X,Y]=\omega_{\xi_{j}}[X,Y]
. For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}
, we have that

\omega_{\xi}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}.

We conclude that on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
, the Pfaffian function is the polymial of degree
\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
given by

\mathsf{Pf}(\xi)=\mathsf{Pf}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}\right).

The polynomial
\mathsf{Pf}(\xi)
depends only on the coordinates
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{p}
. We note that this polynomial is identically
0
if
\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
is odd. Moreover,
G
admits flat orbits if and only if this polynomial is non-vanishing on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
.

Proposition 0.6.8
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an
n
-dimensional simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits and
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathsf{G})
such that
\#\varepsilon_{n}=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
. Adopting the notations from Remark
0.6.7
filter
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
by the Jordan-Hölder basis, and for
j=1,\ldots,\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
we write
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})_{j}
for the linear span of
X_{\dim(\mathfrak{z})+1},\ldots,X_{\dim(\mathfrak{z})+j}
. The restriction of the bundle of Vergne polarization from Proposition
0.5.2
to the strata
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
in the transversal
\Lambda
for the flat orbits, i.e.
F_{V}|_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}\to\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
, takes the form

F_{V}|_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}=\left\{(\xi,X)\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\times\mathfrak{g}:\;X\in\mathfrak{z}\oplus\sum_{j=1}^{\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{g})}\ker(\omega_{\xi}|_{(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})_{j}})\right\}.

Proof.

Recall the definition of the Vergne polarization from Equation (
7
). It is clear that for
k=1,\ldots,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{z})
,

\mathsf{stab}(\xi|_{\mathfrak{g}_{k}})=\mathfrak{g}_{k}\subseteq\mathfrak{z},

with equality at
k=\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{z})
. The proposition therefore follows upon noting that for
\xi\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathfrak{z}^{*}
we have for
j=1,\ldots,\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
that

\mathsf{stab}(\xi|_{\mathfrak{g}_{j+\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{z})}})=\mathfrak{z}\oplus\ker(\omega_{\xi}|_{(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})_{j}}),

where we have identified
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{z}\oplus\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
as vector spaces.
∎

Proposition 0.6.9
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an
n
-dimensional simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Define

\Xi:=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{\xi}\mbox{ is flat}\}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon:\#\varepsilon_{n}=2d}\Xi_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

Then the quotient by the coadjoint action defines a smoothly trivializable vector bundle

\Xi\to\Gamma,

with fibre
\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

Proof.

By definition,

\Xi=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{\xi}\in\Gamma\}=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{\xi}=\xi+\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\}.

If we identify
\Gamma=\Lambda
, we see that

\psi:\Lambda\times\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\to\Xi,\quad\psi(\xi,\eta):=\xi+\eta,

defines a diffeomorphism that equips
\Xi
with the structure of a trivializable smooth vector bundle.
∎

Definition 0.6.10
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. We define the ideal
I_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
as

I_{\mathsf{G}}:=\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\;\forall\pi\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\Gamma\}.

We call
I_{\mathsf{G}}
the ideal of flat orbits.

Lemma 0.6.11
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. For any
a\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathsf{G})
, the mapping

\mathrm{Tr}_{a}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{C},\quad[\pi]\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\pi(a)),

is continuous.

Proof.

Since
\Gamma
is a strata in the coarse stratification of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
, the lemma follows from
[
121
, Lemma 4.4.4]
.

∎

Proposition 0.6.12
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group. Then
I_{\mathsf{G}}
is a continuous trace algebra over
\Gamma
.

Proof.

The
C^{*}
-algebra is a continuous trace algebra since it has Hausdorff spectrum and each element of the dense subspace
I_{\mathsf{G}}\cap C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathsf{G})\subseteq I_{\mathsf{G}}
has continuous trace by Lemma
0.6.11
.
∎

We shall return to study the structure of
I_{G}
further below in Section
0.8
.

0.7.
Automorphisms and their action on
\hat{\mathsf{G}}

In order to understand the global structure of a Carnot manifold, we must describe how automorphisms of the nilpotent Lie group act on the representations. Let
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
denote the group of automorphisms of a group
\mathsf{G}
and let
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
denote the group of automorphisms of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We first note that if
\mathsf{G}
is a simply connected nilpotent Lie group, the exponential map induces a group isomorphism
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
. For an automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
, we let
\varphi^{*}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}
denote its dual.

Proposition 0.7.1
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
maps coadjoint orbits to coadjoint orbits. In particular, the
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
-action defines a continuous action on
\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
defined by

\varphi.\mathcal{O}_{\xi}:=\mathcal{O}_{\varphi^{*}(\xi)}\equiv\varphi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi}),

for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

Moreover, the set of flat orbits
\Gamma\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is invariant for the
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
-action. In other words, if
\mathcal{O}\in\Gamma
is flat and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
then
\varphi.\mathcal{O}\in\Gamma
is also flat.

We note that in general, a strata in the fine stratification can fail to be preserved by a group automorphism that does not preserve the Jordan-Hölder flag.

Proof.

The first part of the proposition is an immediate consequence of the fact that the inner automorphisms, i.e. the adjoint action, is a normal subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
. That an arbitrary automorphism preserves the space of flat orbits is a consequence of that an arbitrary automorphism preserves the center of the Lie group. In other words, we have for
\xi\in\Xi
that

\mathcal{O}_{\xi}=\xi+\mathfrak{z}^{\bot}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\varphi.\mathcal{O}_{\xi}=\mathcal{O}_{\varphi^{*}\xi}=\varphi^{*}\xi+\mathfrak{z}^{\bot}.

∎

We make use of the following convention for gradings of nilpotent Lie algebras.

Definition 0.7.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a nilpotent Lie algebra. A grading on
\mathfrak{g}
is a decomposition indexed by negative integers

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p},

with
[\mathfrak{g}_{p},\mathfrak{g}_{q}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{p+q}
for any
p,q<0
.

If we have a grading on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of a simply connected nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
, we let
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})
denote the space of graded Lie algebra automorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})
its image under the group isomorphism
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
. We make the following remark about gradings in relation to certain one-parameter families of automorphisms. Following
[
70
]
, we say that a one-parameter group of automorphisms
\delta:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
is a dilation if
\dot{\delta}(1)\in\mathrm{End}(\mathfrak{g})
is diagonalizable with positive integer eigenvalues.

Proposition 0.7.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a nilpotent Lie algebra. There is a one-to-one correspondence between dilations and gradings on
\mathfrak{g}
given by the following two constructions that are mutually inverse.

•

If
\delta
is a dilation, and we set
\mathfrak{g}_{p}:=\ker(\dot{\delta}(1)+p)
, then

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p},

defines a grading of
\mathfrak{g}
.

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p}
is a grading of
\mathfrak{g}
, then

\delta_{t}:=\bigoplus_{p<0}t^{-p}\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{p}},\quad t>0,

defines a dilation of
\mathfrak{g}
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is graded by a dilation
\delta
, then

\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}):\,[\dot{\delta}(1),\varphi]=0\}=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}):\,\delta_{t}\circ\varphi=\varphi\circ\delta_{t}\;\forall t>0\}.

Remark 0.7.4
.

Not all nilpotent Lie algebras admit gradings. Indeed, by
[
58
]
there is a nine dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra not admitting a dilation. However, any quotient of the free graded Lie algebra of any rank by a homogeneous ideal admits a dilation.

We remark that this showcases a subtle, yet important, distinction between gradings and filterings on Lie algebras. Existence of a finite filtration by ideals on a Lie algebra is equivalent to it being nilpotent. Moreover, any automorphism of a nilpotent Lie algebra preserves the filtration defined from the descending central series. In the geometric setting, we see this phenomena appear in the Examples
0.16.3
and
0.16.4
.

Definition 0.7.5
.

A Carnot-Lie group is a pair
(\mathsf{G},\delta)
consisting of a nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
equipped with a choice of dilation
\delta
(or equivalently a grading of the Lie algebra). We often drop the
\delta
from the notation when it is clear from context.

Proposition 0.7.6
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected Carnot-Lie group and let
\Gamma
denote the set of flat orbits of
\mathsf{G}
. The action of the dilation group preserves
\Gamma
and the set

\Gamma_{\partial}:=\Gamma/\delta(\mathbb{R}_{+}),

is a well defined topological space. Moreover, if
\mathsf{G}
is non-abelian, the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action on
\Gamma
is free and proper, and the homeomorphism
\Gamma\cong\Lambda
of Proposition
0.6.6
induces a homeomorphism

\Lambda_{\partial}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma_{\partial},

where
\Lambda_{\partial}
is the smooth algebraic hypersurface in
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
defined from

\Lambda_{\partial}=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\mathsf{det}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})=1\}\subseteq\Lambda.

In particular,
\Gamma_{\partial}
is canonically homeomorphic to
\Lambda_{\partial}
.

We remark that since the homeomorphism
\Gamma\cong\Lambda
depends on a choice of Jordan-Hölder basis, so does the homeomorphism
\Lambda_{\partial}\cong\Gamma_{\partial}
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
0.7.1
that
\delta
preserves
\Gamma
. The homeomorphism
\Gamma\cong\Lambda
of Proposition
0.5.1
intertwines the action of
\delta
on
\Gamma
with an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
. For the remainder of the proof, we consider non-abelian
\mathsf{G}
. If
\mathsf{G}
is non-abelian, the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action on
\Gamma
is free and proper. Indeed, we note that for
\xi\in\Lambda
, there are constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that for
t>0
,

c_{0}\min(t^{\lambda^{\prime}},t^{\lambda})\leq|\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{t.\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})|\leq c_{1}c_{0}\max(t^{\lambda^{\prime}},t^{\lambda})

where
\lambda
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\dot{\delta}(1)|_{\mathfrak{z}}
and
\lambda^{\prime}
is the largest eigenvalue of
\dot{\delta}(1)|_{\mathfrak{z}}
. Since
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
, we conclude that there is a unique
t>0
, depending continuously on
\xi
, such that
\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{t.\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})=\pm 1
. As such,
\Lambda/\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be identified with

\{\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})=\pm 1\}=\Lambda_{\partial},

where we in the last equality use that
\mathsf{det}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})=|\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}})|^{2}
. The determinant is a regular function outside of its zero set, so
\Lambda_{\partial}
is a smooth algebraic hypersurface in
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
.
∎

Remark 0.7.7
.

Proposition
0.7.6
induces a continuous splitting of the quotient map
\Gamma\to\Gamma_{\partial}
. We identify
\Gamma_{\partial}
with its image in
\Gamma\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
as a transversal to the dilation action. In particular,
\Gamma_{\partial}\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is a closed Hausdorff subset.

Definition 0.7.8
.

We define the
C^{*}
-algebra
I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
as the quotient of
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
by the ideal

\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\;\forall\pi\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\Gamma_{\partial}\}.

Remark 0.7.9
.

We note that the restriction of the quotient map
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
to
I_{\mathsf{G}}
is surjective and that the spectrum of
I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
is the closed Hausdorff subset
\Gamma_{\partial}\subseteq\hat{G}
.

The next proposition follows from Proposition
0.7.6
.

Proposition 0.7.10
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected non-abelian Carnot-Lie group. There is an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-equivariant
*
-isomorphism
\alpha_{\partial}:I_{\mathsf{G}}\to C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
(for the trivial action on
I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
) such that

•

The maps induced from
\alpha_{\partial}
, the restricted quotient map
I_{\mathsf{G}}\to I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
, and the evaluation-at-
1
-map
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}\to I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}
on the spectrum fits into an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-equivariant commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Gamma_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \Gamma_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\partial}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

•

These
*
-homomorphisms fit into a commuting diagram of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-equivariant
*
-homomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(\mathsf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ I_{\mathsf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes I_{\partial,\mathsf{G}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

Let us describe how automorphisms act on the space of flat orbits in terms of its realization as the transversal
\Lambda
. Recall our convention to identify
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
with a subspace of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
via the Jordan-Hölder basis. The following proposition is immediate from Proposition
0.7.3
and
0.7.6
.

Proposition 0.7.11
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected, nilpotent Lie group and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
. Define the polynomial map

\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}:\Lambda\to\Lambda,\;\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}(\xi):=(\varphi^{*}\xi)|_{\mathfrak{z}}.

Then
\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}
is a self-homeomorphism fitting into a commuting diagram wtih the homeomorphism
\Gamma\cong\Lambda
of Proposition
0.5.1

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}

(13)

For any two
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
,
(\varphi\circ\varphi^{\prime})_{\Lambda}^{*}=\varphi^{\prime}{}_{\Lambda}^{*}\circ\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}
.

Additionally, if
\mathsf{G}
is a Carnot-Lie group and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})
then
\varphi_{\Lambda}^{*}
commutes with the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action and induces a self-diffeomorphism

\varphi_{\Lambda_{\partial}}^{*}:\Lambda_{\partial}\to\Lambda_{\partial},

fitting into a commutative diagram with
\varphi^{*}:\Gamma_{\partial}\to\Gamma_{\partial}
as in Equation (
13
).

Remark 0.7.12
.

If
\dot{\delta}(1)|_{\mathfrak{z}}
is scalar (i.e. the dilation acts by scalars on the center) or
\varphi
preserves the determinant function on
\Lambda
, then
\varphi_{\Lambda_{\partial}}^{*}
is polynomial since it is the restriction of a linear map on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
.

0.8.
The continuous trace algebra structure of the ideal of flat orbits

We saw in Proposition
0.6.12
that the ideal of flat orbits
I_{\mathsf{G}}
was a continuous trace algebra with spectrum
\Gamma
. For an overview of continuous trace algebras, see
[
131
,
134
]
. We use the notation
\delta_{\rm DD}
for the Dixmier-Douady invariant in the third integral Cech cohomology, e.g.
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})\in\check{H}^{3}(\Gamma;\mathbb{Z})
. It is well known that
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0\in\check{H}^{3}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z})
if and only if there exists a Hilbert space bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
and a continuous homomorphism
\pi_{\musFlat}:\mathsf{G}\to C(\Gamma,U(\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}))
to the strongly continuous sections of the unitary homomorphisms of the bundle such that for each
\xi\in\Gamma
, we have that
\xi=[\pi_{\musFlat}|_{\{\xi\}}]
. We shall see that indeed
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
and call a choice of such a bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
a bundle of flat orbit representations.

In this subsection we produce an explicit construction of a bundle of flat orbit representations and study its equivariance properties under automorphisms. While this construction in its own right might not be overly exciting, it will be used later in this work on Carnot manifolds where a global construction is less immediate. The equivariance properties will in some examples let us construct global bundles by induction from principal bundles.

The problem of constructing bundles of flat orbit representations is interesting in light of the following conjecture of Lipman-Rosenberg. Recall that a subquotient of the
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
is a
C^{*}
-algebra of the form
A:=I_{1}/I_{2}
where
I_{2}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
are ideals. By
[
57
]
, we can identify
\hat{I}_{1}
and
\hat{I}_{2}
with open subsets of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
and
\hat{A}
with a subset of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
.

Conjecture 0.8.1

(Conjecture 3.2 in
[
105
]
)
.

For any connected nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
and continuous trace subquotient
A
of
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
(in particular
\hat{A}\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is a Hausdorff subset), it holds that
\delta_{\rm DD}(A)=0
.

We shall mainly be interested in the case that
A=I_{\mathsf{G}}
. It seems as if experts in the field know the case of
A=I_{\mathsf{G}}
to fulfil Conjecture
0.8.1
, but we have not been able to find a reference. We note the following cases where
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
can be concluded at this stage:

a)

If
\mathsf{G}
has step length
2
, Conjecture
0.8.1
holds true by
[
105
, Theorem 3.4]
. In particular,
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
for all step
2
nilpotent Lie groups
\mathsf{G}
. For examples of step
2
Lie groups, see Subsection
0.9.2
.

b)

If
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata, Proposition
0.5.9
implies that
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
. We shall see an abundance of examples below in Section
0.9
where
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata. An important special case when
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata is when
\mathsf{G}
has one-dimensional center and admits flat orbits (see Subsection
0.9.3
below), in this case
\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
.

c)

More generally than in b), if there exists a bundle of polarizations
F\to\Gamma
, with
F\subseteq\Lambda\times\mathfrak{g}
satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
0.5.2
, then
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
. Indeed, in this case the Hilbert space bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
can be constructed from
F
as in Proposition
0.5.4
.

Let us describe the structure of the continuous trace algebra
I_{\mathsf{G}}
and make the construction of the bundle of flat orbit representations more explicit. First, we show that the top fine stratas, while varrying over all Jordan-Hölder bases, is an open cover of the set of flat orbits. We let
\mathfrak{JH}
denote the set of Jordan-Hölder bases of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{\dim(\mathfrak{z})}=\mathfrak{z}
. For a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
, we let
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\Gamma
denote the top fine strata in the fine stratification defined from
\boldsymbol{B}
.

Theorem 0.8.2
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a nilpotent simply connected Lie group admitting flat orbits. Then
(U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
is an open cover of
\Gamma
.

To prove Theorem
0.8.2
, we shall first need a lemma.

Lemma 0.8.3
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a nilpotent simply connected Lie group with a flat orbit through
\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Let
\Gamma_{\mathsf{G}}
denote the set of flat orbits for
\mathsf{G}
. Fix an
X\in\ker(\xi)\cap\mathfrak{z}
. The following holds the Lie algebra
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{g}/\mathbb{R}X
:

i)

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
admits a flat orbit through the element
\bar{\xi}\in(\bar{\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
induced by
\xi
.

ii)

The centre
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}
of
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
is given by
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}=\mathfrak{z}/\mathbb{R}X
.

iii)

The set
\Gamma_{\bar{\mathsf{G}}}
of flat orbits for
\bar{\mathsf{G}}
can be identified with a hypersurface in
\Gamma_{\mathsf{G}}
via the injection
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{z}^{*}
dual to the projection from item ii).

Proof.

Since
\xi
lies in a flat orbit,

\mathfrak{z}/\mathbb{R}X=\mathsf{stab}(\xi)/\mathbb{R}X=\mathsf{stab}(\bar{\xi}).

We conclude from the series of inclusions
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}\subseteq\mathsf{stab}(\bar{\xi})=\mathfrak{z}/\mathbb{R}X\subseteq\bar{\mathfrak{z}}
that
\mathfrak{z}/\mathbb{R}X=\bar{\mathfrak{z}}
and that
\bar{\xi}
lies in a flat orbit. The lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
0.8.2
.

We prove the theorem by induction on the dimension of the centre of
\mathsf{G}
. If
\mathsf{G}
has one-dimensional centre and flat orbits, the statement holds because
\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
and
\Gamma=U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
for any
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
. Assume that the statement of the theorem holds true for nilpotent simply connected Lie groups admitting flat orbits with centre of dimension
\leq m-1
. Consider a nilpotent simply connected Lie group
\mathsf{G}
admitting flat orbits and whose centre is of dimension
m
. Take a
\xi\in\Gamma_{\mathsf{G}}
and an
X\in\ker(\xi)\cap\mathfrak{z}
. We follow the notation of Lemma
0.8.3
.

The nilpotent simply connected Lie group
\bar{\mathsf{G}}
that
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
integrates to satisfy the statement of the theorem by the induction assumption, because
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}=\mathfrak{z}/\mathbb{R}X
has dimension
m-1
. Therefore, there is a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}=(\bar{X}_{2},\ldots\bar{X}_{n})
for
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
\bar{\xi}\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{1}}\subseteq\Gamma_{\bar{\mathsf{G}}}
. We can lift
(\bar{X}_{2},\ldots\bar{X}_{n})
to
X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
and, up to permutation, consider the Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}=(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
for
\mathfrak{g}
where
X_{1}=X
.

The induction step is complete upon proving that
\Gamma_{J_{\xi}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}
contains an open subset, in which case
\Gamma_{J_{\xi}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}=U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
since
\xi\in\Gamma_{J_{\xi}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}
by definition. Take an
\eta\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}
such that
\eta(X)=1
and
\eta(X_{j})=0
for
j>1
. By Lemma
0.8.3
.iii), it suffices to prove that
J_{\xi^{\prime}+t\eta}^{\boldsymbol{B}}
is independent of sufficiently small
t
for
\xi^{\prime}\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{1}}\subseteq\Gamma_{\bar{\mathsf{G}}}
in a neighbourhood of
\bar{\xi}
. Consider the family of Jordan-Hölder bases
\boldsymbol{B}(t)=(X_{1}-tX_{2},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
. By definition, we have that
J_{\xi^{\prime}+t\eta}^{\boldsymbol{B}}=J_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{B}(t)}
and since the variation in
t
only can alter jump indices in the first step, we have that
J_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{B}(t)}
is independently of sufficiently small
t
. We conclude that
J_{\xi^{\prime}+t\eta}^{\boldsymbol{B}}
is independent of sufficiently small
t
, and
\Gamma_{J_{\xi}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}
contains an open neighbourhood around
\xi
.
∎

Remark 0.8.4
.

Consider the statement:

Assume that
\mathsf{G}
has orbits of dimension at most
2d
, and consider the space of orbits of maximal dimension
\tilde{\Gamma}:=\{\mathcal{O}\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}:\dim(\mathcal{O})=2d\}
.

Then
(U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
is an open cover of
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

Theorem
0.8.2
shows that the above statement holds in the presence of flat orbits – in this case
\Gamma=\tilde{\Gamma}
. Generally we have that
\cup_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\tilde{\Gamma}
is Zariski open (since each
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
is). However, the statement above fails in general as seen from the four dimensional Lie algebra spanned by
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4}
with brackets
[X_{4},X_{3}]=X_{2}
and
[X_{4},X_{2}]=X_{1}
and all other brackets zero. For this Lie algebra we can identify
\tilde{\Gamma}=\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\neq 0\}
but
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}=\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):\xi_{1}\neq 0\}
for any Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
. This example can be found in
[
128
, Remark 1, p. 274]
.

To simplify notation, we shall drop the notation involving jump indices, as we henceforth only use the minimal jump index that corresponds to the top strata, and instead indicate the basis. For instance,
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
denotes the top fine strata in the stratification defined from the basis
\boldsymbol{B}
,
F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
denotes the bundle of Vergne polarizations, and
\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
the associated Hilbert space bundle of
\mathsf{G}
-representations over
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
.

For any
\boldsymbol{B}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{B}_{k}\in\mathfrak{JH}
, we write

\displaystyle I_{\mathsf{G},\boldsymbol{B}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{B}_{k}}:

\displaystyle=C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{1}}\cap\cdots U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{k}})I_{\mathsf{G}}=

\displaystyle=\{a\in C^{*}(\mathsf{G}):\pi(a)=0\;\forall\pi\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}\setminus U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{1}}\cap\cdots U_{\boldsymbol{B}_{k}}\}.

For each
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
, Proposition
0.5.7
and
0.5.9
produces canonical trivializations

\pi_{\boldsymbol{B}}:I_{\mathsf{G},\boldsymbol{B}}\to C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}})),

where
d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
. For any
\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}
, the corrected Lion intertwiner from Proposition
0.4.5
(see also Lemma
0.4.2
) defines a fibrewise unitary intertwiner

\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}:=\mathfrak{L}_{F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}}:\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}\to\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}.

By possibly shrinking the open sets in the cover, we can also ensure continuity. In other words,

\mathsf{Ad}(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}}):C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}))\to C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}})),

is a unitary isomorphism fitting into the commuting diagram of
*
-isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{G,\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Ad}(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}

We conclude the following from Proposition
0.4.5
.

Proposition 0.8.5
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Then the collection
(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
glues together as bundles of Hilbert spaces with a fibrewise unitary
\mathsf{G}
-action to a bundle of flat representations

\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma,

via the corrected Lion intertwiners
(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
from Proposition
0.4.5
. In particular, the fibrewise unitary
\mathsf{G}
-action induces a
C_{0}(\Gamma)
-linear
*
-isomorphism

\pi_{\musFlat}:I_{\mathsf{G}}\to C_{0}(\Gamma,\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma})).

The existence of a bundle of flat orbit representations also follow from a deformation argument below in Theorem
0.14.6
.

By the universal property of the group
C^{*}
-algebra, any group automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(G)
induces an automorphism
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
that we by an abuse of notation also denote by
\varphi
. Since the associated group homomorphism
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathrm{Aut}(C^{*}(\mathsf{G}))
is injective this abuse of notation should not be a cause of confusion. By Proposition
0.5.8
and
0.7.1
, any automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
preserves the ideal
I_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
of flat orbits. We abstractly know that
I_{\mathsf{G}}
is a continuous trace algebra over
\Gamma
with fibre
\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where
d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
, and
I_{\mathsf{G}}
trivializes by Proposition
0.8.5
. We now describe the action of automorphisms on
I_{\mathsf{G}}
and their lifts to the bundle of flat orbit representations.

For a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathfrak{JH}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, with
\mathfrak{g}_{\dim\mathfrak{z}}=\mathfrak{z}
as in Section
0.5
and
0.6
, recall the construction of
F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
from Proposition
0.5.2
, where we write
F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}
and
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}
for the top strata in the basis
\boldsymbol{B}
(cf. Section
0.6
). For a
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
, we write
\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathfrak{JH}
for the Jordan-Hölder basis

\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}):=(\varphi^{-1}X_{1},\ldots,\varphi^{-1}X_{n}).

Proposition 0.8.6
.

Let
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
be automorphisms of a simply connected nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
admitting flat orbits. Take
\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}
.

a)

The mapping

\varphi^{*}\times\varphi^{-1}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}

restricts to a homeomorphism
\varphi^{*}\times\varphi^{-1}:F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to F_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
that fits into a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ U_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\times\varphi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}

b)

The map
(\xi,f)\mapsto(\varphi^{*}\xi,f\circ\varphi^{*})
defines a fibrewise unitary morphism

t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi):\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})},

such that:

•

The following diagram of Hilbert space bundles commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ U_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}

and
t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)
is equivariant for the ordinary
\mathsf{G}
-action on
\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}
and the
\mathsf{G}
-action on
\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
pulled back along
\varphi
.

•

It holds that

t_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}(\varphi^{\prime})t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)=t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi\varphi^{\prime}),

as morphisms of Hilbert space bundles
\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi\varphi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{B})}
.

•

The following diagram of Hilbert space bundles commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}|_{U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}\cap U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}|_{U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}|_{U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}\cap U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}(\varphi)}

c)

The automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(I_{\mathsf{G}})
restricts to a
*
-isomorphism
\varphi|:I_{\mathsf{G},\boldsymbol{B}}\to I_{\mathsf{G},\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
that together with
t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)
from the previous item fit into the commuting diagram of
*
-isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathsf{G},\boldsymbol{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathsf{G},\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B)}},\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ad}(t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi))}

Proof.

Note that
\mathfrak{h}
is a real algebraic polarization of
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
if and only if
\varphi^{-1}\mathfrak{h}
is a real algebraic polarization of
\varphi^{*}\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. We also note that since
\varphi^{*}(\Xi_{\varepsilon,\boldsymbol{B}})=\Xi_{\varepsilon,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
for any
\varepsilon
, and
\varphi^{*}
is a homeomorphism on
\Gamma
, we have that
\varphi^{*}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}})=U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
. To prove item a), we simply note that for
\xi\in U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}
,

\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi^{-1*}\xi))=\mathfrak{h}_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}(\xi),

which implies that

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\times\varphi^{-1})F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}

\displaystyle=\{(\varphi^{*}\xi,\varphi^{-1}X):\,\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}},X\in\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)\}=

\displaystyle=\{(\xi,X):\,\xi\in U_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})},X\in\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi^{-1*}\xi)\}=F_{V,\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}.

Item b) and c) are proven by similar, lengthier but straightforward, computations and we omit their proofs.
∎

To describe how to lift an automorphism to the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
of flat orbit representations constructed in Proposition
0.8.5
, we follow
[
131
]
. We let
(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}\in\check{C}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))
denote the cocycle defined from

u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}:=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\eta(F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}})}.

Here we have for notational simplicity disregarded the fact that the covering needs to be refined to ensure continuity. We define the map

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}:\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to

\displaystyle\check{C}^{1}(((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))),

(14)

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto(\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})u_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}^{-1})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}.

Proposition 0.8.7
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Then the mapping
\zeta_{0}
satisfies

(1)

The range of
\zeta_{0}
is contained in the Cech cocycles, i.e.
\zeta_{0}
induces a mapping

\zeta:\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)).

(2)

\zeta_{0}\in Z^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{Z}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1)))
is a group cocycle for the induced
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-action on
\check{Z}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))
, and induces a cohomology class

[\zeta_{0}]\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{Z}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))),

and
\zeta\in Z^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
induces a cohomology class

[\zeta]\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))).

(3)

The class
[\zeta]\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
does not depend on the choice of
(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}\in\check{C}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))
as long as the cochain
(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}
solves

u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}u_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}}u_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{B}}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\mathrm{Mas}(F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}},F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}},F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}})}.

(15)

Moreover, the action of
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
on
\Gamma
lifts to a bundle morphism on
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
inducing
\varphi
on
I_{\mathsf{G}}
if and only if
\zeta(\varphi)=0\in\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))
. Additionally, for a line bundle
L\to\Gamma
,
\varphi
lifts to a bundle morphism on
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\otimes L
inducing
\varphi
on
I_{\mathsf{G}}
if and only if
\zeta(\varphi)=\varphi^{*}(c_{1}(L))c_{1}(L^{-1})\in\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))
.

Proof.

The coboundary of
\zeta_{0}(\varphi)
is the Cech cocycle given by

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle u_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}^{-1}\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}})u_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime})}^{-1}\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{B}})u_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}^{-1}=

\displaystyle=t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{B}})t_{\boldsymbol{B}}(\varphi)^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}\mathfrak{L}_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime})}\mathfrak{L}_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})}=1,

by Proposition
0.8.6
. Item 1 follows. To prove item 2, we note that the group coboundary
\delta\zeta_{0}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is given by

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi^{*}\zeta_{0}(\varphi^{\prime})\zeta_{0}(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}\zeta_{0}(\varphi)=

\displaystyle=((\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})\varphi^{*}u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})^{-1}u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}^{-1})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}=1.

To prove item 3), we note that if
(u^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}\in\check{C}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))
is another choice of
U(1)
-cochain solving (
15
), then
(u^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}u^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}\in\check{Z}^{1}((U_{\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}},U(1))
. Denote the associated cohomology class by
x\in\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))
and let
\zeta^{\prime}\in Z^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
denote the cocycle constructed from
(u^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
. Clearly,
\zeta^{\prime}(\varphi)\zeta(\varphi)^{-1}=\varphi^{*}(x)x^{-1}
so

[\zeta]=[\zeta^{\prime}]\quad\mbox{in}\quad H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))).

To prove the final remark, we note that Proposition
0.8.6
(and a computation involving the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
) implies that
\varphi
lifts to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
if and only if there exists a group cocycle
(\tau_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}
such that

\varphi^{*}(u_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}})u_{\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}=\tau_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{B}}\tau_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}^{-1}.

In particular,
\varphi
lifts to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
if and only if
\zeta(\varphi)=[\delta(\tau_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{B}})_{\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}}]=0
. The argument for the case when
\zeta(\varphi)=\varphi^{*}(c_{1}(L))c_{1}(L^{-1})
, for a line bundle
L\to\Gamma
, goes similarly.
∎

Proposition 0.8.8
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Assume that
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
is a subgroup. The following are equivalent

(1)

[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))

(2)

Up to tensoring
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
with a line bundle
L\to\Gamma
(solving
\zeta(\varphi)=\varphi^{*}(c_{1}(L))c_{1}(L^{-1})
for each
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
), each
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
lifts to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
and the choice can locally in
\mxedr
.g
be made so that the lift depends strongly continuously on
\varphi
.

Proof.

If
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
, there exists an
x\in\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))
with
\zeta(\varphi)=\varphi^{*}(x)x^{-1}
for all
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
. There is a line bundle
L\to\Gamma
with
c_{1}(L)
mapped to
x
under
\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))\cong\check{H}^{2}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z})
. By Proposition
0.8.7
, we can lift all
\varphi
in a strongly continuous manner. The converse follows in the same way.
∎

Definition 0.8.9
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits and
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
a subgroup such that
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
.

(1)

Let
L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}\to\Gamma
be a line bundle such that
\zeta(\varphi)=\varphi^{*}(c_{1}(L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}))c_{1}(L_{\text{\mxedr.g}})^{-1}
for each
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
, and define

\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}:=\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\otimes L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}.

(2)

For each
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
, pick lifts
\alpha(\varphi)
to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
as in Proposition
0.8.8
. Define the map

c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}:\text{\mxedr.g}\times\text{\mxedr.g}\to C(\Gamma,U(1)),\quad(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\mapsto\alpha(\varphi)\varphi^{*}(\alpha(\varphi^{\prime}))\alpha(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}.

Proposition 0.8.10
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Assume that
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
is a subgroup such that
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
and construct
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
and
c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}
as in Definition
0.8.9
. Then
c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}
satisfies the following:

(1)

c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}\in Z^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1)))
is a group cocycle for the induced
\mxedr
.g
-action on
C(\Gamma,U(1))
.

(2)

The class
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1)))
is independent of the choice of
L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}
and lifts
\alpha(\varphi)
.

(3)

The lifts of
\mxedr
.g
to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
can be choosen to define a strongly continuous
\mxedr
.g
-action if and only if
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1)))
.

Proof.

Item 1 is a short computation. Item 2 follows from noting that the lift
\alpha
is unique up to a
C(\Gamma,U(1))
-factor, so two different choices will give rise to cohomologous
c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}
.

To prove item 3, we first note that the lifts
\alpha(\varphi)
are uniquely determined by
\varphi
up to a
C(\Gamma,U(1))
-factor. We have that
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1)))
if and only if there is a
\tau_{\text{\mxedr.g}}:\text{\mxedr.g}\to C(\Gamma,U(1))
such that

c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(\varphi,\varphi)=\tau_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(\varphi)\varphi^{*}(\tau_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(\varphi^{\prime}))\tau_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}.

Existence of such a
\tau_{\text{\mxedr.g}}
is clearly equivalent to existence of a modification of the lifts
\alpha(\varphi)
by a
C(\Gamma,U(1))
-factor such that
\alpha(\varphi)\varphi^{*}\alpha(\varphi^{\prime})=\alpha(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})
.
∎

Theorem 0.8.11
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits and
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a Hilbert space bundle
\mathcal{H}\to\Gamma
with a fibrewise strongly continuous
\mathsf{G}
-action, with the unitary equivalence class of the representation in the fibre over
\xi\in\Gamma
is exactly
\xi
, such that the
\mxedr
.g
-action on
\Gamma
lifts to a strongly continuous action on
\mathcal{H}\to\Gamma
and a
\mxedr
.g
-equivariant
*
-isomorphism

\pi_{\musFlat}:I_{\mathsf{G}}\to C_{0}(\Gamma,\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H})).

(2)

It holds that
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
and
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1)))
.

(3)

There exists a
C_{0}(\Gamma)
-linear
\mxedr
.g
-equivariant Morita equivalence

I_{\mathsf{G}}\sim_{M}C_{0}(\Gamma).

Proof.

That item 1 and 3 are equivalent follows from Kasparov’s stabilization theorem and the definition of equivariant Morita equivalence. Clearly item 1 implies item 2, and item 2 implies item 1 by Proposition
0.8.10
(with
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
).
∎

Remark 0.8.12
.

Let us take a moment to summarize the constructions above. To construct an equivariant bundle of flat representations on a simply connected nilpotent Lie group one first constructs
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
as in Proposition
0.8.5
. Secondly, if
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
satisfies
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
we can modify
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
by a line bundle to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
as in Proposition
0.8.8
and lift the action of
\mxedr
.g
to
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
. If
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1))
, Proposition
0.8.10
allow us modify each
\alpha(\varphi)
,
\varphi\in\text{\mxedr.g}
, by a scalar valued function such that
\mxedr
.g
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma,L_{\text{\mxedr.g}}}
.

Remark 0.8.13
.

In
[
42
]
, an equivariant Brauer group was defined. For a second countable group
\mxedr
.g
acting on a second countable space
T
, the equivariant Brauer group
\mathrm{Br}_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(T)
is defined as the
T
-linear
\mxedr
.g
-equivariant Morita equivalence classes of
\mxedr
.g
-continuous trace algebras
A
over
T
. The product in
\mathrm{Br}_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(T)
is defined by
\otimes_{C_{0}(T)}
and the unit by
C_{0}(T)
. In
[
42
, Theorem 5.1]
, it was proven that there is a filtration
0\subseteq B_{1}\subseteq B_{2}\subseteq B_{3}=\mathrm{Br}_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(T)
and morphisms

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm DD}:B_{3}=\mathsf{Br}_{\text{\mxedr.g}}(T)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\widecheck{H}^{3}(T,\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\zeta:B_{2}=\ker\delta_{\rm DD}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\widecheck{H}^{1}(T,U(1))),

\displaystyle c:B_{1}=\ker\zeta

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(T,U(1))),

such that
A\sim_{M}C_{0}(T)
(
\mxedr
.g
-equivariantly over
T
) if and only if
\delta_{\rm DD}(A)=0\in H^{3}(T,\mathbb{Z})
,
\zeta(A)=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(T,U(1)))
and
c(A)=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(T,U(1)))
. As such, Theorem
0.8.11
is a special case of the results in
[
42
]
.

The description above can be made substantially simpler in the special case that there is a global bundle of polarizations over all of
\Gamma
. This occurs for instance when the
\Gamma
only consists of one fine strata or the bundle of Vergne polarizations is a constant subbundle.

Proposition 0.8.14
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Assume that there exists a sub-bundle
F\subseteq\Gamma\times\mathfrak{g}
such that the fibre over
\xi\in\Gamma
is a polarization of
\xi
. Then the following holds:

•

It holds that
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
.

•

Define the associated Hilbert space bundle
\mathcal{H}_{F}\to\Gamma
, as in Proposition
0.5.4
, with a strongly continuous fibrewise
\mathsf{G}
-action such that
[\mathcal{H}_{F,\xi}]=\xi
for each
\xi\in\Gamma
. Then each
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
lifts to the morphism given by the composition

\mathcal{H}_{F}\xrightarrow{t_{F}(\varphi)}\mathcal{H}_{F_{\varphi}}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{L}^{(0)}_{F,F_{\varphi}}}\mathcal{H}_{F}.

where
F_{\varphi}=(\varphi^{*}\times\varphi^{-1})(F)\to\Gamma
is the transformed bundle of polarizations (cf. Proposition
0.8.6
.a) and
t_{F}(\varphi):\mathcal{H}_{F}\to\mathcal{H}_{F_{\varphi}}
the map
(\xi,f)\mapsto(\varphi^{*}\xi,f\circ\varphi^{*})
(cf. Proposition
0.8.6
.b) and
\mathfrak{L}^{(0)}_{F,F_{\varphi}}:\mathcal{H}_{F_{\varphi}}\to\mathcal{H}_{F}
is the Lion transform (see Proposition
0.4.2
).

•

The cocycle

c_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})}:\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\times\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to C(\Gamma,U(1)),

is the image of
c_{F,{\rm fl}}\in Z^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),C(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/8))
defined by

c_{F,{\rm fl}}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}):=\mathrm{Mas}(F_{\varphi},\varphi^{*}F_{\varphi^{\prime}},F_{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}})\!\!\!\mod 8\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The first item follows from the second item by Proposition
0.8.7
. The second and third item follows from Proposition
0.4.2
.
∎

Proposition 0.8.15
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Let
d:=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
. Assume that there exists a sub-bundle
F\subseteq\Gamma\times\mathfrak{g}
such that the fibre over
\xi\in\Gamma
is a polarization of
\xi
and
T_{F}:\mathcal{H}_{F}\to\Gamma\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a global trivialization. Let
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
be a subgroup. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

[c_{\rm fl}|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]\in\ker(H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/8))\to H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1))))

(2)

The natural homomorphism
\text{\mxedr.g}\to\mathrm{Aut}(I_{\mathsf{G}})\cong C(\Gamma,PU(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
(via
\mathrm{Ad}(T_{F})
) lifts to a homomorphism
\text{\mxedr.g}\to C(\Gamma,U(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
.

(3)

The
\mxedr
.g
-action on
I_{\mathsf{G}}
is induced from a strongly continuous
\mxedr
.g
-action on
\mathcal{H}_{F}
.

Proof.

The proposition follows from Proposition
0.8.10
and
0.8.14
.
∎

0.9.
Examples of graded nilpotent Lie groups

In this subsection we give some salient examples of simply connected, nilpotent Lie groups geared towards the geometric scenarios arising later in the monograph. The examples aim at exemplifying the contents of the recent subsections with a focus on describing gradings, computing the top strata
\Gamma
, the Hilbert space bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
, the ideal
I_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
and in particular how to single out a compact subgroup
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
lifting to an action on
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
.

0.9.1.
Step 1 graded nilpotent Lie groups

Nilpotent Lie groups of step length 1 are just the abelian Lie groups. So the term simply connected, nilpotent, graded Lie group of step length 1 is a lengthy description of the Lie group
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, equipped with a vector space grading

\mathfrak{g}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathbb{R}^{n_{p}}[p],

where we write
\mathbb{R}^{n_{p}}[p]
to indicate the space
\mathbb{R}^{n_{p}}
where the grading on this subspace is
p
. Clearly any such decomposition with
\sum_{p<0}n_{p}=n
defines a grading of the Lie algebra
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
is abelian, we have that

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})=GL_{n}(\mathbb{R})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})=\bigoplus_{p<0}GL_{n_{p}}(\mathbb{R})

We can also conclude that

\hat{\mathsf{G}}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\Gamma=\Xi,

which only consists of one strata. In particular,
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})=I=C_{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})=C_{0}(\Xi)=C_{0}(\Gamma)
. We have that
\mathcal{H}_{V}=\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}
is a trivial line bundle and the
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-action on
I
lifts to an action on
\mathcal{H}_{V}
which is trivial on the fibre, so in particular
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
,
\zeta=0
, and
[c_{\rm flat}]=0\in H^{2}(GL_{n}(\mathbb{R}),C_{0}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))
. This example is likely too trivial to improve the understanding of the reader, as the whole theory collapses to the standard Fourier transform on a graded vector space, but we have included it as it contains the local model for foliations as a special case (see more below in Example
0.16.2
) and isolates the effects of choosing a grading.

0.9.2.
Step 2 graded nilpotent Lie groups

Let us consider the case of step length
2
. This situation arises geometrically when for instance having a non-integrable length 2 filtration of a manifold, i.e. one specified subbundle of the tangent bundle which is not integrable.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra of step length
2
, then
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{g}/[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
is an abelian Lie algebra. In particular,
\mathfrak{g}
as a Lie algebra is determined by a subspace
C(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
and a surjective map
\omega:\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\to C(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{g}/C(\mathfrak{g})
, by

[X,Y]=\omega(\overline{X},\overline{Y}),

where we use the notation
\overline{X}:=X+C(\mathfrak{g})
. Surjectivity of
\omega
ensures that
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=C(\mathfrak{g})
. We say that
\mathfrak{g}
is a 2-step nilpotent lie algebra of type
(p,q)
if

\dim[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=p\quad\mbox{and}\quad\dim\overline{\mathfrak{g}}=q.

For more details, see
[
62
]
.
Following Remark
0.6.7
, we let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
denote a basis of
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
and we define
\overline{\omega}_{k}:\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathbb{R}
