,
k=1,\ldots,p
, by

[X,Y]=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\overline{\omega}_{k}(\overline{X},\overline{Y})X_{k}.

Surjectivity of
\omega
ensures that the forms
\overline{\omega}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\omega}_{p}
are linearly independent in
(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
. The basis induces an inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
and we can identify
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
with the orthogonal complement of
C(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
and
(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
with
\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
. We identify the collection
\overline{\omega}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\omega}_{p}\in(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
with a collection
\omega^{1},\ldots,\omega^{p}\in\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
and let
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
denote the space spanned by
\omega^{1},\ldots,\omega^{p}
.

For an arbitrary finite-dimensional inner product space
V
and a subspace
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
, we can equip
V\oplus W
with a Lie bracket by declaring
W
to be central and for
X,Y\in V
the element
[X,Y]\in W
is given by that

([X,Y],Z)_{\mathfrak{so}(V)}=(Z(X),Y)_{V}\quad\forall Z\in W.

We denote this Lie algebra by
V_{W}
. It is readily verified that
V_{W}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra of step length
2
of type
(\dim(W),\dim(V))
with
C(V_{W})=W
. The reader should note that
V_{\mathfrak{so}(V)}
is the free step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra on
V
and that for any
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
the following sequence is exact

0\to W^{\perp}\to V_{\mathfrak{so}(V)}\to V_{W}\to 0.

(16)

It was proven in
[
62
]
that this construction up to isomorphism exhausts all nilpotent Lie algebras of step length
2
.

Proposition 0.9.1

(Proposition 3.1.1 of
[
62
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra and type
(p,q)
with a choice of inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}}
on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
and a Jordan-Hölder basis
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{p}=C(\mathfrak{g})
. Define
\omega^{1},\ldots,\omega^{p}\in\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
and
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
as above. For the basis
w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}\in W
such that
(\omega^{i},w_{j})_{\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})}=\delta_{i,j}
, the mapping

\mathfrak{g}\to\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{W},\quad X_{j}\mapsto\begin{cases}w_{j},\;j=1,\ldots,p,\\
\overline{X}_{j},\;j=p+1,\ldots,n,\end{cases}

is a Lie algebra isomorphism.

Remark 0.9.2
.

We note that Proposition
0.9.1
implies that for a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, it holds that

\mathfrak{z}=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}:w(\overline{X})=0\forall w\in W\},

where
\mathfrak{z}
denotes the center of
\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
if and only if for any
X\in\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
, there is a
w\in W
with
w(X)\neq 0
.

From this characterization of step
2
nilpotent Lie algebras,
[
62
]
ensures an explicit description of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
. For a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with a choice of inner product on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
, with
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
as above, we define the Lie group

H_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{g\in GL(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}):g^{t}Wg\subseteq W\}.

By universality, the group
GL(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
acts on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})}
– the free step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
. We define
u_{H}:H_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
by noting that the short exact sequence (
16
) ensures that the
H_{\mathfrak{g}}
-action descends to
\mathfrak{g}\cong\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})}/W^{\perp}
. The vector space
\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))
acts as Lie algebra automorphisms on
\mathfrak{g}
by
u_{t}(A)(v\oplus w):=v\oplus(Av+w)
for
v\in\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
,
w\in C(\mathfrak{g})
and
A\in\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))
. It is readily verified that for
g\in H_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
A\in\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))
,

u_{H}(g)u_{t}(A)u_{H}(g)^{-1}=u_{t}(g.A),

where

(g.A)(v):=gA(gv)g^{t}.

(17)

Therefore
u_{t}
and
u_{H}
induces a well defined group homomorphism
u_{t}\rtimes u_{H}:\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))\rtimes H_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
where the crossed product is with respect to the
H_{g}
-action on
\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))
given in (
17
).

Proposition 0.9.3

(Proposition 3.4.2 of
[
62
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra with a choice of inner product on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{g}/C(\mathfrak{g})
. Then

u_{t}\rtimes u_{H}:\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},C(\mathfrak{g}))\rtimes H_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}),

is a group isomorphism.

Remark 0.9.4
.

The moduli space of step 2 nilpotent Lie algebras of type
(p,q)
can by
[
61
]
be identified with
G(p,\mathfrak{so}(q,\mathbb{R}))/GL_{q}(\mathbb{R})
, where
G(p,\mathfrak{so}(q,\mathbb{R}))
denotes the Grassmannian manifold of
p
-dimensional subspaces in
\mathfrak{so}(q,\mathbb{R})
. The automorphism group of a step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra of type
(p,q)
is quite small (see
[
62
, Proposition 3.4.3]
) for most values of
p
and
q
in the generic case (for a dense open subset of the moduli space). In particular, for most values of
p
and
q
, step 2 nilpotent Lie algebras of type
(p,q)
generically do not admit gradings.

The geometric setting our Lie algebras arise from ensures that the Lie algebras are graded which changes what should be meant with generic. Let us describe possible gradings on step 2 nilpotent Lie algebras.

Proposition 0.9.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra. If
\delta
is a dilation on
\mathfrak{g}
it is uniquely determined by the induced dilation
\overline{\delta}
on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
from the isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}\cong\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{W}=\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus C(\mathfrak{g})
(from Proposition
0.9.1
) and the identity

\delta_{t}[X,Y]=\omega(\overline{\delta}_{t}(\overline{X}),\overline{\delta}_{t}(\overline{Y})),\quad\mbox{for $[X,Y]\in C(\mathfrak{g})$}.

For any grading on
\mathfrak{g}
, we have that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{g}})
(see Proposition
0.9.3
for notation).

Moreover, a dilation
\overline{\delta}
on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
is induced from a dilation
\delta
on
\mathfrak{g}
if and only if the grading
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{p}
constructed from
\overline{\delta}
(as in Proposition
0.7.3
) satisfies that

\left(\sum_{k+l<p}\omega(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{l})\right)\bigcap\left(\sum_{k+l=p}\omega(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{l})\right)=0\quad\forall p<0.

(18)

Proof.

The first part of the proposition follows from that a dilation is a homomorphism
\delta:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
and the Lie bracket defines a surjection
\omega:\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\to C(\mathfrak{g})
.

The second part follows from noting that the condition (
18
) is equivalent to that the spaces

C(\mathfrak{g})_{p}:=\sum_{k+l=p}\omega(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}),

define a decomposition

C(\mathfrak{g})=\bigoplus_{p<0}C(\mathfrak{g})_{p}.

We can therefore define a dilation as in Proposition
0.7.3
from grading
\mathfrak{g}
by

\mathfrak{g}_{p}:=\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{p}\oplus C(\mathfrak{g})_{p}.

The decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p}
is indeed a grading as it follows from the construction that

[\mathfrak{g}_{p},\mathfrak{g}_{q}]=\omega(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{p},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{q})\subseteq C(\mathfrak{g})_{p+q}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{p+q}.

∎

We now turn our attention to representation theory of a step
2
simply connected nilpotent Lie group. We denote the Lie group by
\mathsf{G}
and its Lie algebra by
\mathfrak{g}
. As above we fix a Jordan-Hölder basis with
\mathfrak{g}_{p}=C(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\dim(\mathfrak{z})}=\mathfrak{z}
. We let
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{p}:(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\wedge(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\to\mathbb{R}
denote the forms induced from
\overline{\omega}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\omega}_{p}:\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathbb{R}
. The Pfaffian function (see Proposition
0.6.6
and Remark
0.6.7
) on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
can be described in the coordinates of the Jordan-Hölder basis as the polynomial of degree
\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
given by

\mathsf{Pf}(\xi)=\mathsf{Pf}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}\right).

As such, if
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits (i.e. the Pfaffian polynomial is non-vanishing on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
), the top stratum
\Gamma\subseteq\hat{\mathsf{G}}
can be described by the transversal
\Lambda\subseteq\mathfrak{z}^{*}
which is given as follows:

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\left\{\xi=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(\mathfrak{z})}\xi(X_{j})\xi_{j}\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\mathsf{Pf}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}\right)\neq 0\right\}=

(19)

\displaystyle=\left\{\xi=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(\mathfrak{z})}\xi(X_{j})\xi_{j}\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\det\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}\right)\neq 0\right\}=\Lambda_{C}\times(\mathfrak{z}/C(\mathfrak{g}))^{*},

where

\Lambda_{C}=\left\{\xi=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\xi_{j}\in C(\mathfrak{g})^{*}:\mathsf{Pf}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi(X_{j})\omega_{j}\right)\neq 0\right\}.

Remark 0.9.6
.

If
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
, i.e.
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z}
, then we can also write

\Lambda=w_{\mathfrak{z}}^{-1}(W\cap GL(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})),

(20)

where
w_{\mathfrak{z}}:\mathfrak{z}^{*}\to W
is defined by
w_{\mathfrak{z}}(\xi):=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(\mathfrak{z})}\xi(X_{j})\omega^{j}
. We note that by Remark
0.9.2
,
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
if and only if for any
X\in g
,
w(\overline{X})=0
for all
w\in W
implies that
X\in C(\mathfrak{g})
. In light of Equation (
20
), and injectivity of the
W
-action on
\mathfrak{g}
, it is tempting to suspect that the equality
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
implies the existence of flat orbits, but fails since even if
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
there need not exist an individual element of
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
acting injectively on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
. This fails in the example of Remark
0.9.19
below.

Theorem 0.9.7
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a step 2 simply connected nilpotent Lie group of type
(p,q)
admitting flat orbits. Set
d:=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
. We consider
\text{\mxedr.g}=u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{g}})\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
. Then the following holds for the ideal of flat orbits:

(1)

It holds that
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{1}(\text{\mxedr.g},\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
.

(2)

It holds that
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]
is the image of the analogous cohomology class
[c_{GL(q)}]
, defined for the free step 2 simply connected nilpotent Lie group on a
q
-dimensional vector space, under the mapping

H^{2}(GL(q),C(GL(q)/Sp(q),U(1)))\to H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1))),

defined from the inclusion
\text{\mxedr.g}\hookrightarrow GL(q)
and the inclusion
\Gamma\hookrightarrow GL(q)\cap\mathfrak{so}(q)\cong GL(q)/Sp(q)
.

Proof.

Item 1) follows from that
[\zeta|_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]
is the pullback of

[\zeta|_{GL(q)}]\in H^{1}(GL(q),\check{H}^{1}(GL(q)/Sp(q),U(1)))=0,

defined from the quotient map onto
\mathfrak{g}
from the free step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra on a
q
-dimensional vector space. Here
GL(q)\cap\mathfrak{so}(q)\cong GL(q)/Sp(q)
is the space of flat orbits of the free step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra on a
q
-dimensional vector space. Indeed, by homotopy invariance we have that
\check{H}^{1}(GL(q)/Sp(q),U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
H^{1}(GL(q),\mathbb{Z})=0
. Item 2) is now clear from construction.
∎

We let
\overline{G}=G/[G,G]
denote abelianization of
G
which coincides with the simply connected Lie group associated with
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
. We identify
\overline{G}
with
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}
. Pick a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
. By the proof of Proposition
0.5.2
,
(X_{j})_{j\in K_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
spans a complement to the Vergne polarization
\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
for any
\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\Lambda
. Write
K_{\boldsymbol{B}}=\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{d}\}
where
d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
. From this fact we deduce the following proposition.

Proposition 0.9.8
.

Let
G
be a step
2
nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits. Fix a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
with
K_{\boldsymbol{B}}=\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{d}\}
, where
d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2
. Define the mapping
\rho_{\boldsymbol{B}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to G
by

\rho_{\boldsymbol{B}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}):=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}X_{j_{i}}\right).

Then it holds that

•

The composition
\mathbb{R}^{d}\xrightarrow{\rho_{\boldsymbol{B}}}G\to\overline{G}
is linear.

•

For each
\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\Lambda
, the composition
\mathbb{R}^{d}\xrightarrow{\rho_{\boldsymbol{B}}}G\to G/H_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
is a polynomial diffeomorphism.

Proposition 0.9.9
.

Let
G
be a step
2
nilpotent Lie group admitting flat orbits with a Jordan-Hölder basis
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathfrak{JH}
. For
\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\subseteq\Lambda
, the mapping

T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi):\mathcal{H}_{F_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\mathcal{O},\xi),\xi}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)f(x):=f(\rho_{\boldsymbol{B}}(x)\xi),

is a well defined unitary that fits in a unitary trivialization of Hilbert space bundles over
\Gamma

T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}:\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}\to U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi,f):=(\xi,T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)f).

The
G
-action on the trivial Hilbert space bundle
U_{\boldsymbol{B}}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
induced by
T_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}
and the
G
-action on
\mathcal{H}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}
(see Proposition
0.5.4
) takes the form of a continuous homomorphism
\tilde{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{B}}:G\to C(U_{\boldsymbol{B}},U(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
where

\tilde{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{B},\xi}(g)f(x)=\mathrm{e}^{i\xi(\log^{\prime}_{\xi}(g)+[\log(g),\log(\rho(x))])}f(x+\log_{\xi}^{0}(g)),\quad\forall g\in G,\;\xi\in U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\;f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where we write
\log^{0}_{\xi}(g)
for the projection of
\log(g)
onto the linear span of
(X_{j})_{j\in K_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
(along the Vergne polarization) and
\log^{\prime}_{\xi}(g):=\log(g)-\log^{0}_{\xi}(g)\in\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
.

This proposition is a special case of Lemma
0.5.5
.

Proof.

The statements concerning
T_{V}
follows from inspecting the proof of Proposition
0.5.4
. Let us turn to the form of the representation
\tilde{\pi}
. Note that
\log\rho(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}X_{j_{i}}
. For
X=X_{0}+X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
, with
X_{0}
being the projection of
X
onto the linear span of
(X_{j})_{j\in K_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
(along the Vergne projection) and
X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{X}\rho(x)=\mathrm{e}^{X_{0}+X^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{\log\rho(x)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{X_{0}+X^{\prime}+\log\rho(x)+\frac{1}{2}[X,\log\rho(x)]}=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{X_{0}+\log\rho(x)}\mathrm{e}^{X^{\prime}+[X,\log\rho(x)]}=

\displaystyle=\rho(x+X_{0})\mathrm{e}^{X^{\prime}+[X,\log\rho(x)]},

where we have identified
X_{0}
with its coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to
(X_{j})_{j\in K_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is of step length
2
,
[X,\log\rho(x)]\in\mathfrak{z}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
so
\mathrm{e}^{X^{\prime}+[X,\log\rho(x)]}\in H_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)
. Therefore the representation
\tilde{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{B}}
takes the stated form.
∎

Remark 0.9.10
.

The explicit description of the representations of flat orbits for step length
2
from Proposition
0.9.9
allow us to define a “noncommutative Fourier transform” on flat orbits
\mathsf{F}_{\boldsymbol{B}}:C^{*}(G)\to C_{b}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
as the right vertical morphism of Proposition
12
. By Remark
0.5.10
, this “noncommutative Fourier transform” on flat orbits is injective. It can be defined as the closure of the linear map
\mathsf{F}_{\boldsymbol{B}}:\mathcal{S}(G)\to C_{b}(U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))
given by

[\mathsf{F}_{\boldsymbol{B}}a](\xi,x,y):=\int_{\mathfrak{h}_{V,\boldsymbol{B}}(\xi)}[\mathrm{exp}^{*}a](y-x,X^{\prime})\mathrm{e}^{i\xi(X^{\prime}+[X^{\prime}-y,x])}\mathrm{d}X^{\prime}

where we identify
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the linear span of
(X_{j})_{j\in K_{\boldsymbol{B}}}
. We note that the “noncommutative Fourier transform” on flat orbits and Equation
19
produces the isomorphism

I_{G,\boldsymbol{B}}\cong C_{0}((\mathfrak{z}/C(\mathfrak{g}))^{*}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}})\otimes C_{0}(\Lambda_{C}\cap U_{\boldsymbol{B}},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))),

and on the first tensor factor the noncommutative Fourier transform acts just as the ordinary Euclidean Fourier transform.

We now turn our attention to more explicit examples.

Example 0.9.11
.

The most fundamental example of a step 2 nilpotent Lie algebra is the Heisenberg group. The Heisenberg group is the local model for contact manifolds (see more in Example
0.16.5
below). The reader can also consult
[
71
]
for a broader view on the Heisenberg group. If
V
is a finite dimensional real vector space with a non-degenerate 2-form
\omega
and basis vector
Z\in\mathbb{R}
, there is a Lie bracket on
V\oplus\mathbb{R}
such that its centre is the copy of
\mathbb{R}
, and for
X,Y\in V

[X,Y]:=\omega(X,Y)Z.

We often suppress the role of
Z
, and simply denote the associated Lie algebra by
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}
. It is clear from the construction that
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}
is step
2
nilpotent of type
(1,\dim(V))
. A choice of inner product on
V
induces an isomorphism
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}\cong V_{\mathbb{R}\omega^{1}}
by Proposition
0.9.1
. There is a standard grading on
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}
given by

(V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R})_{-1}:=V\quad\mbox{and}\quad(V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R})_{-2}:=\mathbb{R}.

We use the notation
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
for a Heisenberg Lie algebra on a
2n
-dimensional vector space equipped with a non-degenerate
2
-form and
\mathbb{H}_{n}
for the associated step
2
simply connected nilpotent Lie group. Up to (non-canonical) isomorphism,
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
and
\mathbb{H}_{n}
only depend on
n
.

We conclude from Proposition
0.9.3
that

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{h}_{n})=V^{*}\rtimes((Sp(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}).

See also
[
71
, Chapter I.2]
. Here we can identify
Sp(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2
with the elements
g\in GL(V)
such that
g\omega g^{t}=\pm\omega
and the generator of
\mathbb{Z}/2
is an order two element
g
such that
g\omega g^{t}=-\omega
– it can be taken as
i
times complex conjugation upon choosing an isomorphism of
V
with
\mathbb{C}^{\dim(V)/2}
transforming
\omega
to the standard symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{\dim(V)/2}=\mathbb{R}^{\dim(V)}
. The graded automorphisms, for the standard grading, are described similarly as

\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{h}_{n})=(Sp(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

For the sake of simplicity, we fix a complex structure on
V
adapted to
\omega
. This induces an inner product structure on
V
, and we can (and will) identify
\omega
with the associated element in
\mathfrak{so}(V)
. Since
\omega
is non-degenerate, Equation (
19
) implies that

\Gamma=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi\neq 0\},

and
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata. In fact, the equality
\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
holds for all Lie groups admitting flat orbits and whose center is one-dimensional (see below in Subsection
0.9.3
). We note that the action

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{h}_{n})\times\Gamma\to\Gamma,

of the automorphisms on the flat orbits (see Proposition
0.7.11
) factors over the quotient to the unique dilation on the Heisenberg Lie algebra

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{h}_{n})=V^{*}\rtimes((Sp(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

By Proposition
0.7.6
, we can identify

\Gamma_{\partial}=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi(Z)=\pm 1\}.

For the Heisenberg group,
\widehat{\mathbb{H}_{n}}=\Gamma\cup\Gamma_{2}
(as sets) where
\Gamma_{2}=V^{*}
are the characters. Therefore, the representation theory is completely described once having described the representations of flat orbits. We fix a complex structure
J
on
V
adapted to
\omega
and consider
V
as an inner product space. Set
d:=\dim(V)/2
. Note that a subspace
V_{0}\subseteq V
is Lagrangian (i.e.
V_{0}=\{X\in V:\omega(X,Y)=0\;\forall Y\in V_{0}\}
) if and only if
V_{0}
and
JV_{0}
are orthogonal and
V=V_{0}+JV_{0}
. Fix such a subspace
V_{0}
, then for any basis
X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}
we obtain a Jordan-Hölder basis
Z,X_{1},\ldots,X_{1},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d}
where
Y_{j}:=JX_{j}
. If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}
is an ON-basis, we have the well known Heisenberg relations

[X_{j},X_{k}]=[Y_{j},Y_{k}]=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad[X_{j},Y_{k}]=\delta_{jk}Z.

For
\xi\in\Lambda
, a short computation shows that

\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=V_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}.

We conclude that

\mathcal{H}_{V}=(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\})\times L^{2}(JV_{0}),

and the
G
-representation in each fiber is given by

\pi_{\xi}(X+JY+tZ)f(x):=\mathrm{e}^{i\xi(t+\omega(X,x))}f(x+JY),\quad X,Y\in V_{0},\;t\in\mathbb{R},\;f\in L^{2}(JV_{0}),

where we identify
\mathfrak{z}^{*}=\mathbb{R}
via the basis
Z
. The reader should note that the construction does not depend on the Jordan-Hölder basis in this case, but only on the complex structure and the Lagrangian subspace
V_{0}
. This gives an explicit isomorphism
I_{G}\cong C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0})))
and a short exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0})))\to C^{*}(\mathbb{H}_{n})\to C_{0}(V^{*})\to 0.

We also note that since
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata, Proposition
0.8.14
implies that
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
(which also follows from that
\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1))=\check{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\times},U(1))\cong 0
.

It is now possible to describe the cocycle
c_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}
.
Recall the definition of the flatness cocycle
c_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\in Z^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n}),U(1))
from Definition
0.8.9
.

Proposition 0.9.12
.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{n}
denote the Heisenberg Lie group constructed from
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}
. The cohomology class
[c_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}]\in H^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n}),U(1))
is non-trivial and belongs to the image of

H^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n}),C(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/8))\to H^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n}),C(\Gamma,U(1))).

Remark 0.9.13
.

The action of
{\rm Sp}(V,\omega)\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n})
on
\Gamma
is trivial. It is well known that
{\rm Sp}(V,\omega)
has a unique two fold cover
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)
and the action on
I
lifts to an action of
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)
on
\mathcal{H}_{V}=(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\})\times L^{2}(iV_{0})
– the Segal-Shale-Weil representation. See more in
[
71
, Chapter 4]
. In particular,
[c_{{\rm Sp}(V,\omega)}]
is in the image of
H^{2}({\rm Sp}(V,\omega),\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{2}({\rm Sp}(V,\omega),U(1))
.

Proposition 0.9.14
.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{n}
denote the Heisenberg Lie group constructed from
V\ltimes_{\omega}\mathbb{R}
. The action of
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)
on
\mathcal{H}_{V}
extends uniquely to an action of
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2
lifting the action on
I_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}
. In particular, for

\text{\mxedr.g}=(SO(V)\cap Sp(V,\omega))\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=U(V)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},

we have that

[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,U(1))).

In particular, there is a
\mxedr
.g
-equivariant isomorphism
I_{G}\cong C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0})))
for a suitable unitary representation of
\mxedr
.g
on the trivial bundle
\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times L^{2}(JV_{0})\to\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

We can also lift the action of
{\rm Sp}(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n})
to a projective action on
\mathcal{H}_{V}
. We can decompose
\mathcal{H}_{V}
over
\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\dot{\cup}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as
\mathcal{H}_{V}=\mathcal{H}_{V<}\dot{\cup}\mathcal{H}_{V>0}
. The
\mathbb{Z}/2
-factor interchanges the two pieces of
\mathcal{H}_{V}
and we can lift its action to the fiber by defining it as complex conjugation
\mathcal{H}_{V,\xi}=L^{2}(JV_{0})\to L^{2}(JV_{0})=\mathcal{H}_{V,-\xi}
. This produces an action of
\mathbb{Z}/2
on
\mathcal{H}_{V}
that lifts the action on
I_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}
. A short comparison with the construction of the Segal-Shale-Weil representation (see
[
71
, Equation (4.24), (4.25), (4.26)]
) shows that this induces an action of
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2
.

The final conclusion of the proposition follows from that
\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)
is defined from a cocycle which is cohomologically trivial on
U(V)
(as the Weil-Shale-Weil representation restricts to a representation of
U(V)
), so the inclusion
U(V)\to{\rm Sp}(V,\omega)
factors over an inclusion
U(V)\to\widetilde{{\rm Sp}}(V,\omega)
.
∎

Example 0.9.15
.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}
be the complexification of the Heisenberg group. That is we define its Lie algebra as
\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{h}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
viewed as a real Lie algebra. The complex Heisenberg group is the local model for certain polycontact structures constructed from complex manifolds, see Example
0.16.7
below. If
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is defined from an inner product space
V
with a non-degenerate
2
-form
\omega
, then identifying
V_{\mathbb{C}}:=V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=V+iV=V\oplus V
, we have that
\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}=(V\oplus V)_{W}
where
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V\oplus V)
is spanned by

\omega^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega&0\\
0&-\omega\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\omega^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega\\
\omega&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that the complexification
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\omega
can be identified with
\omega^{1}+i\omega^{2}
. The constructions can be carried out starting from a complex vector space
V_{\mathbb{C}}
equipped with a complex non-degenerate
2
-form
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
, but for simplicity we start from
V
which produces a real subspace of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
which is a Lagrangian for the imaginary part of
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{h}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is a step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra of type
(2,2\dim(V))
with
C(\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{z}
. Most things are quite similar to the Heisenberg group reviewed above in Example
0.9.11
,. For instance, there is a standard grading defined from
(\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}})_{-1}=V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, we have that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}})=u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}})
, where
H_{\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}}\subseteq GL_{\mathbb{R}}(V\otimes\mathbb{C})
consists of those
g
preserving the linear span of
\omega^{1}
and
\omega^{2}
. The group
H_{\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}}
contains a subgroup of the form
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})\times U(1)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
denotes the complex symplectic group on the complex symplectic space
(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
, and
U(1)
acts via complex multiplication on
V_{\mathbb{C}}
. We also have that

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{R}^{2})\rtimes H_{\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}}.

For
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}
, a short computation proves

\mathsf{Pf}(\omega_{\xi})=\mathsf{Pf}(\xi_{1}\omega^{1}+\xi_{2}\omega^{2})=(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})^{\dim(V)/2}.

Therefore, the flat orbits are computed as

\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Gamma_{\partial}\cong S^{1}.

The action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}})
on
\Gamma
is linear, so the action on
\Gamma_{\partial}
factors over a quotient
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}})\to U(1)
acting by rotation. A big difference to the real Heisenberg group is that
\Gamma
consists of multiple fine stratas; from a choice of ON-basis on
V
we can construct a Jordan-Hölder basis of
\mathfrak{h}_{n,\mathbb{C}}
and the top fine strata in this basis will be
\Gamma
with a coordinate axis removed.

Following the same arguments as in Example
0.9.11
, for any Lagrangian
V_{0}\subseteq V
, the abelian Lie algebra
V_{0,\mathbb{C}}+\mathfrak{z}
defines a polarization of all flat orbits, and the representation defined from
\xi\in\Gamma
takes the form

\pi_{\xi}(X+JY+tZ)f(x):=\mathrm{e}^{i\xi(t+\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(X,x))}f(x+JY),

for
X,Y\in V_{0,\mathbb{C}}
,
t\in\mathbb{C}
, and
f\in L^{2}(JV_{0,\mathbb{C}})
. Here we have fixed a complex structure
J
on
V
, and where we have identified
\mathfrak{z}=\mathbb{C}
and
\xi
with an
\mathbb{R}
-linear functional
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
. Similarly to Example
0.9.11
, we arrive at an explicit isomorphism
I_{\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}}\cong C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0,\mathbb{C}})))
and a short exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0,\mathbb{C}})))\to C^{*}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}})\to C_{0}(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})\to 0.

Since there is a global polarization of the flat orbits, Proposition
0.8.14
implies that
\delta_{\rm DD}(I_{\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}})=0\in H^{3}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{Z})
(which also follows from the argument above) and
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}),\check{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},U(1)))=H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}),\mathbb{Z})
. We moreover have that

[c_{\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})}]=0,

since the
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
is simply connected. In particular, we conclude from Theorem
0.8.11
that there is an
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
-equivariant isomorphism

I_{\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}}\cong C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(JV_{0,\mathbb{C}}))),

for a suitable unitary representation of
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
on the trivial bundle
\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times L^{2}(JV_{0,\mathbb{C}})\to\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
. We note that
\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
acts trivially on
\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
since it acts trivially on the center of the Lie algebra.

Example 0.9.16
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a product of Heisenberg groups. This is the local model for the tangent group of a product of contact manifolds. For notational convenience we write
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{H}_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathbb{H}_{n_{k}}
and assume that each
\mathbb{H}_{n_{j}}
is defined from a non-zero real inner product space
V_{j}
with equipped with a non-degenerate
\omega^{j}\in\mathfrak{so}(V_{j})
. Then
\mathsf{G}
is of type
(p,q)
where:

p=k,\text{ and }q=2(n_{1}+\ldots n_{k}).

For notational convenience, we write
V:=\oplus_{j=1}^{k}V_{j}
and
\mathfrak{z}=\oplus_{j=1}^{k}\mathfrak{z}_{j}
where
\mathfrak{z}_{j}
is the center of the
j
:th factor. We note that
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
in this case. We call the grading

\mathfrak{g}_{-1}:=V\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{-1}:=\mathfrak{z},

the standard grading on
\mathsf{G}
as it it is the only grading for which all projections onto the standardly graded factors
\mathbb{H}_{n_{j}}
are graded.

Let us describe the automorphisms. By Proposition
0.9.3
,

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(V,\mathfrak{z})\rtimes H_{\mathfrak{g}},

where we for rank reasons can write

\displaystyle H_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in GL(V):\exists(\lambda_{ij}(\varphi))_{i,j=1}^{k}\mbox{ s.t. }\;\varphi^{t}\omega_{i}\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{ij}(\varphi)\omega_{j}\}=

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in GL(V):\forall i\exists j,\lambda\neq 0\mbox{ with }n_{i}=n_{j}\mbox{ s.t. }\;\varphi^{t}\omega_{i}\varphi=\lambda\omega_{j}\}\cong

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{k}\times({\rm Sp}(V_{1},\omega_{1})\times\cdots\times{\rm Sp}(V_{k},\omega_{k}))\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{k})\rtimes F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})},

and
F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})}
is the finite group of all permutations on
k
elements
\sigma\in S_{k}
such that
n_{i}=n_{\sigma(i)}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
. If
\mathsf{G}
is equipped with the standard grading, then

\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})=u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{g}}).

The flat orbits are readily described because we are dealing with a product. A similar argument as in Example
0.9.11
shows that

\Gamma=(\mathfrak{z}^{*}_{1}\setminus\{0\})\times\cdots\times(\mathfrak{z}^{*}_{k}\setminus\{0\})=\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{k}\neq 0\}.

A computation shows that
\Gamma
consists of one fine strata in a suitable Jordan-Hölder basis. The action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
on
\Gamma
factors over the quotient mapping

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to(\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{k}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{k})\rtimes F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})}=(\mathbb{R}^{\times})^{k}\rtimes F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})},

and
(\mathbb{R}^{\times})^{k}\rtimes F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})}
acts on
\Gamma
by rescaling and permutation of the coordinates.
By Proposition
0.7.6
, we can identify

\Gamma_{\partial}=\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{k}=\pm 1\}.

We remark that if we choose a different grading than the standard grading, the action of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})
on
\Gamma_{\partial}
can be quite complicated.

If we pick an adapted complex structure on
V_{j}
and a Lagrangian subspaces
V_{0,j}\subseteq V_{j}
for each
j
, an argument as in Example
0.9.11
shows that we for a suitable Jordan-Hölder basis arrive at the Vergne polarization

\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=V_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{z},

for each
\xi\in\Gamma
where
V_{0}:=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}V_{0,j}
. We conclude that

\mathcal{H}_{V}=\Gamma\times L^{2}(JV_{0}),

and the
\mathsf{G}
-representation in the fiber over
\xi\in\Gamma\subseteq\mathfrak{z}^{*}
is given by

\pi_{\xi}(X+JY+Z)f(x):=\mathrm{e}^{i\sum_{j=1}^{k}\xi_{j}(Z_{j}+\omega^{j}(X_{j},x_{j}))}f(x+JY),

for
X,Y\in V_{0}
,
Z\in\mathfrak{z}
, and
f\in L^{2}(JV_{0})
.

Following the argument for the Heisenberg group from Proposition
0.9.14
, we can describe the restriction of the flatness cocycle
c_{\rm flat}\in Z^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))
to certain subgroups. We remark that Proposition
0.9.14
ensures that
[c_{\rm flat}]\in H^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))
is non-trivial as its restriction to
H^{2}({\rm Sp}(V_{1},\omega_{1})\times\cdots\times{\rm Sp}(V_{k},\omega_{k}),C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))
is non-trivial (but
\mathbb{Z}/2
-valued). We note that for

\text{\mxedr.g}_{0}:=(U(V_{1})\times\cdots\times U(V_{k}))\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{k},

and an argument as in Proposition
0.9.14
implies that
[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}_{0}}]=0\in H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g}_{0},C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))
. Moreover, for

\text{\mxedr.g}:=\text{\mxedr.g}_{0}\rtimes F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})}

we have by a standard argument that

[c_{\text{\mxedr.g}}]\in\mathrm{i}\mathrm{m}\,(H^{2}(F_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})},C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times}))\to H^{2}(\text{\mxedr.g},C(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}^{\times})).

Example 0.9.17
.

For
n>1
, we consider the
2n-1
-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}
such that

[X_{i},X_{i+1}]=Y_{i},\quad i=1,2,\ldots,n-1

with all other brackets in the basis vectors being zero. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent of step length
2
and arises as the quotient of the nilpotent Lie algebra of step length
n
consisting of all real
(n+1)\times(n+1)
upper triangular nilpotent matrices (see more in Example
0.9.23
) by the ideal of all its
n-2
:nd order commutators. We have that
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}=\oplus_{j=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{R}Y_{j}
, and
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
(n-1,n)
and is determined by
\omega^{1},\ldots,\omega^{n-1}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)
given as

\omega^{j}=\iota_{j}\omega^{0}q_{j},

where
\omega^{0}
is the standard symplectic form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\iota_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the embedding in the oriented basis
(X_{j},X_{j+1})
and
q_{j}:=\iota_{j}^{*}
is the projection
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
onto the basis
(X_{j},X_{j+1})
. We note that the associated linear span
W\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(n)
consists of all anti-symmetric matrices
\omega=(\omega_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
with
\omega_{ij}=0
if
|i-j|>1
.

A grading on
\mathfrak{g}
is characterized by an
n
-tuple
(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})
, where
l_{j}\geq 1
for all
j
with equality for at least one
j
. The grading is defined so that
X_{j}
has degree
l_{j}
. Indeed, for such an
n
-tuple we can write

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{p}:=\left(\bigoplus_{j:\,l_{j}=p}\mathbb{R}X_{j}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{j:\,l_{j}+l_{j+1}=p}\mathbb{R}Y_{j}\right).

We call
(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})=(1,\ldots,1)
the standard grading on
\mathfrak{g}
, in this grading the center has degree
2
.

The automorphism group of
\mathfrak{g}
is determined by Proposition
0.9.3
, so

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\rtimes H_{\mathfrak{g}},

where

H_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{g\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{R}):(g^{t}\omega^{i}g)_{jk}=0\;\forall i=1,\ldots,n-1,\;|j-k|>1\}.

In general, there are very few compact subgroups of
H_{\mathfrak{g}}
. In the standard grading,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})=u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

Proposition 0.9.18
.

Consider the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
from the preceding paragraphs with
n=4
, i.e. the
2
-step
7
-dimensional Lie algebra defined from the bracket relations

[X_{1},X_{2}]=Y_{1},\;[X_{2},X_{3}]=Y_{2},\;[X_{3},X_{4}]=Y_{3}.

Let
V
denote the linear span of
\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4}\}
. Then for any Riemannian metric on
V
,
H_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap O(V)
is finite. In particular, there are no infinite compact subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})
for
n=4
.

The take away from this proposition is that in order to expect interesting geometric examples modelled on the Lie algebra appearing in this example, we need to use non-compact structure groups.

Proof.

We have that
H_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap O(V)
is compact, so it suffices to prove that
(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}\cap O(V)
is finite, where
(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}
denotes the connected component containing the identity. A lengthier computation with the Lie algebra of
H_{\mathfrak{g}}
shows that

(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}=\left\{g\in GL(4):g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&0&0&0\\
g_{21}&g_{22}&0&g_{24}\\
g_{31}&0&g_{33}&g_{34}\\
0&0&0&g_{44}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

For
g\in(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}
we have that
g\in O(V)
if and only if

\begin{cases}g_{22}=\pm 1\\
g_{33}=\pm 1\\
g_{21}g_{22}=g_{22}g_{24}=g_{24}g_{22}=g_{34}g_{33}=0\\
g_{11}^{2}+g_{21}^{2}+g_{31}^{2}=1,\\
g_{24}^{2}+g_{34}^{2}+g_{44}^{2}=1,\\
g_{21}g_{24}+g_{31}g_{34}=0.\end{cases}

The first three equations implies that
g\in(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}\cap O(V)
if and only if
g\in O(V)
is diagonal. We conclude that
(H_{\mathfrak{g}})_{(0)}\cap O(V)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{4}
is finite.

The final conclusion follows from that if
\text{\mxedr.g}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})=u_{H}(H_{\mathfrak{g}})
is compact it acts isometrically on
V
for some Riemannian metric and by the previous argument we have that
\mxedr
.g
is finite.
∎

We compute the flat orbits using Equation (
19
):

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\det(\xi_{1}\omega^{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n-1}\omega^{n-1})\neq 0\}=

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset,\;&\mbox{if $n$ is odd},\\
\left\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi_{1}\xi_{3}\cdots\xi_{n-1}\neq 0\right\},\;&\mbox{if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

The space
\Gamma
consists of multiple fine stratas. Indeed, for
n
odd, any antisymmetric matrix is degenerate and for
n
even induction on
n/2
shows that
\det(\xi_{1}\omega^{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n-1}\omega^{n-1})=\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{3}^{2}\cdots\xi_{n-1}^{2}
.
Using Proposition
0.7.6
, we identify

\Gamma_{\partial}=\begin{cases}\emptyset,\;&\mbox{if $n$ is odd},\\
\left\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}:\xi_{1}\xi_{3}\cdots\xi_{n-1}=\pm 1\right\},\;&\mbox{if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Remark 0.9.19
.

Consider
n=3
for which the above construction produces the 5-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y_{1},Y_{2}
subject to the relations

[X_{1},X_{2}]=Y_{1},\ [X_{2},X_{3}]=Y_{2},

and all other brackets being zero. This nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is of step length
2
, of type
(2,3)
with
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}
but admits no flat orbits.

We henceforth restrict our attention to
n
being even, so that there exists flat orbits. The Vergne polarization of the flat orbits is computed using Proposition
0.6.8
to be

\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{z}+\sum_{j=1}^{n/2}\mathbb{R}X_{2j-1},

for all
\xi\in\Lambda
. Note that
\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
is abelian and independent of
\xi
. Using the same argument as in Proposition
0.9.9
we conclude that

\mathcal{H}_{V}=\Lambda\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n/2}),

and the
\mathsf{G}
-representation in the fiber over
\xi\in\Gamma\subseteq\mathfrak{z}^{*}
is given by

\displaystyle\pi_{\xi}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}X_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}y_{j}Y_{j}\right)\right)f(t_{1},t_{3},\ldots,t_{n-1}):=

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{ih(\xi,t,x,y)}f(t_{1}+x_{1},t_{3}+x_{3},\ldots,t_{n-1}+x_{n-1}),

for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n/2})
, where

\displaystyle h(\xi,t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\xi\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}y_{j}Y_{j}\right)+\xi\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}X_{j},\sum_{l=1}^{n/2}t_{2l-1}X_{2l-1}\right]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\xi_{j}y_{j}-\xi_{1}x_{1}t_{1}+\sum_{l=2}^{n/2}(\xi_{2l-2}x_{2l-2}-\xi_{2l-1}x_{2l})t_{2l-1}.

Since there is a global polarization of the flat orbits, Proposition
0.8.14
implies that
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{1}(\Gamma,U(1)))
. In lack of a good description of the automorphisms, we settle for noting that there is an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-equivariant isomorphism
I_{\mathsf{G}}\cong C_{0}(\Gamma,\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n/2})))
for some projective
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-action on the trivial bundle
\Gamma\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n/2})\to\Gamma
.

Example 0.9.20
.

Consider the free step
2
nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
on a finite-dimensional real inner product space
V
. In the notations of above,
\mathfrak{g}=V_{\mathfrak{so}(V)}
and
\mathfrak{g}
satisfies
C(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{z}=\mathfrak{so}(V)
. Indeed, the only nontrivial bracket is for
x,y\in V\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
and it is given by

[x,y]=x\wedge y,

under the identification
\mathfrak{so}(V)\cong\wedge^{2}V
defined from the inner product. The free step
2
nilpotent Lie algebra is of type
(n(n-1)/2,n)
where
n:=\dim(V)
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence of vector space gradings
V=\bigoplus_{p<0}V_{p}
with
V_{-1}\neq 0
and Lie algebra gradings
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p}
given by associating a grading on
\mathfrak{g}
with a grading on
V
by

\mathfrak{g}_{p}:=V_{p}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{j+l=p}V_{j}\wedge V_{l}\right).

The inverse construction induces a grading on
V
from a grading on
\mathfrak{g}
by declaring the quotient
\mathfrak{g}\to V
to be graded. We call the grading with
V
having constant degree
-1
the standard grading.

By universality and Proposition
0.9.3
,

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(V,\mathfrak{so}(V))\rtimes GL(V),

and

\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})=u_{H}(GL_{\rm gr}(V)),

in the standard grading. Equation (
19
) shows that we can identify

\Gamma=\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap GL(V)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Gamma_{\partial}=\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap SL(V),

using the self-duality
\mathfrak{so}(V)^{*}=\mathfrak{so}(V)
. We note that
\Gamma
is empty if
\dim(V)
is odd and in this case readily seen to be a Zariski open subset of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
if
\dim(V)
is even. The action of the automorphisms of
\mathsf{G}
factors over the surjection
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\to GL(V)
and
GL(V)
acts on
\Gamma=\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap GL(V)
by matrix multiplication

\varphi.\xi:=\varphi^{t}\xi\varphi\in\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap GL(V).

Since any non-degenerate two form admits a basis in which it is the standard symplectic form
\omega_{0}
on
V
, the
GL(V)
-action is transitive and there is a
GL(V)
-equivariant homeomorphism

GL(V)/\mathrm{Sp}(V,\omega_{0})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma.

As above, we pick a Jordan-Hölder basis for
\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{n(n-1)/2}=\mathfrak{z}=\mathfrak{so}(V)
(for
n=\dim(V)
). Such a Jordan-Hölder basis induces a basis of
V
and a filtration
V_{j}:=\mathfrak{g}_{n(n-1)/2+j)}/\mathfrak{z}
of
V
. We can describe the Vergne polarization of the flat orbits using Proposition
0.6.8
as

\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=\mathfrak{z}+\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(V)}\ker(\xi|_{V_{j}}),

for all
\xi\in\Lambda=\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap GL(V)
. We note that
\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
is neither abelian nor constant in
\xi\in\Lambda
. Moreover, it does not extend from the top fine strata (defined relative to the fixed Jordan-Hölder basis) to all flat orbits. We shall therefore take a more direct route using Fock-Bargmann spaces (see for instance
[
71
, Chapter 1.6]
) to constructing a bundle of flat representations. An additional feature that comes in handy is that orthogonal automorphisms act most naturally on the Fock-Bargmann spaces. We only consider the case that
V
is even-dimensional, so there exists flat orbits.

We fix an inner product metric on
V
, it sets up a linear bijection between antisymmetric forms and
\mathfrak{so}(V)\subseteq\mathrm{End}(V)
, and between symmetric forms and symmetric elements of
\mathrm{End}(V)
. In terms of this fixed inner product, there is a bijective correspondences between symplectic forms
\omega
and pairs
(J,g)
of inner products
g
on
V
and a complex structure
J
on
V
with
g(x,Jy)=-g(Jx,y)
for all
x,y\in V
. Indeed, the pair
(J,g)
determines the symplectic form
\omega_{(J,g)}(x,y)=g(x,Jy)
, and conversely we can given
\omega
define
g_{\omega}:=(-\omega^{2})^{1/2}
and
J_{\omega}:=(-\omega^{2})^{-1/2}\omega
. We remark that

\langle x,y\rangle_{\omega}:=g_{\omega}(x,y)+i\omega(x,y),

defines a hermitean inner product on
V
.

Given a symplectic form
\omega
on
V
, we can define the associated Fock-Bargmann space as
\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega):=\mathcal{O}(V)\cap L^{2}(V,\mathrm{e}^{-g_{\omega}})
. That is,
\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega)
is the subspace of holomorphic functions (with respect to the complex structure
J_{\omega}
) in the weighted
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}(V,\mathrm{e}^{-g_{\omega}})
defined from the weight
\mathrm{e}^{-g_{\omega}}(x):=\mathrm{e}^{-g_{\omega}(x,x)}
. One can equivalently define
\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega):=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}V^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{s}k}
, were
V^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{s}0}=\mathbb{C}
and
V^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{s}k}
is the
k
-fold symmetric tensor product of
V^{*}
over
\mathbb{C}
defined with respect to the complex structure
J_{\omega}
, with Hilbert space structure defined from
g_{\omega}
.

The Bargmann-Fock representation of
\mathfrak{h}_{\dim(V)/2}=V_{\mathbb{R}\omega}
(see for instance
[
71
, Chapter 1.6]
) extends to the free step
2
nilpotent Lie group
\pi_{\omega}:\mathsf{G}\to U(\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega))
by identifying
\omega
with a central character, using that
\Gamma=\mathfrak{so}(V)\cap GL(V)
, and by defining

\pi_{\omega}(X)f(x):=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{g_{\omega}(X,X)}{2}-\langle x,X\rangle_{\omega}}f(x+X),

(21)

for
X\in X
and
f\in\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega)
. By construction,
\pi_{\omega}
is a unitary irreducible representation representing the orbit
\omega\in\Gamma
under the Kirillov correspondence (beware that we follow the convention of identifying
\Lambda
with the set of flat orbits
\Gamma\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}/\mathsf{Ad}^{*}
). Since this construction depends continuously on
\omega
, some arguments with the Bargmann transform shows that the family of Hilbert spaces
(\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega))_{\omega\in\Gamma}
and representations
(\pi_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Gamma}
glues together to a locally trivial bundle of Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
with a strongly continuous representation of
\mathsf{G}
.

The group
O(V)
(defined with respect to the fixed inner product) is a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathsf{G})
and acts on the flat orbits
\Gamma
by
\alpha.\omega:=\alpha^{t}\omega\alpha
. The homeomorphism
\Gamma=GL(V)\cap\mathfrak{so}(V)\cong GL(V)/\mathrm{Sp}(V,\omega_{0})
is clearly
O(V)
-equivariant for the left action on
GL(V)/\mathrm{Sp}(V,\omega_{0})
. The
O(V)
-action on
\Gamma
lifts to a strongly continuously action on the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}
, by simply letting
\alpha\in O(V)
act as the unitary

\alpha:\mathpzc{F}(V,\omega)\to\mathpzc{F}(V,\alpha.\omega),\;\alpha f:=\alpha^{*}f.

This map is well defined due to the fact that
J_{\alpha.\omega}=\alpha^{-1}J_{\omega}\alpha
and
g_{\alpha.\omega}=\alpha^{t}g_{\omega}\alpha
, so the action is well defined as soon as
\alpha^{t}=\alpha^{-1}
, i.e.
\alpha
orthogonal.

We summarize this argument in the following proposition.

Proposition 0.9.21
.

We let
\mathsf{G}
denote free step
2
nilpotent Lie group on the even-dimensional inner product space
V
with space of flat orbits
\Gamma=GL(V)\cap\mathfrak{so}(V)
being all symplectic forms on
V
. The
O(V)
-equivariant bundle of Bargmann-Fock spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
satisfies that

I_{\mathsf{G}}\cong C_{0}(\Gamma,\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\Gamma})),

as
O(V)
-equivariant
C_{0}(\Gamma)-C^{*}
-algebras via the family
(\pi_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Gamma}
. In particular,

\begin{cases}[\zeta|_{O(V)}]=0\in H^{1}(O(V),\check{H}^{2}(GL(V)\cap\mathfrak{so}(V),\mathbb{Z}))=0,\;\mbox{ and}\\
c_{O(V)}=0\in H^{2}(O(V),C(GL(V)\cap\mathfrak{so}(V),U(1))).\end{cases}

0.9.3.
Nilpotent Lie groups with one-dimensional center

Let us consider another class of nilpotent Lie groups, namely those with a one-dimensional center. Given any nilpotent Lie group, a quotient by a codimension one subgroup of the center produces a nilpotent Lie group with one-dimensional center, making the case of one-dimensional center interesting. We shall also consider a construction found in
[
112
]
that realize a nilpotent Lie group as a closed subgroup of a nilpotent Lie group with one-dimensional center admitting flat orbits – this example holds potential for index theoretical considerations.

Assume that
\mathsf{G}
is a simply connected nilpotent Lie group with one-dimensional center, and as above
\mathfrak{g}
denotes its Lie algebra that we equipp with a choice of Jordan-Hölder basis such that
X_{1}
is central. As in Remark
0.6.7
, there is an anti-symmetric mapping
\omega:(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\wedge(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{z})\to\mathfrak{z}
. The group
\mathsf{G}
admits flat orbits if and only if
\omega
is non-degenerate, and Remark
0.6.7
shows that

\Gamma=\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}.

If
\mathsf{G}
is graded, the associated dilation on
\mathfrak{z}
is scalar and we can identify
\Gamma_{\partial}=\{\xi\in\Gamma:\xi(X_{1})=\pm 1\}
.

To simplify our discussion, we assume that
\mathsf{G}
is graded. If
\xi_{0}\in\Gamma
and
\mathfrak{h}
is a real algebraic polarization of
\xi_{0}
, we can extend
\mathfrak{h}
to a bundle of polarizations of
\Gamma
by setting

\mathfrak{h}(\xi):=\delta_{t}\mathfrak{h},\quad\mbox{for $t>0$ such that $\xi=\pm\delta_{t}(\xi_{0})$.}

If
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi_{0})
and the Jordan-Hölder basis is homogeneous for the grading, we have that
\mathfrak{h}(\xi)=\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\Gamma
. In general, it is non-trivial to describe
\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)
beyond Proposition
0.6.8
. But we note the following concerning the ideal
I\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
, if
\mathfrak{h}
is any real algebraic polarization at
\xi_{0}\in\Gamma
, and
\pi_{\pm}:\mathsf{G}\to U(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
denotes the representations associated with
\pm\xi_{0}
(polarized by
\mathfrak{h}
) and
d=\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})/2=(\dim(\mathsf{G})-1)/2
, the dilation induces an explicit isomorphism

I_{\mathsf{G}}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))),\quad a\mapsto(\xi\mapsto\pi_{\mathrm{sign}(\xi)}(\delta_{t(\xi)}(a))),

where
\mathrm{sign}(\xi)\in\{\pm\}
and
t(\xi)>0
are determined by
\xi=\mathrm{sign}(\xi)t(\xi)\xi_{0}
. For nilpotent Lie groups with one-dimensional center, the ideal of flat orbits fits into an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant short exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))\to C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to C^{*}(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{Z})\to 0.

Example 0.9.22
.

We start with an example of a nilpotent Lie group with one-dimensional center lacking flat orbits. Consider the
4
-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
,
Y_{3}
, and
Y_{4}
subject to the relations

[Y_{2},Y_{4}]=Y_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad[Y_{3},Y_{4}]=Y_{2},

and all other Lie brackets between the basis vectors are zero. This example was studied in
[
40
, Example 1.3.10 and 2.2.2]
and
[
97
, Chapter 3.3]
. We grade
\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{-3}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
where
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
is spanned by
Y_{3},Y_{4}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
is spanned by
Y_{2}
and the center
\mathfrak{g}_{-3}=\mathfrak{z}
is spanned by
Y_{1}
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is step
3
nilpotent with one-dimensional center. The associated Lie group
\mathsf{G}
readily seen to be a subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{3}
(see Example
0.9.23
). A short computation shows that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}\rtimes(\mathbb{R}^{\times})^{2}
with action induced from an action on
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
, so the Jordan-Hölder flag defined from
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3},Y_{4}
is invariant under graded automorphisms, so computations in this basis globalizes in a controlled way. Since the centre has odd codimension there exists no flat orbits. The top coarse strata will however consist of generic orbits and produce structures similar to that of the flat orbits. We include this example to indicate how the techniques of this monograph can be extended beyond the usage of flat orbits.

We use
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3},Y_{4}
as a Jordan-Hölder basis and we use the dual basis for
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with associated coordinates
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}
. Following
[
40
, Example 1.3.10 and 2.2.2]
and
[
97
, Chapter 3.3]
, the embedding
(p,q)\mapsto(0,\frac{q}{2p},0,p)
identify

\tilde{\Gamma}:=\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}_{q},

with an open, dense Hausdorff subset of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
lifting to the subset
\{\xi:\xi_{4}\neq 0\}
. under the projection map
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\hat{\mathsf{G}}
. The inverse to the identification is given by
p(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})=\xi_{1}
and
q(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})=2\xi_{3}\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}^{2}
. Each element
\xi\in\tilde{\Gamma}
defines a two-dimensional coadjoint orbit and is polarized by the abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathbb{R}Y_{1}+\mathbb{R}Y_{2}+\mathbb{R}Y_{3}
. For dimensional reasons,
\tilde{\Gamma}
can be identified with the top coarse strata in
\hat{G}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}
is preserved by all of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
. For
\xi\in\tilde{\Gamma}
, we construct the representation

\pi_{\xi}:\mathsf{G}\to U(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})),\quad\pi_{\xi}:=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\,_{\mathsf{H}}^{\mathsf{G}}\chi_{\xi},

where
\mathsf{H}
is the Lie group constructed from
\mathfrak{h}
,
\chi_{\xi}:H\to U(1)
the character defined from
\xi
and where we have identified
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{H}=\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
denote the ideal corresponding to
\tilde{\Gamma}
. The arguments above shows that the family
(\pi_{\xi})_{\xi\in\tilde{\Gamma}}
induces an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-equivariant isomorphism

\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}}\cong C_{0}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))),

for some projective
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-action on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular,
\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}}
is an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-continuous trace algebra with
\delta_{\rm DD}(\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}})=0\in\check{H}^{3}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{Z})
and
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),\check{H}^{2}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{Z}))
.

Example 0.9.23
.

We proceed with a family of nilpotent Lie groups with one-dimensional center lacking flat orbits. Let
\mathsf{G}_{n}
denote the nilpotent Lie group of real
(n+1)\times(n+1)
upper triangular unipotent matrices. This is a nilpotent Lie group of step length
n
. Its center is one-dimensional and consists of matrices
g=(g_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n+1}
such that
g_{ij}=0
unless
i=j
or
i=1
and
j=n+1
(the rigthmost, upmost entry can be non-zero). For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{z}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
, the Lie algebra
\mathsf{stab}(\xi)
consists of matrices with zero entries on and below the diagonal as well as in the right most column with the exception of the rigthmost, upmost entry. In particular,
\mathrm{codim}(\mathsf{stab}(\xi))=n-1<\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
, so there exists no flat orbits on
\mathsf{G}_{n}
if
n>2
even if
\mathrm{codim}(\mathfrak{z})
for certain
n
is even-dimensional.

The case
n=3
was computed in more detail in
[
40
, Example 1.3.11 and 2.2.3]
. In the same way as in Example
0.9.22
,
[
40
, Example 1.3.11 and 2.2.3]
provides an open dense Hausdorff subset
\tilde{\Gamma}\cong\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\mathbb{R}
of
\hat{\mathsf{G}}_{3}
. Each coadjoint orbit defined from elements of
\tilde{\Gamma}
are of dimension
4
and the points of
\tilde{\Gamma}
are all polarized by the same
4
-dimensional abelian ideal
\mathfrak{h}
. For dimensional reasons,
\tilde{\Gamma}
can be identified with the top coarse strata in
\hat{G}_{3}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}
is preserved by all of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}_{3})
. Let
\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}_{3}}\subseteq C^{*}(\mathsf{G}_{3})
denote the ideal corresponding to
\tilde{\Gamma}
. The arguments above shows that the family
(\pi_{\xi})_{\xi\in\tilde{\Gamma}}
induces an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}_{3})
-equivariant isomorphism

\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}_{3}}\cong C_{0}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))),

for some projective
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}_{3})
-action on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In particular,
\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}}
is an
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}_{3})
-continuous trace algebra with
\delta_{\rm DD}(\tilde{I}_{\mathsf{G}_{3}})=0\in\check{H}^{3}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{Z})
and
[\zeta]=0
in the group
H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}_{3}),\check{H}^{2}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathbb{Z}))
. It would be interesting to extend this construction to
n>3
as it relates to parabolic geometries for
SL(n,\mathbb{R})
(see Example
0.16.11
below).

Example 0.9.24

(The Mohsen construction)
.

We will now consider a construction from
[
112
]
that to each simply connected nilpotent Lie group associates a larger simply connected Lie group with one-dimensional center and flat orbits. This construction is of interest since it creates flat orbits, and the modification is functorial enough to extend to bundle of Lie groups and to Carnot manifolds.

Consider a simply connected nilpotent Lie group
\mathsf{G}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The Lie algebra acts via the coadjoint action as Lie algebra homomorphisms on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
equipped with the trivial Lie bracket. We can form the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g}
. More precisely, for
(\eta,X),(\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
their Lie bracket is defined by

[(\eta,X),(\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime})]_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g}}:=(\mathsf{ad}^{*}(X)\eta^{\prime}-\mathsf{ad}^{*}(X^{\prime})\eta,[X,X^{\prime}]).

The duality pairing defines a symplectic form
\omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
which is readily verified to defined a cocycle on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g}
. We let
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
denote the associated central extension. More precisely,
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathbb{R}
as a vector space and the Lie bracket of
((\eta,X),t),((\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime}),t^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathbb{R}
is defined by

[((\eta,X),t),((\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime}),t^{\prime})]_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}:=([(\eta,X),(\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime})]_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g}},\omega_{\mathfrak{g}}((\eta,X),(\eta^{\prime},X^{\prime}))).

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
denote the simply connected Lie group constructed from
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. The step length of
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
is exactly one step larger than that of
\mathsf{G}
.

Definition 0.9.25
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The Lie algebra
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
constructed in the paragraph above is called the Mohsen modification of
\mathfrak{g}
and the Lie group
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
is called the Mohsen modification of
\mathsf{G}
.

It is readily verified that the center
\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}
of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
is the one-dimensional summand
\mathbb{R}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
and that the antisymmetric mapping
\omega:\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}\wedge\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}\to\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}
can be identified with the symplectic pairing
\omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
. In particular, we can from the discussion in the start of this subsubsection deduce that
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie algebra with one-dimensional center that admits flat orbits

\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{\times}=\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}\setminus\{0\}.

The automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
is slightly convoluted to describe and for our purposes it suffices to note that there are injections

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{ad}^{*}}\mathfrak{g})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}).

(22)

The Lie algebra
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
can always be graded by
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}:=\mathbb{R}
. We call this grading the standard grading on
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. In examples, when
\mathfrak{g}
is graded it is more natural to consider the induced grading on
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let us describe how to induce gradings from
\mathfrak{g}
to
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
(this construction is also found in
[
112
, Subsection 3.1]
). If
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{-n\leq p<0}\mathfrak{g}_{p}
is a grading of
\mathfrak{g}
, we define

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{p}:=\begin{cases}0,&p<-n-1,\\
\mathbb{R},&p=-n-1,\\
\mathfrak{g}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{-n-p-1},&p\geq-n,\end{cases}

In the standard grading
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
and in an induced grading
\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{g})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{\rm gr}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
.

We polarize
\xi_{0}=1\in\mathbb{R}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
by the real algebraic polarization

\mathfrak{h}:=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

For a suitable Jordan-Hölder basis and either the standard grading or an induced grading on
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
, the dilation reconstructs the Vergne polarization of the flat orbits from this polarization and
\mathfrak{h}_{V}(\xi)=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*}
for any
\xi\in\Gamma
. Let us describe the flat representations constructed from the polarization
\mathfrak{h}:=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. We have that

\mathcal{H}_{V}=\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times L^{2}(\mathsf{G}),

and the representation in the fibre over
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
is given by

\displaystyle\pi_{\xi}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\eta+z}g\right)f(t):=\mathrm{e}^{i\xi\left(z+\eta(\log(t))\right)}f(gt),

for
g\in\mathsf{G}
,
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
z\in\mathbb{R}=\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{G})
. The reader should note that the restriction of any flat orbit representation of
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
to
\mathsf{G}
is equivalent to the left regular representation of
\mathsf{G}
.

We arrive at an isomorphism
I_{\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}\cong C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\times},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathsf{G})))
and a short exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\times},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathsf{G})))\to C^{*}(\tilde{\mathsf{G}})\to C^{*}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes_{\mathsf{Ad}^{*}}\mathsf{G})\to 0.

The action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\mathsf{G}})
on
I_{\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}
restricts to the ordinary fibrewise
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
-action on
I_{\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}\cong C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\times},\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(\mathsf{G})))
under the mapping
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\mathsf{G}})
from Equation (
22
). Furthermore, we deduce that
[\zeta]=0\in H^{1}(\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}),\check{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\times},\mathbb{Z}))=0
and
[c_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G})}]=0\in H^{2}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathsf{G}),C(\mathbb{R}^{\times},U(1)))=0
.

Part II
Groupoids and twists

Introduction to part 2

The aim of the current part is to extend the structures defined in the previous part to bundles of nilpotent Lie groups and to prove the
K
-theoretical results needed for further index theoretical considerations. The motivation to do so comes from the fact that the tangent bundle of a Carnot manifold can be seen as a groupoid globally describing the osculating, nilpotent Lie group structure on each fibre. In the following table we gather the main characters of the story and compare notation for their incarnations on the level of the fiber and the level of frame bundle
P\to X
of the bundle of Lie groups:

Lie group (group theoretic model fiber)

\mathsf{G}

Locally trivial bundle of Lie groups (e.g. the tangent groupoid of a Carnot manifold)

\mathcal{G}

Lie algebra (Lie algebraic model fiber)

\mathfrak{g}

Lie algebroid with trivial anchor map (e.g. the tangent algebroid of a Carnot manifold)

\mathbf{g}

Center

\mathsf{Z}

Central groupoid

\mathcal{Z}

Set of flat orbits

\Gamma

Fiber bundle of flat orbits

\Gamma_{P}

Total space of flat orbits

\Xi

Symplectic vector bundle of flat orbits over the total space of the fiber bundle of flat orbits

\Xi_{P}

Ideal of flat representations

I_{\mathsf{G}}

Ideal of flat orbit representations in
C^{*}(\mathcal{G})

I_{P}

Later, when
P
is constructed from a regular Carnot manifold
X
we indicate
X
rather than
P
in the notation.

Homological information about
H
-elliptic operators on Carnot manifolds is naturally contained in
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
. The aim is to develop computation tools in these
K
-groups. In particular, there will be three main results in this part:

(1)

The Connes-Thom isomorphism allow us to establish isomorphism
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathsf{G}))\simeq K_{*}(C_{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}))
, this is known since work of Nistor
[
119
]
. This result extends to the level of bundles, i.e. for a locally trivial bundle of nilpotent connected simply connected Lie groups we show that
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))\simeq K_{*}(C_{0}(\mathbf{g}^{*}))
. The technique used to construct Nistor’s Connes-Thom isomorphism is that of adiabatic deformations: a groupoid that smoothly deforms
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathbf{g}
.

(2)

We prove that the procedure of Nistor above restricts from
C^{*}(\mathcal{G})
to
I_{P}
and gives an isomorphism
K_{*}(I_{P})\simeq K^{*}(\Xi_{P})
. Both isomorphisms for
C^{*}(\mathcal{G})
and for
I_{P}
are of an abstract nature and do not provide concrete recipe for transforming cycles in
K_{*}(I_{P})
to cycles in
K^{*}(\Xi_{P})
. Extending the proof that
\delta(I_{\mathsf{G}})=0
– by constructing a concrete Morita equivalence bundle – to the case of a bundle of Lie groups we construct an isomorphism
K_{*}(I_{P})\xrightarrow{Mor}K^{*}(\Gamma_{P})\xrightarrow{Thom}K^{*}(\Xi_{P})
from Morita invariance and the Thom isomorphism. This isomorphism coincides with the Connes-Thom isomorphisms up to a line bundle called the metaplectic correction bundle.

(3)

Finally, we show that the map
K_{*}(I_{P})\rightarrow K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
, induced from inclusion, is surjective. Thus, the classes of
H
-elliptic operators in
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
can be parametrized by
K^{*}(\Gamma_{P})
as it is possible to lift to
K_{*}(I_{P})
and then use the Morita equivalence to transform to cycles in
K^{*}(\Gamma_{P})
.

In Section
0.10
we review definitions, basic facts and examples from the theory of Lie groupoids and algebroids. We recall the construction of the
C^{*}
-algebra of a Lie groupoid and the definition of the Lie algebroid associated with a Lie groupoid. In Section
0.11
we describe central decomposition of a Lie group as a twisted semidirect product. This allows to establish equivariant isomorphisms between
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Z}},\omega_{\mathsf{Z}})
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Z}}
is a certain groupoid over the dual of the center
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
. For nilpotent groups this isomorphism restricts to the ideal of flat orbits
I_{\mathsf{G}}
and provides isomorphism
I_{\mathsf{G}}\simeq C^{*}(\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma},\omega_{\Gamma})
where
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Z}}|_{\Gamma}
.

In Section
0.12
we extend the structures of Section
0.10
and
0.11
from nilpotent Lie groups to bundles of nilpotent Lie groups. For instance, we prove analogues of central decompositions considered in Section
0.11
. In Section
0.13
we construct Connes-Thom isomorphisms
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))\simeq K_{*}(C_{0}(\mathbf{g}^{*}))
and
K_{*}(I_{P})\simeq K^{*}(\Gamma_{P})
. The construction requires notions of classical adiabatic groupoids, parabolically blowed adiabatic groupoid and a cocycle deformation of the adiabatic groupoid. At the end of the section we prove surjectivity of the map
K_{*}(I_{P})\rightarrow K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
induced from the inclusion.

The results of Section
0.14
are those of most relevance for later results on index theory. A crucial result in this section states that the Connes-Thom isomorphism
K_{*}(I_{X})\simeq K^{*}(\Gamma_{X})
differs from the Morita equivalence defined from a bundle of flat orbit representations up to a line bundle – the metaplectic correction bundle. This result lies at the heart of the index theorem as it allow us to explicitly compute the Connes-Thom map
K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathsf{G}))\to K_{*}(C_{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}))
by finding a preimage under
K_{*}(I_{P})\rightarrow K_{*}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
and the applying Morita invariance.

0.10.
Lie groupoids and their
C^{*}
-algebras

A groupoid can abstractly be defined as a small category in which all morphisms are isomorphisms. The abstract definition says quite litte and should rather be interpreted as having a space (the objects) with symmetries (the morphisms) that could vary over the space. We shall solely be concerned with Lie groupoids and twists of them.

We will recall the basic definitions for groupoids for the purpose of setting notations. The reader is expected to be acquainted with groupoids. The references
[
48
,
49
,
51
,
111
,
120
,
149
,
150
]
can be consulted for a more extensive overview of groupoids.

Definition 0.10.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. A Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
over
M
is a smooth manifold of morphisms
\mathcal{G}
equipped with the following structure maps:

(1)

A source and range maps
s,r:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow M
.

(2)

A composition map
\circ:\mathcal{G}^{(2)}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, where

\mathcal{G}^{(2)}=\{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}:r(\gamma_{2})=s(\gamma_{1})\},

which is associative in the sense that
(\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2})\circ\gamma_{3}=\gamma_{1}\circ(\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{3})
whenever all compositions are defined.

(3)

A unit map
e:M\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, subject to
re=se=e
, that maps each object to the identity morphism in the sense that
e(r(\gamma))\circ\gamma=\gamma\circ e(s(\gamma))=\gamma
for each
\gamma\in G
.

(4)

An inverse map
i:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, subject to
ri=s
,
si=r
and
i^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}}
, which sends each morphism to its inverse in the sense that
i(\gamma)\circ\gamma=e(s(\gamma))
and
\gamma\circ i(\gamma)=e(r(\gamma))
.

All structure maps are required to be smooth and
s,r
are required to be submersions.

A Lie group is a Lie groupoid over a point. In the same way that a Lie group has a Lie algebra, a Lie groupoid will have a Lie algebroid.

Definition 0.10.2
.

A Lie algebroid over a smooth manifold
M
is a triple
(\mathbf{g},\rho,[\cdot,\cdot])
where

•

\mathbf{g}\rightarrow M
is a vector bundle.

•

\rho:\mathbf{g}\rightarrow TM
is a vector bundle map called the anchor map.

•

[\cdot,\cdot]:C^{\infty}(M,\mathbf{g})\times C^{\infty}(M,\mathbf{g})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,\mathbf{g})
is a Lie bracket such that

(1)

The map on sections induced from the anchor map
\rho:C^{\infty}(M,\mathbf{g})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,TM)
is a Lie algebra homomorphism.

(2)

For any
X,Y\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbf{g})
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
one has

[X,fY]=f[X,Y]+(\rho(X)f)Y.

Definition 0.10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid over
M
. The Lie algebroid
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})
of
\mathcal{G}
is defined as follows:

(1)

The vector bundle
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})=\ker Ds|_{M}
with Lie bracket defined from identifying sections of
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})
with right invariant vector fields on
\mathcal{G}
.

(2)

The anchor map
\rho=Dr:\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})\rightarrow TM
.

Remark 0.10.4
.

Under certain conditions one has an analogue of Lie’s Third Theorem, which associates a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with a Lie algebroid
\mathbf{g}
such that
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})=\mathbf{g}
. In general, it can fail, see the discussion in
[
48
, Chapter 5.6]
.

Let us describe some examples that will play an important role later on in the monograph.

Example 0.10.5

(Pair groupoid)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. The manifold
\mathcal{G}:=M\times M
is a groupoid over
M
by setting

\displaystyle r(x,y)

\displaystyle:=x,\quad s(x,y):=y,

\displaystyle(x,y)\circ(y,z)

\displaystyle:=(x,z),

\displaystyle i(x,y)

\displaystyle:=(y,x),\quad e(x):=(x,x).

Example 0.10.6

(Transformation groupoid)
.

Given a Lie group
G
acting by diffeomorphisms on a smooth manifold
M
, define the Lie groupoid
M\rtimes G:=M\times G\rightrightarrows M
with operations

\displaystyle r(x,g)

\displaystyle:=x,\quad s(x,g):=gx,

\displaystyle(x,g)\circ(xg,h)

\displaystyle:=(x,gh),

\displaystyle i(x,g)

\displaystyle:=(xg,g^{-1}),\quad e(x):=(x,e_{G}).

Example 0.10.7

(Deformation groupoid)
.

For a closed smooth submanifold
M_{0}
of a smooth manifold
M
with a normal bundle
N
. The deformation to the normal cone is the smooth manifold

D(M_{0},M)=N\times\{0\}\sqcup M\times(0,1].

See
[
50
]
for the definition of the smooth structure on
D(M_{0},M)
. A smooth groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called a deformation groupoid if
\mathcal{G}=D(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H})
for a groupoid
\mathcal{H}
and closed Lie subgroupoid
\mathcal{H}_{0}
with object space
M
.
\mathcal{G}
has object space
M\times[0,1]
. For more details on this construction and the next, see
[
50
]
. We occasionally consider the deformation groupoid construction over
[0,\infty)
instead of
[0,1]
.

Example 0.10.8

(Adiabatic groupoid)
.

Given a smooth groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
one can consider its Lie algebroid
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})
(see definition
0.10.3
).
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})
is considered to be a Lie groupoid with the vector bundle operation. It is isomorphic to the normal bundle of unit the inclusion
M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
. The adiabatic groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{ad}
is defined to be

\mathcal{G}_{\rm ad}=D(M,\mathcal{G})\equiv\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{G})\times\{0\}\sqcup\mathcal{G}\times(0,1].

Example 0.10.9

(Tangent groupoid)
.

The tangent groupoid
\mathbb{T}M\rightrightarrows M\times[0,1]
of a smooth manifold
M
is defined to be adiabatic groupoid of the pair groupoid
M\times M\rightrightarrows M
. It was first studied by Connes
[
39
]
as a way of conceptualizing the Atiyah-Singer index theorem. The Lie algebroid of
\mathbb{T}M
is
TM\times[0,1]
. An important variation of the tangent groupoid is the parabolic tangent groupoid of a Carnot manifold constructed by van Erp-Yuncken (see
[
149
]
), we review it below in the section on Carnot manifolds, see Section
0.17
.

0.10.1.
Twisted groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras

To encode the representations of a groupoid, one associates a
C^{*}
-algebra. In order to be able to describe the full range of situations arising for nilpotent groups and Carnot manifolds, we will need the generality of twisted groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras. Before defining these
C^{*}
-algebras, we recall some basic notions for groupoids.

Given a smooth groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
, for
x\in M
we denote the
s
-fiber of
\mathcal{G}
by

\mathcal{G}_{x}:=s^{-1}(\gamma)=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{G}:s(\gamma)=x\}.

By definition,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a smooth submanifold of
\mathcal{G}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{G}^{x}:=r^{-1}(x)
denotes the
r
-fibre in
x\in M
. For any
\eta\in\mathcal{G}_{x}\cap\mathcal{G}^{y}
there is an associated diffeomorphism

R_{\eta}:\mathcal{G}_{y}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{x},\ R_{\eta}(\gamma)=\gamma\circ\eta.

Definition 0.10.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid. A Haar system on
\mathcal{G}
is a family of smooth measures
(\mu_{x})_{x\in M}
, where
\mu_{x}
is a measure on the
s
-fiber
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, such that

(1)

For any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{G})
, the function
M\ni x\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}fd\mu_{x}\in\mathbb{C}
is smooth on
M
.

(2)

For any
\eta\in\mathcal{G}_{x}\cap\mathcal{G}^{y}
, the diffeomorphism
R_{\gamma}:G_{y}\rightarrow G_{x}
is measure-preserving.

We note that the second condition in the definition of a Haar system encodes that our convention is to use right invariant Haar system. Any two Haar systems on a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
differ by a smooth, positive function on
M
. Therefore, the Haar system is essentially unique.

Definition 0.10.11
.

A smooth 2-cocycle on a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is a smooth map
\omega:\mathcal{G}^{(2)}\rightarrow U(1)
such that

\omega(x,y)\omega(xy,z)=\omega(x,yz)\omega(y,z)\text{ whenever }(x,y),\ (y,z)\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)},

\omega(x,s(x))=1=\omega(r(x),x).

Definition 0.10.12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid and
\omega
a smooth
2
-cocycle on
\mathcal{G}
. The twisted convolution algebra of
(\mathcal{G},\omega)
is the
*
-algebra
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{G},\omega):=C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{G})
of compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathcal{G}
equipped with the product defined from twisted convolution

f\ast g(\gamma)=\int_{\mathcal{G}_{r(\gamma)}}\omega(\eta,\eta^{-1}\gamma)f(\eta)g(\eta^{-1}\gamma)\mathrm{d}\mu^{r(\gamma)},\quad f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{G},\omega),

and the involution is given by

f^{*}(\gamma)=\overline{f(\gamma^{-1})\omega(\gamma,\gamma^{-1})}.

Remark 0.10.13
.

We say that a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is a polynomial Lie groupoid if
M
and
\mathcal{G}
are manifolds with polynomial structures (see
[
123
]
