, where Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand constructed a BGG-complex as a method of resolving finite-dimensional representations of
G
by Verma modules for
P
being the Borel subgroup. For more details on how BGG-complexes relate to other notions, e.g. Kostant’s Borel-Bott-Weil theorem
[
101
]
or Rumin complexes, we refer the reader to
[
46
]
. In fact, we refer the entire construction of general BGG-complexes to
[
47
]
as it is quite technical. We shall in this work focus on the abstract setting coming out of
[
47
]
. As we discuss below in Section
0.25
, the associated index problem is still open but due to its relation to Julg’s approach
[
92
,
93
]
to the Baum-Connes conjecture for
Sp(n,1)
, we believe it to be of general interest.

To describe the differential complexes, that act between graded vector bundles, we need some further terminology. A vector bundle
\boldsymbol{E}\to X
is said to be graded if it is equipped with a vector bundle decomposition
\boldsymbol{E}=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{R}}\boldsymbol{E}[k]
. The space of graded Heisenberg operators of order
m
between two graded vector bundles
\boldsymbol{E}
and
\boldsymbol{F}
is defined as

\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F}):=\bigoplus_{j,k}\Psi_{H}^{m+k-j}(X;\boldsymbol{E}[k],\boldsymbol{F}[j]).

We consider an element of
T=(T_{j,k})_{j,k}\in\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F})
as a matrix, implementing an inclusion

\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F})\subseteq\Psi^{*}_{H}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F}),

into the ungraded calculus. The matrix product defines a composition

\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F})\times\Psi^{m^{\prime}}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{G})\to\Psi^{m+m^{\prime}}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{G}),

for any three graded vector bundles
\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{G}\to X
and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Similarly, we define the graded symbol algebra as

\Sigma^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F}):=\bigoplus_{j,k}\Sigma_{H}^{m+k-j}(X;\boldsymbol{E}[k],\boldsymbol{F}[j]),

and the graded symbol mapping

\sigma_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}:\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F})\to\Sigma^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{F}),\quad(T_{j,k})_{j,k}\mapsto(\sigma_{H}^{m+k-j}T_{j,k})_{j,k}.

The constructions of Subsection
0.18
extends ad verbatim to the graded case. We remark that graded pseudodifferential operators plays a central role in the theory of boundary value problems under the name of Douglis-Nirenberg calculus, see for instance
[
1
,
76
]
, see also
[
88
, Chapter XIX.5]
and
[
6
]
.

The structure arising from BGG-complexes, and the graded complexes appearing in
[
46
]
, fits into the following definition that we refine below in Definition
0.19.17
.

Definition 0.18.11
.

Consider a sequence

0\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\xrightarrow{D_{1}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{D_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{D_{N-1}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\xrightarrow{D_{N}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\to 0,

where
\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1}\to X
are graded vector bundles and, for some
m_{1},\ldots,m_{N}
, we have that
D_{j}\in\Psi^{m_{j}}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{j},\boldsymbol{E}_{j+1})
.

•

If
D_{j+1}D_{j}=0
for all
j
, we say that the sequence is a graded
H
-complex.

•

If
\sigma_{H}^{m_{j+1}}(D_{j+1})\sigma_{H}^{m_{j}}(D_{j})=0
for all
j
, we say that the sequence is a graded
H
-almost-complex.

We say that the sequence is of order
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
.

Example 0.18.12
.

An instructive example of a graded
H
-complex comes from the de Rham complex on a Carnot manifold. If
X
has a Carnot structure, we can view
\boldsymbol{E}_{j}:=\wedge^{j}T^{*}X\otimes\mathbb{C}
as graded vector bundles upon choosing a splitting
TX\cong\mathsf{gr}(TX)
. Note that
T^{*}X
is graded as a dual vector space. Therefore, the exterior differential
\mathrm{d}:C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{j})\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{j+1})
is of graded order
0
in the Heisenberg calculus. The de Rham sequence

0\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\to 0,

is a graded
H
-complex of order
\boldsymbol{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
.

More generally, if
\boldsymbol{E}\to X
is a graded vector bundle equipped with a connection
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{E}}
of graded order zero and curvature of degree
\leq-1
, then with
\boldsymbol{E}_{j}:=\wedge^{j}T^{*}X\otimes\boldsymbol{E}
, we have a sequence

0\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{E}}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{E}}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{E}}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{E}}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\to 0,

which is a graded
H
-almost-complex of order
\boldsymbol{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
.

Associated with such a graded
H
-almost-complex of differential operators defined from a graded connection, Dave-Haller
[
46
]
associates a new BGG-like sequence

0\to C^{\infty}(X;\mathcal{H}_{1})\xrightarrow{D_{1}}C^{\infty}(X;\mathcal{H}_{2})\xrightarrow{D_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{D_{N-1}}C^{\infty}(X;\mathcal{H}_{N})\xrightarrow{D_{N}}C^{\infty}(X;\mathcal{H}_{N+1})\to 0,

where the bundles are defined from Lie algebra cohomology of the fibre, producing a new graded
H
-almost-complex. This construction extends the curved BGG-complex of Cap-Slovak-Souček
[
28
]
.

An example of curved BGG-complexes that has played a particular role in the study of the Baum-Connes conjecture for rank
1
semisimple Lie groups is the Rumin complex
[
91
,
92
,
93
]
, compare to
[
153
]
for the higher rank case. In the special case of a contact manifold, a construction of the Rumin complex can be found in
[
91
]
and for the construction on a quaternionic contact manifold see
[
92
,
93
]
.

Szegö projections

A well studied class of operators in complex analysis is Szegö projections. For a domain in a complex manifold, the Szegö projection is a projection in a space of functions on the boundary onto the subspace of functions extending holomorphically to the interior. As mentioned in Example
0.16.6
, if
X=\partial\Omega
is a compact boundary of a strictly pseudoconvex domain, viewed as a Carnot manifold in its contact structure, the Szegö projection is a projection
P_{S,\Omega}\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X,\mathbb{C})
by
[
141
, Chapter III.6]
assuming the technical condition that the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
-operator on
X
has closed range on
L^{2}
. The closed range condition on the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
-operator is equivalent to
X
being
CR
-embeddable in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
(for a large
N
, see more in
[
99
]
) and is automatic if
\dim(X)\geq 5
by a theorem of Boutet de Monvel
[
20
]
.

More generally,
[
65
, Chapter 6]
considered generalized Szegö projections on a co-orientable contact manifold
X
. We return to study generalized Szegö projections further below in Example
0.19.18
.

Let us end this example by making some remarks on the Szegö projection for the boundary
X
of a weakly pseudo-convex domains. The weak pseudo-convexity produces a depth
2
Carnot structure on
X
, and it is regular of type
\mathbb{H}_{n}
if and only if
X
is strictly pseudoconvex. The situation for solving the
\bar{\partial}
-problem is more complicated in the weakly pseudoconvex case, and a well studied problem by the school of Kohn (for an overview, see
[
44
]
). For weakly pseudo-convex domains of finite type (see for instance
[
44
,
82
]
), the situation is better. A weakly pseudo-convex domain
\Omega=\{z:\phi(z)<0\}
is of finite type if the sub-bundle
H:=\ker(\mathrm{d}^{c}\phi|_{TX})\subseteq TX
is bracket generating, i.e. the Carnot structure
H
defines can be refined to a singular Carnot structure (in the sense of Remark
0.15.4
). defined by
\mathpzc{E}^{-1}=C^{\infty}(X,H)
and
\mathpzc{E}^{-j-1}=\mathpzc{E}^{-j}+[\mathpzc{E}^{-j},C^{\infty}(X,H)]
. Compare to the explicit example in Example
0.16.6
. For instance, in a weakly pseudoconvex domain of finite type the
\bar{\partial}
-problem comes with well understood sub-elliptic estimates and the Szegö projection preserves
C^{\infty}(X)
. For weakly pseudo-convex domains that are not of finite type, there are examples where sub-elliptic estimates fail, the Szegö projection does not preserve the space of real analytic functions (see
[
38
]
) and the closely related Bergman projection does not preserve the space of smooth functions (see
[
37
]
), indicating that the Heisenberg calculus is not suited for studying the Szegö projection of a pseudoconvex domain which is not of finite type. This fact indicates that Carnot manifolds and their Heisenberg calculus do not form suitable tools for dealing with weakly pseudo-convex domains that are not of finite type. It poses an interesting problem to study if the Szegö projection of a weakly pseudoconvex domain of finite type belongs to its Heisenberg calculus or perhaps a Heisenberg calculus adapted to singular Carnot manifolds, such as the calculus of
[
3
]
. The results of
[
34
,
82
]
indicate that it might be the case.

0.19.
H
-ellipticity and the Rockland condition

The symbol algebra in the Heisenberg calculus is in general a noncommutative algebra. The standard way to deal with the issues arising from such complications is to study symbols as (unbounded) multipliers on suitable domains in the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra of the oscullating Lie groupoid. As such, ellipticity properties in the calculus can be verified fibrewise in all representations of that fibre via generalizations of the Rockland theorem. We give an overview of relevant results following
[
46
]
, see also
[
66
,
67
]
.

We first introduce some notations. Let
\mathsf{G}
denote a nilpotent Lie group. Recall that a nilpotent Lie group carries a unique polynomial structure determined by the exponential map. Let
\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G})
denote the Schwartz space of
\mathsf{G}
. We write
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathsf{G})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G})
for the subspace of functions
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G})
such that

\int_{\mathsf{G}}p(g)f(g)\mathrm{d}g=0,

for all polynomials
p
on
\mathsf{G}
.

For a unitary representation
\pi:\mathsf{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
, we write

\mathcal{S}(\pi):=\pi(\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G}))\mathcal{H}_{\pi},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi):=\pi(\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathsf{G}))\mathcal{H}_{\pi}.

We note that the inclusion
\mathcal{S}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
is dense in the norm topology. Moreover, the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
is the closed subspace of all
\mathsf{G}
-invariant vectors in
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
; this follows from the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathsf{G})
is dense in the kernel of the trivial representation
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbb{C}
. In particular, the inclusion
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
is dense as soon as
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
contains no invariant vectors. We can identify
\mathcal{S}(\pi)
with the closed subspace of the Frechet space
\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{H})
consisting of the functions
f(g):=\pi(g)v
for
v\in\mathcal{H}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)
can be identified with a closed subspace of the Frechet space
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{H})
. Unless otherwise stated, we topologixe
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{S}(\pi)
as Frechet spaces using the inclusions
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{S}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{H})
, respectively. We write
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
for the space of continuous linear operators between Frechet spaces and
\mathcal{L}(V):=\mathcal{L}(V,V)
(we reserve the notation
\mathbb{B}
for the space of bounded operators between Hilbert spaces).

Before describing the general construction, let us digest a bit on the Lie algebra case. The following result is well known, and more or less follows from definition.

Proposition 0.19.1
.

Let
\pi:\mathsf{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
be a unitary representation, and define

\pi:\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}(\pi)),\ \pi(X)v:=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\pi(\exp(tX))v\quad\mbox{for}\;X\in\mathfrak{g}.

The mapping
\pi
is a well defined
*
-homomorphism on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
when equipping
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
with the
*
-operation such that
X^{*}=-X
for
Z\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}(\pi))
with the partially defined
*
-operation defined from formal adjoints on
\mathcal{S}(\pi)
.

The reader should beware that the Hilbert space adjoint of elements in
\pi(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}))
is generally different from its formal adjoint on
\mathcal{S}(\pi)
, see more in Remark
0.20.5
. We return to study how Proposition
0.19.1
extends to differential operators on a Carnot manifold (where
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x}
for
x\in X
) below in Example
0.19.9
.

Let us now extend the construction of Proposition
0.19.1
to the symbol algebra on a Carnot manifold. In light of Proposition
0.18.7
.iii, to define a map on the symbol algebra we should first define it on the space of fibrewise tempered distributions. For a point
x\in X
, We write

\Sigma^{m}_{H}(x;E_{1,x},E_{2,x})\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(T_{H}X_{x};E_{1,x},E_{2,x})/P^{m}_{H}(T_{H}X_{x};E_{1,x},E_{2,x}),

for the homogeneous elements of degree
m
. Restriction to a point induces a mapping

\sigma_{x}^{m}:\Sigma^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})\to\Sigma^{m}_{H}(x;E_{1,x},E_{2,x}),

that respects products and adjoints. For an
x\in X
and a unitary representation
\pi:T_{H}X_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
, we define

\displaystyle\pi:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(T_{H}X_{x};E_{1,x},E_{2,x})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{1,x},\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{2,x}),

\displaystyle\pi(k)(\pi(f)v):=\pi(k*f)v\quad\mbox{for}\;f\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(T_{H}X_{x};E_{1,x}),\;v\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}.

By the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
,
\pi(a)=0
if
a\in P^{m}_{H}(T_{H}X;E_{1},E_{2})
so
\pi
descends to a morphism

\pi:\Sigma^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{1,x},\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{2,x}).

Next, we define the represented symbol of a Heisenberg operator. It will be considered as a family of operators parametrized by the set
\widehat{T_{H}X}
of all irreducible unitary representations of the osculating Lie groupoid.

Definition 0.19.2
.

For
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
,
x\in X
and a unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
, we define the represented symbol (in
(x,\pi)
) as

\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m}(D):=\pi(\sigma_{x}^{m}(D))\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{1,x},\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes E_{2,x}).

Proposition 0.19.3
.

Let
X
be a Carnot manifold,
x\in X
and
\pi
a unitary representation of
T_{H}X_{x}
.
The represented symbol satisfies the following. For
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{2},E_{3})
and
D^{\prime}\in\Psi_{H}^{m^{\prime}}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
it holds that

\displaystyle\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D^{*})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)^{*};

(40)

\displaystyle\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m+m^{\prime}}(DD^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m^{\prime}}(D^{\prime});

(41)

as operators on
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)
where the adjoint is formally defined in the inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
. Moreover, if
m\leq 0
and
\pi
does not weakly contain the trivial representation, then
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)
extends to a bounded linear operator

\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D):\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{2,x}\to\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{3,x}.

Proof.

The formulas (
40
) and (
41
) are readily verified from the fact that the representation is defined in terms of convolution.

For
m=0
in the last statement, we recall
[
66
, Theorem 8.18]
, extending
[
98
, Theorem 1]
, ensuring that
\sigma^{0}_{x}(D)
acts as a bounded operator
L^{2}(T_{H}X_{x},E_{1,x})\to L^{2}(T_{H}X_{x},E_{2,x})
. Boundedness of
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)
follows. For
m<0
in the last statement, we use
[
47
, Theorem 2]
(reviewed below in Theorem
0.19.21
). By taking a strictly positive even order differential operator
\mathfrak{D}
(see for instance Example
0.19.14
below) with
\mathfrak{D}^{s}\in\Psi_{H}^{s\tilde{m}}(X;E_{2})
for some
\tilde{m}\in\mathbb{N}
and all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
D=D_{0}\mathfrak{D}^{m/\tilde{m}}
with
D_{0}=D_{0}\mathfrak{D}^{-m/\tilde{m}}\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X;E_{2},E_{3})
by Proposition
0.18.7
. By (
41
),
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)=\sigma_{x,\pi}^{0}(D_{0})\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}^{m/\tilde{m}})
. The first factor
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{0}(D_{0})
has a bounded extension by the preceding paragraph. By using
[
47
, Theorem 2]
, we see that
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(\mathfrak{D})
is invertible and therefore the second factor has a bounded extension being a negative power of an invertible operator.
∎

Definition 0.19.4
.

An element
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
satisfies the Rockland condition if
\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m}(D)

is injective for every
x\in X
and every non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi\in\widehat{T_{H}X}_{x}
.

An element
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
is
H
-elliptic if
\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D)

is bijective for every
x\in X
and every non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi\in\widehat{T_{H}X_{x}}
. If
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
lifts an
H
-elliptic
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
, we also say that
\mathbb{D}
is
H
-elliptic.

Similarly, if
\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\to X
are graded vector bundles (cf. Subsubsection
0.18.1
), we say that
D\in\Psi_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
satisfies the graded Rockland condition/is graded
H
-elliptic if the represented graded symbols
\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m}(D)

is injective/bijective for every
x\in X
and every non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi\in\widehat{T_{H}X}_{x}
.

The term
H
-elliptic is justified below in Theorem
0.19.7
. We give the following variation of an important result concerning the Rockland condition from
[
46
]
which extends a result from
[
36
]
.

Lemma 0.19.5
.

Let
X
be a Carnot manifold,
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
two vector bundles and
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
. Then the following are equivalent:

i)

D
satisfies the Rockland condition.

ii)

There exists a
b\in\Sigma^{-m}_{H}(X;E_{2},E_{1})
such that

b\sigma^{m}(D)=1.

iii)

There exists an
R\in\Psi^{-m}_{H}(X;E_{2},E_{1})
such that

RD-1\in\Psi^{-1}_{H}(X;E_{1}).

Furthermore, if
m=0
, the conditions above are equivalent to that for any
x\in X
and any non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
, the bounded linear operator

\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D):\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{1,x}\to\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{2,x},

is an injection of Hilbert spaces, in which case it also has closed range.

Proof.

The implication i)
\Rightarrow
ii) is found in
[
46
, Lemma 3.11]
and the converse is immediate. The equivalence of item ii) and iii) follows from Proposition
0.18.7
. The last statement follows from Proposition
0.19.3
.
∎

The contents of Lemma
0.19.5
also extends to the graded setting. For clarity, we formulate the next two results in the graded setting.

Lemma 0.19.6
.

Let
X
be a Carnot manifold,
\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\to X
two hermitean graded vector bundles and
D\in\Psi_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
. Then the following are equivalent:

i)

D
is graded
H
-elliptic.

ii)

\sigma^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(D)\in\Sigma^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
has an inverse
b\in\Sigma^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
.

iii)

D
and
D^{*}
satisfy the graded Rockland condition.

iv)

The operator

\tilde{D}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&D\\
D^{*}&0\end{pmatrix},

satisfies the graded Rockland condition.

Furthermore, if
m=0
and
E_{1}=\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
and
E_{2}=\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
are trivially graded, the conditions above are equivalent to that for any
x\in X
and any non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
, the bounded linear operator

\sigma_{x,\pi}^{m}(D):\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{1,x}\to\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{2,x},

is an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces.

Proof.

It is clear that iii)
\Leftrightarrow
iv). If iii) holds, there are
b\in\Sigma^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
and
b^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
such that
b\sigma^{m}(D)=1
and
b^{\prime}\sigma^{m}(D^{*})=1
by Lemma
0.19.5
. It follows from Proposition
0.18.7
that
b\sigma^{m}(D)=1
and
\sigma^{m}(D)(b^{\prime})^{*}=1
, so uniqueness of inverses shows that
b=(b^{\prime})^{*}\in\Sigma^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
is an inverse to
\sigma^{m}(D)
. Therefore we have the implication iii)
\Rightarrow
ii). The implication ii)
\Rightarrow
i) is immediate. Penultimately, if i) holds, then
D
by definition satisfies the Rockland condition and surjectivity of
\pi(\sigma^{m}(D))
for all
\pi
ensures injectivity of
\pi(\sigma^{m}(D^{*}))
for all
\pi
(using Proposition
0.18.7
) so
D^{*}
satisfies the Rockland condition. Therefore i)
\Rightarrow
iii). Finally, the last condition stated for
m=0
immediately implies item iv) and follows from from item ii) using Proposition
0.19.3
.
∎

The next theorem summarizes the key features of Rockland- and
H
-elliptic operators from
[
47
, Section 3]
. We formulate it in the graded case which is proven in the same way as the ungraded case. Starting from Lemma
0.19.5
and
0.19.6
, the theorem is readily deduced from asymptotic completeness of the Heisenberg calculus.

Theorem 0.19.7
.

Let
X
be a closed Carnot manifold of homogeneous dimension
n
equipped with a volume density,
\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\to X
graded hermitean vector bundles and
m\in\mathbb{C}
. Assume that
D\in\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
.Then it holds that:

(1)

If
D
satisfies the Rockland condition, then there is a left parametrix
R\in\Psi^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
, i.e.
RD-1
is smoothing. In particular,
D
is hypoelliptic and
\ker(D)\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{1})
is finite-dimensional.

(2)

If
D
is
H
-elliptic, there is a parametrix
R\in\Psi^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
, i.e.
RD-1
and
DR-1
are smoothing. In particular,
D
and
D^{*}
are hypoelliptic and
\ker(D)\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{1})
and
\ker(D^{*})\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{2})
are finite-dimensional.

In particular, for any
D\in\Psi^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
the following are equivalent:

•

D
is
H
-elliptic.

•

There is an
R_{0}\in\Psi^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
with
R_{0}D-1\in\Psi^{-1}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
and
DR_{0}-1\in\Psi^{-1}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
,

•

There is a parametrix
R\in\Psi^{-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2},\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
, i.e.
RD-1\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
and
DR-1\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
,

Remark 0.19.8
.

In the recent work
[
3
]
, the Rockland condition was proven to be equivalent to
maximal hypoellipticity
, proving a conjecture of Helffer-Nourigat. The results of
[
3
]
hold in the context of the singular Carnot structures considered in Remark
0.15.4
.

Example 0.19.9
.

To tie together Proposition
0.19.1
with Proposition
0.19.3
, let us make a computation with the represented symbol of a differential operator. Assume that
X
is a Carnot manifold and
D\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
. To simplify notation, set
n_{j}:=\mathrm{rk}(T^{-j}X/T^{-j+1}X)
and
n:=\dim(X)=\sum_{j}n_{j}
. Fix an
x\in X
and set
\mathsf{G}:=T_{H}X_{x}
. In a neighbourhood
U
of
x
, we pick a basis
\{\{X_{j,k}\}_{k=1}^{n_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{r}
of
TX
such that
\{X_{j,k}\}_{k=1}^{n_{j}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(X,T^{-j}X)
and its images in
C^{\infty}(X,T^{-j}X/T^{-j+1}X)
spans
T^{-j}X/T^{-j+1}X
in all points. For a multiindex
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}=\prod_{j}\mathbb{N}^{n_{j}}
we write
|\alpha|_{H}:=\sum_{j}j|\alpha_{j}|
and

X^{\alpha}=X_{1,1}^{\alpha_{1,1}}X_{1,2}^{\alpha_{1,2}}\cdots X_{r,n_{r}-1}^{\alpha_{r,n_{r}-1}}X_{r,n_{r}}^{\alpha_{r,n_{r}}}.

We can assume the neighbourhood
U
is small enough so that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
trivializes, set
k_{1}:=\mathrm{rk}(E_{1})
and
k_{2}:=\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})
. Therefore, in
U
we can write

D=\sum_{|\alpha|_{H}\leq m}a_{\alpha}X^{\alpha},

for some
(a_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|_{H}\leq m}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U;\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{k_{1}},\mathbb{C}^{k_{2}}))
. As in Example
0.18.5
,
\sigma^{m}(D)
is given by the convolution kernel
K\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{r}(T_{H}X;E_{1},E_{2})
that over
U
is determined by
\sum_{|\alpha|_{H}=m}a_{\alpha}X^{\alpha}
as acting only in the fibre direction. In light of Proposition
0.19.1
, we compute that for a unitary representation
\pi
we have that

\sigma^{m}_{(x,\pi)}(D)=\sum_{|\alpha|_{H}=m}a_{\alpha}(x)\pi(X^{\alpha}_{x})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{k_{1}},\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{k_{2}}),

where by construction
\{\{X_{j,k,x}\}_{k=1}^{n_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{r}
is a homogeneous basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x}
of
\mathsf{G}:=T_{H}X_{x}
.

0.19.1.
Revisiting the examples of Subsection
0.18.1

We now return to study
H
-ellipticity in the specific examples appearing above in Subsection
0.18.1
.

Example 0.19.10

(Baum-van Erp type operators)
.

We return to study Baum-van Erp type operators, and to give more precise descriptions of
H
-ellipticity of the formal expressions appearing in Subsubsection
0.18.1
. In light of the terminology of Example
0.19.9
, a Baum-van Erp type operator is an
H
-elliptic differential operator
D_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
such that each point in
X
has a neighbourhood
U
with

D_{\gamma}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}}\gamma_{j,k}\nabla_{X_{j,k}}^{m/j}+\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{m-1}(U;E_{1},E_{2}),

(42)

for some local basis
\{\{X_{j,k}\}_{k=1}^{n_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{r}
of
TX
as in Example
0.19.9
, a connection
\nabla
on
E_{1}
and a collection
\gamma=(\gamma_{j,k})_{j,k}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U;\mathrm{Hom}(E_{1},E_{2}))
such that
j|m
when
\gamma_{j,k}\neq 0
. It would be quite arduous to in general characterize when a differential operator
D_{\gamma}
satisfying the condition (
42
) is
H
-elliptic. We consider the following special case.

Assume that
X
has depth
r=2
and is equipped with a Riemannian metric. For notational simplicity, write

H:=T^{-1}X.

Let
E\to X
be a hermitean vector bundle. Consider a differential operator as in (
42
) of the form

D_{\gamma}=\Delta_{H}+T_{\gamma},

where
\Delta_{H}
is the sub-laplacian associated with the Riemannian metric and
T_{\gamma}
is a first order differential operator (first order in the classical sense). Write
\mathfrak{t}_{H}X=\mathfrak{t}_{H,-1}X\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{H,-2}X=H\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{H,-2}X
and identify

\mathfrak{t}_{H,-2}X^{*}=(TX/T^{-1}X)^{*}=H^{\perp}.

Define the fibrewise linear polynomial

\gamma:=\sigma^{1}(T_{\gamma})|_{H^{\perp}}\in C^{\infty}(H^{\perp},\mathrm{End}(E)).

A short argument shows that
\sigma^{2}_{H}(D_{\gamma})
is determined by the metric on
H
and
\gamma
. For notational simplicity, we write a graded orthonormal local basis for
TX
as
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n_{1}}
,
Z_{1},\ldots Z_{n_{2}}
where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n_{1}}
is an orthonormal basis of
H
and
Z_{1},\ldots Z_{n_{2}}
induces an orthonormal basis for
TX/H
. The order
2
differential operator
D_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2}(X;E)
will in each coordinate chart look like

D_{\gamma}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}X_{j}^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{n_{2}}\gamma_{l}Z_{l}+\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{1}(X;E),

(43)

for the collection
(\gamma_{l})_{l=1}^{n_{2}}
of smooth
\mathrm{End}(E)
-valued sections determined by

\gamma=\sigma^{1}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n_{2}}\gamma_{l}Z_{l}\right)\bigg|_{H^{\perp}}.

In other words, up to lower order terms, the term
\sum_{l=1}^{n_{2}}\gamma_{l}Z_{l}
is determined by the restriction of its ordinary symbol to cotangent vectors annihilating
H=T^{-1}X
. Let us give result characterizing those
\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{t}_{H,-2}X^{*},\mathrm{End}(E))
for which
D_{\gamma}
is
H
-elliptic and as such defines a Baum-van Erp type operator.

For the flat orbits, we introduce some further notations. Write
\Gamma_{X}
for the set of flat orbits of the osculating Lie groupoid, and
p_{\Gamma}:\Gamma_{X}\to X
for the projection. If
X
is
\boldsymbol{F}
-regular,
\Gamma_{X}
is a fibre bundle but in general it is just a topological space (possibly empty). The vector bundle
\Xi_{X}=p_{\Gamma}^{*}H\to\Gamma_{X}
carries a symplectic structure defined from the Kirillov form
\omega
. Relative to a metric on
H
, we can associate a new metric
g_{\omega}
on
\Xi_{X}
and a complex structure
J_{\omega}
on
\Xi_{X}
. Using the auxiliary metric, we can identify
g_{\omega}
with a symmetric complex linear endomorphism of
\Xi_{X}
and
\omega
with an anti-symmetric complex antilinear endomorphism of
\Xi_{X}
. The two are related by
g_{\omega}=|\omega|=\sqrt{-\omega^{2}}
. We define the endomorphism

s_{k}(g_{\omega}):\Xi_{X}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k}\to\Xi_{X}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k},

(44)

on the
k
:th symmetric tensor power, from

s_{k}(g_{\omega})(v_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}v_{k}):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}g_{\omega}v_{j}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}v_{k}.

Theorem 0.19.11
.

Let
X
be a Carnot manifold of depth
2
defined from a sub-bundle
H=T^{-1}X
with a Riemannian metric
g
and
D_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2}(X;E)
as in Equation (
43
). Let
\mathcal{L}:H\wedge H\to TX/H
denote the Levi bracket and
\omega_{\xi}:=\xi\circ\mathcal{L}
the associated antisymmetric
2
-form on
H
. Then
D_{\gamma}
is
H
-elliptic if and only if for any
x\in X
, and
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{H,-2}X^{*}_{x}\setminus\{0\}=H_{x}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
the following holds

•

If
\omega_{\xi}
has rank
k
, for some
k<n_{1}
, then
\lambda+\gamma(\xi)
is invertible for all real
\lambda
with
|\lambda|\geq\mathrm{Tr}(|\omega_{\xi}|)/2
, where
|\omega_{\xi}|
is defined from polar decomposition of
\omega_{\xi}
relative to the metric on
H
.

•

If
\omega_{\xi}
has full rank (i.e. is a symplectic form on
H
), then

\displaystyle s_{k}(g_{\omega}(\xi))+\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(|\omega_{\xi}|)}{2}+\gamma(\xi):\Xi_{X}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k}\otimes E_{x}\to\Xi_{X}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k}\otimes E_{x},

(45)

also have the full ranks for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

The proof of the proposition is in spirit to reduce to rays in the representation space and there use that the sub-Laplacian on the Heisenberg group has a well understood represented symbol. The characterization of Theorem
0.19.11
is by
[
85
, Theorem 1.1]
equivalent to hypoellipticity with loss of one derivative.

Proof.

Fix an
x\in X
. We write
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x}
and
\mathsf{G}:=T_{H}X_{x}
. We need to characterize when
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi)}(D_{\gamma})
is bijective for a non-trivial representations
\pi\in\hat{\mathsf{G}}
. Write
\pi_{\xi}
for a unitary representation associated with
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
via the orbit method. Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
is a subspace of the centre of
\mathfrak{g}
. If
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
then
\pi_{\xi}
is a character and so
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma})=\sum_{j}(\xi(X_{j}))^{2}>0
is bijective. We can therefore assume that
\xi|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-2}}\neq 0
.

If
\omega_{\xi}=\xi\circ\mathcal{L}=0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is abelian and

\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma})=\sum_{j}(\xi(X_{j}))^{2}+\gamma(\xi).

Using that
\gamma
is linear in
\xi
, we see that
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma})
is bijective for all
\xi
if and only if
\lambda+\gamma(\xi)
is invertible for all
\lambda\geq 0
and all
\xi\neq 0
. Similarly,
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma}^{*})
is bijective for all
\xi
if and only if
\lambda-\gamma(\xi)
is invertible for all
\lambda\geq 0
and all
\xi\neq 0
.

For the remainder of the proof, consider a
\xi
with
\xi|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-2}}\neq 0
and
\omega_{\xi}\neq 0
. Consider the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\xi}:=(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})_{\mathbb{R}\omega_{\xi}}
(cf. Subsection
0.9.2
for notation) and grade
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\xi}
by declaring the natural quotient map
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\xi}
to be graded. Define the model operator

L_{\gamma,\xi}:=-\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}X_{j}^{2}-i\gamma(\xi)Z\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x}),

where
Z
denotes the canonical basis for
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{-2}
. Note that
L_{\gamma,\xi}^{*}=L_{-\gamma^{*},\xi}
. It is clear that
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma})
and
\sigma^{2}_{(x,\pi_{\xi})}(D_{\gamma}^{*})
are injective if and only if
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})
and
\pi(L_{-\gamma^{*},\xi})
are injective for the non-abelian representations
\pi
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\xi}
defined from
\xi
. Pick a complex structure on
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
compatible with
\omega_{\xi}
and the Riemannian metric. Using the associated polarization of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, we can write
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})
as the densely defined operator

\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})=H_{d,\omega_{\xi}}+\lambda_{0}(\xi)+\gamma(\xi),

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},E_{x})
where
2d
is the rank of
\omega_{\xi}
,
H_{d,\omega_{\xi}}
is the harmonic oscillator defined from the metric and the symplectic form on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{-1}/\mathrm{Ann}(\omega_{\xi})
and
\lambda_{0}(\xi)
is a homogeneous degree two polynomial in the projection of
\xi
onto
\mathrm{Ann}(\omega_{\xi})
.

The bottom of the spectrum of
H_{d,\omega_{\xi}}
is
\mathrm{Tr}(|\omega_{\xi}|)/2
. If
k<n_{1}
, i.e.
\omega_{\xi}
is degenerate, using that
\gamma
is linear in
\xi
and
\lambda_{0}
is quadratic, we see that
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})
is injective for all
\xi
if and only if
\lambda+\gamma(\xi)
is invertible for all
\lambda\geq\mathrm{Tr}(|\omega_{\xi}|)/2
for all
\xi
. Therefore
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})
and
\pi(L_{-\gamma^{*},\xi})
are injective for all
\xi
if and only if
\lambda+\gamma(\xi)
is invertible for all real
\lambda
with
|\lambda|\geq\mathrm{Tr}(|\omega_{\xi}|)/2
.

If
\omega_{\xi}
has full rank, so
2d=\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
,
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})=H_{d,\omega_{\xi}}+\gamma(\xi)
. Therefore, injectivity of
\pi(L_{\gamma,\xi})
and
\pi(L_{-\gamma^{*},\xi})
is equivalent to the condition (
45
) by the standard construction with creation/annihilation operators and regularity theory in the Shubin calculus on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(cf.
[
139
, Chapter IV]
).
∎

Remark 0.19.12
.

Let
D_{\gamma}
be as in Theorem
0.19.11
and assume that
D_{\gamma}
is
H
-elliptic. It follows from Theorem
0.19.11
that if the Levi bracket of a step
2
Carnot manifold is non-zero but degenerate in all points, there is a smooth homotopy from
D_{\gamma}
to a positive differential operator through
H
-elliptic operators. The structure of the characterization in Theorem
0.19.11
indicates that the index theory of operators of the form
D_{\gamma}
is found in the flat orbits, i.e. when the form
\omega_{\xi}
induced from the Kirillov form is non-degenerate after quotienting out the center so the represented symbol of
D_{\gamma}
has discrete spectrum.

Example 0.19.13

(Dirac type operators)
.

Consider a Dirac type operator
\not{D}_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathfrak{g}_{r}}i\gamma_{j}X_{j}
, for a basis
X_{1},\ldots X_{\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})}
and Clifford matrices
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})}
, as in Subsubsection
0.18.1
. By the argument in
[
112
, Example 1.6.3]
, the operator
\not{D}_{H}
is not
H
-elliptic unless we are dealing with a step
1
group so
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
.

Let us give more details describing the failure of
H
-ellipticity in the special case of the three dimensional Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. Write
X,Y,Z
for the standard basis such that
[X,Y]=Z
graded as
\mathfrak{h}_{1,-1}=\mathbb{R}X+\mathbb{R}Y
and
\mathfrak{h}_{1,-2}=\mathbb{R}Z
. In a suitable choice of Clifford matrices,

\not{D}_{H}=\begin{pmatrix}0&X+iY\\
-X+iY&0\end{pmatrix}.

The operator
\not{D}_{H}
is formally self-adjoint, so by Lemma
0.19.6
the operator
\not{D}_{H}
is
H
-elliptic if and only if it satisfies the Rockland condition, which readily is seen to be equivalent to
\not{D}_{H}^{2}
satisfying the Rockland condition. We compute that

\not{D}_{H}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-X^{2}-Y^{2}+iZ&0\\
0&-X^{2}-Y^{2}-iZ\end{pmatrix}.

So in the Schrödinger representations parametrized by
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, defined from
\xi_{\hbar}\in\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
given by
\xi_{\hbar}(xX+yY+zZ):=\hbar z
, it holds that

\pi_{\hbar}(\not{D}_{H}^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}H_{\hbar}-\hbar&0\\
0&H_{\hbar}+\hbar\end{pmatrix},

where
H_{\hbar}
is the harmonic oscillator whose spectrum is
(2\mathbb{N}+1)|\hbar|
, so injectivity of
\pi_{\hbar}(\not{D}_{H}^{2})
fails in the upper left corner for
\hbar>0
and in the lower right corner for
\hbar<0
.

A potential fix for this problem is to correct
\not{D}_{H}
by the failure of the Rockland condition. As we shall see in the Example
0.19.18
below, there is a self-adjoint order
0
Heisenberg operator
P
whose represented symbol
p_{\hbar}:=\pi_{\hbar}(P)
is the projection onto the ground state of
H_{\hbar}
for
\hbar\neq 0
, respectively, so for
\varepsilon>0
small enough the operator

\not{D}_{\varepsilon,H}=\begin{pmatrix}0&X+iY+\varepsilon\sqrt{-X^{2}-Y^{2}}P\\
-X+iY+\varepsilon\sqrt{-X^{2}-Y^{2}}P&0\end{pmatrix},

is
H
-elliptic. Here
\sqrt{-X^{2}-Y^{2}}
can made sense of using results of
[
47
]
on complex powers in the Heisenberg calculus, or
[
126
]
, reviewed below as Theorem
0.19.21
. These type of constructions were considered more generally in
[
77
]
. In combination with commutator estimates, these ideas were applied in
[
72
]
to realize the Carnot-Caratheodory distance on contact manifolds as a Gromov-Hausdorff limit of a sequence Connes’ spectral distances associated with a family of spectral triples defined from
H
-elliptic operators.

Example 0.19.14

(A positive even order Heisenberg differential operator)
.

Consider a hermitean vector bundle
E\to X
over a compact Carnot manifold
X
with a volume density and the differential operator

\mathfrak{D}:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j}}(\nabla_{X_{j,k}}^{*})^{r!/j}\nabla_{X_{j,k}}^{r!/j}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2(r!)}(X;E),

from Subsubsection
0.18.1
. Recall that the vector fields span the oscullating Lie groupoid in all points, and are choose so that for
x\in X

\sigma^{2(r!)}_{x}(\mathfrak{D})=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j}}(-1)^{r!/j}X_{j,k,x}^{2(r!)/j}\in\mathcal{U}_{2(r!)}(\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x})\otimes\mathrm{End}(E_{x}).

Let us prove that
\mathfrak{D}
is
H
-elliptic. Since
\mathfrak{D}
is formally self-adjoint, it suffices to prove the Rockland condition by Lemma
0.19.6
. Fix
x\in X
and choose a non-trivial representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
. Take the smallest
r_{0}\leq r
such that
\pi
vanishes on the image of
T_{x}X/T^{-r_{0}}_{x}X
in
\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x}
. After re-ordering, we can assume that
\pi(X_{r_{0},1})
acts as a non-zero imaginary scalar
i\lambda
. In particular,

\sigma^{2(r!)}_{(x,\pi)}(\mathfrak{D})=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{0}-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j}}(-1)^{r!/j}X_{j,k,x}^{2(r!)/j}+\sum_{k=2}^{N_{j}}(-1)^{r!/r_{0}}X_{r_{0},k,x}^{2(r!)/r_{0}}+\lambda^{2(r!)/r_{0}}.

We have for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(\pi)
that

\displaystyle\langle\sigma^{2(r!)}_{(x,\pi)}(\mathfrak{D})\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r_{0}-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j}}\|X_{j,k,x}^{(r!)/j}\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{N_{j}}\|X_{r_{0},k,x}^{(r!)/r_{0}}\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}^{2}+\lambda^{2(r!)/r_{0}}\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lambda^{2(r!)/r_{0}}\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}^{2}.

Since
\lambda^{2(r!)/r_{0}}>0
,
\sigma^{2(r!)}_{(x,\pi)}(\mathfrak{D})
is injective. Therefore
\mathfrak{D}
is
H
-elliptic. We conclude the following upon adding a positive constant to
\mathfrak{D}
.

Proposition 0.19.15
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold of depth
r
with a fixed volume density and
E\to X
a hermitean vector bundle. Then for any
m
with
r!|m
, there exists a positive even order
H
-elliptic differential operator
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2m}(X;E)
such that

\langle\mathfrak{D}\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(X;E)}\geq\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(X;E)}^{2},\;\mbox{ for all $\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X;E)$}.

Example 0.19.16

(BGG)
.

In Subsubsection
0.18.1
above we discussed the BGG-complex and its generalizations in
[
28
,
46
]
.
These notions were abstracted in Definition
0.18.11
to that of a graded
H
-(almost-)complex. We now consider the corresponding notion of
H
-ellipticity.

Definition 0.19.17
.

Consider a graded
H
-almost-complex

\displaystyle 0\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\xrightarrow{D_{1}}

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{D_{2}}\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\xrightarrow{D_{N-1}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\xrightarrow{D_{N}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\to 0,

of order
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
. We say that this is a
graded Rockland sequence
if for any
x\in X
and non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
, we have that

\mathrm{im}(\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{j}}(D_{j}))\subseteq\ker(\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{j+1}}(D_{j+1})),

is dense for all
j
. Here we consider the represented symbols as operators
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\boldsymbol{E}_{j,x}\to\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\boldsymbol{E}_{j+1,x}
.

It was proven in
[
46
]
that the two sequences appearing in Example
0.18.12
above are graded Rockland sequences. Therefore (curved) BGG-complexes are examples of graded Rockland sequences.

Example 0.19.18

(Szegö projections)
.

Let us describe the Szegö projections discussed in Subsubsection
0.18.1
further. Albeit the Szegö projection was originally defined on boundaries of domains in complex manifolds, and the existence in that case is a bit subtle, one can consider more general situations.

For instance,
[
65
, Chapter 6]
considered generalized Szegö projections on a co-orientable contact manifold
X
. The co-orientability ensures the existence of complex structures on
H
, cf. Example
0.16.5
. A generalized Szegö projection at level
N
is a projection
P\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X,\mathbb{C})
such that in some complex structure on
H
one has that
\sigma_{0}(P)\in\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X,\mathbb{C})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{r}(T_{H}X)
is represented in the Fock space bundle
\mathpzc{F}_{X}\cong\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{\Gamma}^{*}H^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k}
as the orthogonal projection onto

(\mathbb{R}_{-}\times X\times\{0\})\dot{\cup}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}H^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}k}\right)\subseteq\mathpzc{F}_{X},

where we have trivialized
\Gamma_{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times X
. Examples of generalized Szegö projections at level
N
were considered in
[
22
, Chapter 15.3]
and further studied in
[
64
]
in terms of solution spaces to a higher Cauchy-Riemann equation

\underbrace{\bar{\partial}_{T^{*}X^{\otimes N}}\circ\cdots\circ\bar{\partial}_{T^{*}X}\circ\bar{\partial}}_{N+1\mbox{ times}}u=0,

in
\Omega
which is a strictly pseudoconvex domain with boundary
X
.

We remark that an alternative description of a generalized Szegö projection is as projections
P\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X,\mathbb{C})
whose symbol is represented in a fibre of
\mathpzc{F}_{X}\cong\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{\Gamma}^{*}H^{*\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}}
over a point
(x,\xi)\in\Gamma_{X}
as a finite rank spectral projection of the image of
\sigma_{2}(\Delta_{H})
in the representation, for the sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H}
defined from the contact structure, the complex structure and a choice of volume density. Based on this observation one could on a Carnot manifold
X
and for an
H
-elliptic operator
D
, talk about generalized Szegö projectors (with respect to
D
) as projections
P\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X,\mathbb{C})
whose symbol commutes with
\sigma^{m}(D_{\gamma})
and as a multiplier of
C^{*}(T_{H}X)
in each fibrewise representation of the osculating Lie groupoid is a finite rank spectral projector for the represented image of
\sigma^{m}(D)
.

For the sake of keeping the discussion concrete, we restrict the discussion to step
2
Carnot manifolds. We use the notation
H=T^{-1}X
. Let us recall the following result of van Erp
[
148
]
. For a step
2
Carnot manifold, we can define the ideal of Hermite operators
\mathcal{I}_{H}^{m}(X;\mathbb{C})\subseteq\Psi^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
as those operators whose symbol vanish on
H^{\perp}
.

Theorem 0.19.19
.

Assume that
X
is a step
2
Carnot manifold. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an idempotent
P\in\Psi^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
with
\sigma_{H}^{0}(P)\neq 0,1
.

(2)

There exists an idempotent in
\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
which is neither
0
nor
1
.

(3)

There exists an idempotent
P\in\mathcal{I}_{H}^{m}(X;\mathbb{C})
with
\sigma_{H}^{0}(P)\neq 0,1
.

(4)

X
is polycontact (see Example
0.16.7
).

If
X
is poly-contact, for any idempotent
P\in\Psi^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
either
P\in\mathcal{I}_{H}^{m}(X;\mathbb{C})
or
1-P\in\mathcal{I}_{H}^{m}(X;\mathbb{C})
and any idempotent in
\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
lifts to an idempotent in
\Psi^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
.

This theorem from
[
148
]
shows that in the step
2
case, it is natural to assume the Carnot manifold to be polycontact. To give further examples, we restrict to regular polycontact manifolds. Let
X
be a regular polycontact manifold of type
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
and let
H=T^{-1}X\subseteq TX
denote the contact structure. We have that
\mathfrak{g}_{-2}=\mathfrak{z}
is the centre and
\mathfrak{g}
admits flat orbits since the Kirillov form
\omega_{\xi}
is non-degenerate for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{z}
. See more in Example
0.16.7
and
0.16.8
. We can choose a complex structure on
p_{\Gamma}^{*}H\to\Gamma_{X}
adapted to the Kirillov form
\omega
(defined by
\omega_{\xi}:=\xi\circ\mathcal{L}
on
p_{\Gamma}^{*}H_{\xi}
). Moreover, the complex structure and the Kirillov form induces a Fock space bundle
\mathpzc{F}_{X}\to\Gamma_{X}
(cf. Example
0.16.8
) with
\mathpzc{F}_{X}\cong\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{\Gamma}^{*}H^{\otimes^{*\rm sym}_{\mathbb{C}}n}
. Given
N\geq 0
, consider the projection
p_{N}\in\mathcal{M}(I_{X})
defined the projection onto the direct summand

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}p_{\Gamma}^{*}H^{\otimes^{*\rm sym}_{\mathbb{C}}n}\subseteq\mathpzc{F}_{X}.

If there exists a projection
P\in\Psi^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
such that
\sigma^{0}_{(x,\pi)}(P)=\pi(p_{N,x})
for any
x\in X
and any flat representation
\pi
, then
P
is a generalized Szegö projection; we say that such a generalized Szegö is of level
N
. While our construction of
p_{N}
formally makes sense for more general pluricontact manifolds, it can fail that
p_{N,+}\in\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
. For instance,
p_{N}\notin\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
when
\mathfrak{g}=\mathbb{H}_{n_{1}}\times\mathbb{H}_{n_{2}}
and so when
X
is a product of contact manifolds.

Szegö projections on co-oriented contact manifolds

We describe the situation for co-oriented contact manifolds in full detail. Let
X
be a
2n+1
-dimensional co-oriented contact manifold. We write coordinates in the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}_{n}
as
(z,t)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
with the product

(z,t)(w,s)=(z+w,s+t+\mathrm{Im}\langle z,w\rangle).

In this convention, the complex structure associated with the Kirillov form and the standard (real) metric on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is the standard complex structure on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In this case, a computation with the Fourier transform (cf.
[
140
, Chapter XII]
) shows that a Szegö projection on
X
has Heisenberg symbol
p_{0}\in\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
which in local coordinates takes the form of the
0
-homogeneous distributional density

p_{0}(z,t):=c\frac{|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t|}{(it+|z|^{2})^{n+1}},

for a suitable dimensional constant
c
. As a homogeneous distribution,
p_{0}
is uniquely determined outside
(z,t)=0
. The behaviour of
p_{0}
at
(z,t)=0
is defined from a limiting procedure in
t
in the upper complex halfplane; this definition of
p_{0}
at
(z,t)=0
ensures that
\pi(p_{0})=0
for
\pi\in\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{+}
. By homogeneity arguments, a generalized Szegö projection of level
N
(in the sense of Epstein-Melrose
[
65
]
) has the Heisenberg symbol
p_{N}\in\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
of the form

p_{N}(x,z,t):=\frac{q_{N}(t,z)|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t|}{(it+|z|^{2})^{n+N+1}},

for a homogeneous polynomial
q_{N}
of degree
N
. The reader should be aware that these are pointwise expressions in the sense that they implicitly depend on the isomorphism
T_{H}X_{x}\cong\mathbb{H}_{n}
.

Szegö projections on general contact manifolds

Consider now a
2n+1
-dimensional contact manifold
X
, but we make no assumption on co-orientability. In particular, the bundle
H^{\perp}\to X
need not be oriented and
H
need not allow a complex structure. Also in this case, a computation with the Fourier transform as above shows that there is a Szegö projection on
X
, constructed using Theorem
0.19.19
, from a Heisenberg symbol
p_{tw}\in\Sigma^{0}_{H}(X;\mathbb{C})
that in local coordinates takes the form of the
0
-homogeneous distributional density

p_{tw}(z,t):=c\frac{|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t|}{(it+|z|^{2})^{n+1}}+c\frac{|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t|}{(-it+|z|^{2})^{n+1}}.

for a suitable dimensional constant
c
. As a homogeneous distribution,
p_{tw}
is uniquely determined outside
(z,t)=0
and the behaviour of
p_{tw}
at
(z,t)=0
is defined from a suitable limiting procedure. A suitable limiting procedure ensures that
p_{tw}
acts as the projection onto the ground state bundle
X\times\mathbb{C}=(p_{\Gamma}^{*}H)^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}0}\subseteq\mathpzc{F}_{X}
. Geometrically, a lift
P\in\Psi_{H}^{0}(X;\mathbb{C})
could modulo lower order terms for
X
being the boundary of a strictly pseudoconvex domain be interpreted as the projection onto the direct sum of the spaces of functions with a holomorphic extension and anti-holomorphic extension, respectively, to the interior of the domain.

Szegö projections on regular Carnot manifolds of type
\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}

Consider the polycontact manifold of type
\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}
constructed as in Example
0.16.7
from a compact complex manifold
X
and a complex subbundle
H\subseteq TX
of complex codimension
1
such that the the Levi bracket
H\times H\to TX/H
is non-degenerate. Write
2n+1=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
. We write coordinates on
\mathbb{H}_{n,\mathbb{C}}
as
(z,t)\in\mathbb{C}^{2n}\times\mathbb{C}
. A computation with the Fourier transform (in the central direction) and homogeneity considerations shows that the that the projection
p_{\mathbb{C}}
onto the ground state bundle
X\times\mathbb{C}=(p_{\Gamma}^{*}H)^{\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm sym}0}\subseteq\mathpzc{F}_{X}
is an element
p_{\mathbb{C}}\in\Sigma_{H}^{0}(X;\mathbb{C})
that in local coordinates takes the form

p_{\mathbb{C}}(z_{1},z_{2},t):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}c_{k}|z|^{2}|t|^{2k}\frac{|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t|}{(|t|^{2}+|z|^{4})^{n+k-3/2}},

for suitable dimensional constants
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
. The geometric interpretation of the idempotent
p_{\mathbb{C}}
is less clear, but at a symbolic level
p_{\mathbb{C}}
is the projection onto the kernel of the symbol of an operator of the form

\Box_{H}=(\bar{\partial}_{H}^{*}\bar{\partial}_{H})^{2}-T^{*}T,

where
\bar{\partial}_{H}:C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(X;(H^{0,1})^{*})
is the
\bar{\partial}
-operator restricted to
H
and
T
is a suitable vector field transversal to
H
.

0.19.2.
Analytic properties of
H
-elliptic operators

To study the analytic properties of
H
-elliptic operators with ease, we will describe their actions on a natural scale of Sobolev spaces on a Carnot manifold. Such Sobolev spaces has been studied in for instance
[
46
]
. We model the setup close to that of an ordinary manifold in
[
139
]
. First, we recall the following result from
[
46
]
.

Proposition 0.19.20

(Proposition 3.9
[
46
]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
hermitean vector bundles and
m\in\mathbb{R}
. For
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
it holds that

(1)

If
\mathfrak{R}(m)\leq 0
,
D
is bounded in
L^{2}
-norm and extends uniquely to a bounded operator
D:L^{2}(X,E_{1})\rightarrow L^{2}(X,E_{2})
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{R}(m)<0
, then we have a compact operator
D:L^{2}(X,E_{1})\rightarrow L^{2}(X,E_{2})
.

(3)

If
\mathfrak{R}(m)<-\dim_{h}(X)
, then
D:L^{2}(X,E_{1})\rightarrow L^{2}(X,E_{2})
is trace class.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold equipped with a volume density,
E\to X
a hermitean vector bundle and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Following
[
46
]
, we define the Sobolev space

W^{s}_{H}(X;E):=\{f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X;E):Af\in L^{2}(X;E)\quad\forall A\in\Psi^{s}_{H}(X;E)\}.

In
[
46
]
, this space was denoted by
H^{s}(E)
. Using similar constructions as in
[
139
, Chapter I]
, one can equip
W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
with a Hilbert space structure (albeit a rather non-canonical one). For a graded hermitean vector bundle
\boldsymbol{E}\to X
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the graded Sobolev space

\boldsymbol{W}^{s}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}):=\bigoplus_{j}W^{s-j}_{H}(X;\boldsymbol{E}[j]).

By construction, the actions of Heisenberg pseudo-differential operators

\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})\times W^{s}_{H}(X;E_{1})\to W^{s-m}_{H}(X;E_{2}),

and graded Heisenberg pseudo-differential operators

\Psi_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\times\boldsymbol{W}^{s}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\to\boldsymbol{W}^{s-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2}),

are continuous. We shall make use of another description of Sobolev spaces, based on complex powers. We recall the following result from
[
47
]
.

Theorem 0.19.21

(
[
47
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density and
E\to X
a hermitean vector bundle. Suppose that
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2m}(X;E)
is a positive even order
H
-elliptic differential operator.
Then
\mathfrak{D}
is essentially self-adjoint on
L^{2}(X;E)
and for
z\in\mathbb{C}
the complex power
\mathfrak{D}^{-z}
is a Heisenberg pseudodifferential operator of order
-zm
.

The reader can find more details on Heisenberg pseudodifferential operator of complex order in
[
47
, Section 6]
. Only real powers will be used in this monograph. We remark that the essential self-adjointness of
\mathfrak{D}
is automatic since
\mathfrak{D}
is positive in form sense on
C^{\infty}(X;E)
. Therefore, the existence of the complex powers
\mathfrak{D}^{-z}
is ensured by the spectral theorem. The importance of Theorem
0.19.21
is that the complex powers can be constructed inside the Heisenberg calculus. The existence of operators
\mathfrak{D}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
0.19.21
is ensured by Proposition
0.19.15
.

Proposition 0.19.22
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E\to X
a hermitean vector bundle and
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2m}(X;E)
as in Proposition
0.19.15
. Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Consider the subspace
\mathfrak{D}^{-s/2m}L^{2}(X;E)\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X;E)
viewed as a Hilbert space by declaring
\mathfrak{D}^{s/2m}:\mathfrak{D}^{-s/2m}L^{2}(X;E)\to L^{2}(X;E)
to be a unitary. Then the identity map on
C^{\infty}(X;E)
extends by continuity to a Banach space isomorphism
\mathfrak{D}^{-s/2m}L^{2}(X;E)\cong W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
.

Henceforth, we consider
W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
as a Hilbert space in the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{W^{s}_{H}(X;E)}:=\langle\mathfrak{D}^{s/2m}f,\mathfrak{D}^{s/2m}g\rangle_{L^{2}(X;E)},

for some fixed choice of
\mathfrak{D}
as in Proposition
0.19.15
of some order
m

The next result is proven as in
[
139
, Chapter I]
. It is a statement that entails the locality of the Sobolev spaces
W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
, i.e.
u\in W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
if and only if it holds in each coordinate chart.

Proposition 0.19.23
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E\to X
a hermitean vector bundle and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then for
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X;E)
it holds that
u\in W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
if and only if for any
x_{0}\in X
, there exists a neighborhood
U
of
x_{0}
and a
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
with
\varphi(x_{0})\neq 0
and
\varphi u\in W^{s}_{H}(X;E)
.

We now come to a result showing that index theory of
H
-elliptic operators is on a solid analytic footing. This type of result is standard in light of the methods above and the ideas in
[
139
, Chapter I]
, we include the statement for clarity and convenience of the reader.

Theorem 0.19.24
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
hermitean vector bundles,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
D\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
an
H
-elliptic operator. Then the following holds:

i)

The operator
D_{s}:W^{s}_{H}(X;E_{1})\to W^{s-m}_{H}(X;E_{2})
defined from
D
is a continuous Fredholm operator with
\ker(D_{s})\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{1})
independent of
s
and there exists a finite-dimensional space
V\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{2})
(independent of
s
) such that the restricted quotient
V\to W^{s-m}_{H}(X;E_{2})/DW^{s}_{H}(X;E_{1})
is an isomorphism. In particular, the Fredholm index
\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\,(D_{s})
is independent of
s
.

ii)

If
m>0
, the densely defined operator
D_{L^{2}}:L^{2}(X;E_{1})\dashrightarrow L^{2}(X;E_{2})
defined from
D
with domain
\mathrm{dom}(D_{L^{2}}):=W^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1})
is Fredholm and
\ker(D_{L^{2}})=\ker(D_{s})\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;E_{1})
for any
s
and
\mathrm{c}\mathrm{o}\mathrm{k}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{r}\,(D_{L^{2}})=\mathrm{c}\mathrm{o}\mathrm{k}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{r}\,(D^{*}_{m})
. In particular,

\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\,(D_{L^{2}})=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\,(D_{s}),

for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

iii)

The index of
D_{s}
and
D_{L^{2}}
(if
m>0
) only depends on
\sigma_{H}(D)\in\Sigma^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
.

Remark 0.19.25
.

The Fredholm theory of graded
H
-elliptic operator as operators on graded Sobolev spaces is the same as the ungraded theory. That is, if
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
D\in\Psi_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
is a graded
H
-elliptic operator, then

(1)

D_{s}:\boldsymbol{W}^{s}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\to\boldsymbol{W}^{s-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
defined from
D
is a continuous Fredholm operator with
\ker(D_{s})\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
independent of
s
and there exists a finite-dimensional space
V\subseteq C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
(independent of
s
) such that the restricted quotient
V\to\boldsymbol{W}^{s-m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})/DW^{s}_{H}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
is an isomorphism.

(2)

The Fredholm index
\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\,(D_{s})
is independent of
s
and only depends on
\sigma_{H,{\rm gr}}^{m}(D)\in\Sigma^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
.

0.20.
The action of
H
-elliptic operators on certain Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules

To study the index theory of
H
-elliptic operators, we need to better understand the action of related operators on certain Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules.

For a Carnot manifold
X
and vector bundles
E,E^{\prime}\to X
consider the space

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E):=C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X;r^{*}(E)).

There are actions by convolution

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E),

(46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X;E,E^{\prime})\times\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E^{\prime}).

If
E
is hermitean, the convolution product induces a
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-sesqui-linear inner product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}}:\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)\times\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X).

We let
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)
denote the completion of
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)
as a
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module. We note that
C_{0}[0,\infty)
act as central multipliers of
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
, indeed
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
is a
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-
C^{*}
-algebra. This fact induces a natural
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-structure on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)
and any
T\in\mathrm{End}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E))
is
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear. We remark that for
t>0
and
\pi_{X}
denoting the regular representation of
X\times X
(with respect to a fixed volume density), the localization of
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)
in the associated representation
\pi_{X,t}:C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)\to\mathbb{K}(L^{2}(X))
takes the form

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)\otimes_{\pi_{X,t}}L^{2}(X)=L^{2}(X;E).

Moreover, for
t=0
and any
x\in X
, a unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
induces a
\pi:C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)\to\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
and the localization of
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)
takes the form

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)\otimes_{\pi}\mathcal{H}_{\pi}=\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes E_{x}.

Any
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
acts by left convolution as a densely defined
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-linear operator
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1})\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2})
with domain
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E)
.

Definition 0.20.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Carnot manifold and
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
two vector bundles. An adjointable morphism
T\in\mathrm{Hom}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}),\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2}))
is said to be locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact if for any
\varphi\in C_{0}[0,\infty)
it holds that

\varphi T\in\mathbb{K}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}),\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2})).

Lemma 0.20.2
.

In the setting of the previous paragraph, with
m\leq 0
, any
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
is closable as a densely defined operator
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1})\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2})
. The closure of
\mathbb{D}
, that we by an abuse of notation denote by
\mathbb{D}
, is bounded and adjointable. Moreover, if
m<0
,
\mathbb{D}
is locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact.

Proof.

To prove that
\mathbb{D}
is closable with adjointable and bounded closure, it suffices to prove that
\mathbb{D}
is norm-bounded; in this case the Hilbert module adjoint of
\mathbb{D}
is the closure of the formal adjoint of
\mathbb{D}
. To show that
\mathbb{D}
is bounded, we note that it suffices to prove that there is a uniform norm bound on the localizations
\mathbb{D}\otimes_{\pi}1
where
\pi
ranges over all irreducible representations of
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
. The set of unitary equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
can be written as
\widehat{T_{H}X}\cup(0,\infty)
consisting of representations over
t=0
and the representations for
t>0
of the pair groupoid
X\times X
. Since
m\leq 0
, the scaling invariance and Proposition
0.19.20
gives a uniform norm bound on
(0,\infty)
. The uniform norm bound
\widehat{T_{H}X}
follows from that for each
x
,
\mathrm{ev}_{t=0}(\mathbb{D})_{x}
can be viewed as a properly supported compactly based operator on
T_{H}X_{x}
and Proposition
0.19.20
again produces a uniform estimate on
\widehat{T_{H}X}
.

To prove that
\mathbb{D}
is locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact for
m<0
, we can upon replacing
\mathbb{D}
with
(\mathbb{D}^{*}\mathbb{D})^{N}
assume that
m<<0
can be taken arbitrarily negative. We can therefore, by
[
149
, Proposition 43]
, assume that
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})\cap C^{k}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X,r^{*}E_{2}\otimes(s^{*}E_{1})^{*}\otimes|\Lambda_{r}|)
for a large
k>>0
. Now, standard approximation arguments show that we on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
, can approximate
\mathbb{D}
in norm by
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact operators so
\mathbb{D}
is locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact.
∎

We choose a
\mathfrak{D}_{E}\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{2m}(X;E)
as in Proposition
0.19.15
. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E}^{s}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{2ms}_{H}(X;E)
denote a strictly positive lift of the complex power
\mathfrak{D}_{E}^{s}\in\Psi_{H}^{2ms}(X;E)
(cf. Proposition
0.19.21
). For
s>0
we define

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}(X;E):=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E}^{-s/2m}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E).

The space
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}(X;E)
is clearly
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-invariant and becomes a
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module by declaring
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E}^{s/2m}:\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}(X;E)\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)
to be unitary. For
s<0
we can formally define
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}(X;E)
in the same way. Equivalently, but more rigorously, one can define
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}(X;E)
for
s<0
by duality using
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X;s^{*}(E))\subseteq\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)^{*}:=\mathbb{K}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
and interchange the role of the left and right actions in (
46
). The definition in the graded case is similar, for a graded hermitean vector bundle
\boldsymbol{E}\to X
we define the
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}):=\bigoplus_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s-j}(X;\boldsymbol{E}[j]).

We note that for
t>0
,

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E})\otimes_{\pi_{X,t}}L^{2}(X)=\boldsymbol{W}^{s}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}).

The operator

\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{E},s}:=\bigoplus_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{E}[j]}^{(s-j)/2m},

defines a unitary equivalence
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E})\cong\bigoplus_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}[j])
.

Proposition 0.20.3
.

Let
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
and
s\geq t+m
. Then as a densely defined operator
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{t}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
,
\mathbb{D}
is closable and its closure is bounded and adjointable. Moreover, if
s>t+m
,
\mathbb{D}:\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{t}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
is locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact.

Proof.

After replacing
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
with
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{E},t}\mathbb{D}\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{E},-s}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m+t-s}_{H}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
, we have reduced the statement to the ungraded case for
t=s=0
which was proved in Lemma
0.20.2
.
∎

Proposition 0.20.4
.

Let
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H,{\rm gr}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1},\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
,
m>0
, be graded
H
-elliptic. Take
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the closure of
\mathbb{D}
as a densely defined operator
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\to\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})
is regular with domain
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s+m}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})
. If
s=0
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
are trivially graded, the adjoint of
\mathbb{D}
is the closure of
\mathbb{D}^{*}
, and in this case, the operator

\tilde{\mathbb{D}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{D}\\
\mathbb{D}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),

is essentially selfadjoint on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
and its closure has locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact resolvent.

The reader can find further details on regularity of multipliers defined from elliptic elements in
[
125
]
.

Proof.

Using order reduction with
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{E},s}
as in the proof of Proposition
0.20.3
, we can reduce to the case that
s=0
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
are trivially graded. We write
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{T}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E_{1}}^{m}
where
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{D}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E_{1}}^{-m}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{0}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
. Since
\mathbb{D}
is
H
-elliptic, so is
\mathbb{T}
. Let
\mathbb{R}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{0}_{H}(X;E_{2},E_{1})
denote a lift of a parametrix of
\mathrm{ev}_{t=1}(\mathbb{T})
. We have that
1-\mathbb{R}\mathbb{T}\in\cap_{k}t^{k}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-k}_{H}(X;E_{1})
and
1-\mathbb{T}\mathbb{R}\in\cap_{k}t^{k}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-k}_{H}(X;E_{2})
. In partcular,
1-\mathbb{R}\mathbb{T}
and
1-\mathbb{T}\mathbb{R}
are locally compact and
0
-homogeneous modulo
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
-compact operators. We conclude that for a sequence
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{\infty}_{c}(X;E_{1})
, it holds that
\varphi_{j}\to\varphi
in
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1})
and
D\varphi_{j}
converges in
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2})
if and only if
\varphi_{j}\to\varphi
in
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E_{1}}^{m}\varphi_{j}\to\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{E_{1}}^{m}\varphi
in
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{2})
. Therefore, the closure of
\mathbb{D}
is the extension to
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{m}(X;E_{1})
. By the same argument, the closure of
\mathbb{D}^{*}
is the extension to
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{m}(X;E_{2})
. The same argument applies in any localization in a pure state of
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
, and the regularity of
\mathbb{D}
as well as the description of its adjoint follows from the local/global principle
[
94
,
125
]
. Again, the local/global principle ensures that
\tilde{\mathbb{D}}
is essentially selfadjoint. Its closure has locally
C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)
-compact resolvent since it factors over the inclusion
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{m}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})\hookrightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
.
∎

Remark 0.20.5
.

To ensure regularity, the condition on
H
-ellipticity in Proposition
0.20.4
can in general not be dropped. Consider an element
a\in\mathcal{U}_{m}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
a=a^{*}
in
\mathcal{U}_{m}(\mathfrak{g})
but for a unitary representation
\pi
of
\mathsf{G}
,
\pi(a)
fails to be essentially self-adjoint. For large classes of nilpotent Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
, the existence of such an
a
follows from
[
5
, Section 3]
. If
X
is a Carnot manifold such that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}_{H}X_{x}
for some
x
and
D\in\mathcal{DO}_{H}^{m}(X;\mathbb{C})
such that
\mathrm{ev}_{t=0}(\mathbb{D})|_{x}=a
, we claim that the closure of
\mathbb{D}
as a densely defined operator on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;\mathbb{C})
is not regular. Localizing to
t=0
and
x
, we see that it suffices to prove that the closure of
\mathrm{ev}_{t=0}(\mathbb{D})|_{x}=a
is not regular as an unbounded multiplier of
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
, for
\mathsf{G}=T_{H}X_{x}
. By
[
125
, Lemma 1.25]
, the Gårding space of
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
is a core for any element of
\mathcal{U}_{m}(\mathfrak{g})
and so
\overline{a}
is a self-adjoint multiplier of
C^{*}(\mathsf{G})
. However, for the representation
\pi
above
\pi(a)
is not essentially selfadjoint so
\pi(\overline{a})^{*}\neq\pi(\overline{a}^{*})
. By the local/global principle
[
94
,
125
]
the multiplier
\overline{a}
is not regular. Again by the local/global principle
[
94
,
125
]
, the closure of
\mathbb{D}
as a densely defined operator on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;\mathbb{C})
is not regular.

To make constructions with Rockland sequences easier we state a result characterizing the Rockland property in terms of the Hilbert modules
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}
defined above. The proof is omitted as it follows from the constructions of
[
46
, Chapter 5]
and the propositions above.

Theorem 0.20.6
.

Consider a graded
H
-almost-complex

0\to C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\xrightarrow{D_{1}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{D_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{D_{N-1}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\xrightarrow{D_{N}}C^{\infty}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\to 0,

of order
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
as in Definition
0.18.11
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

This sequence is a graded Rockland sequence.

(2)

For any
x\in X
and non-trivial irreducible unitary representation
\pi
of
T_{H}X_{x}
, we have that the sequence

\displaystyle 0\to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s_{1}}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{1})\otimes_{\pi}\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{1}}(D_{1})}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s_{2}}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{2})\otimes_{\pi}\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{2}}(D_{2})}\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\xrightarrow{\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{N-1}}(D_{N-1})}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s_{N}}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N})\otimes_{\pi}\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{(x,\pi)}^{m_{N}}(D_{N})}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{s_{N+1}}_{\rm gr}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{N+1})\otimes_{\pi}\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\to 0,

is exact, where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{N},s_{N+1}
are such that
s_{j}-s_{j+1}=m_{j}
for any
j
.

(3)

There exists
B_{j}\in\Psi_{H,{\rm gr}}^{-m_{j}}(X;\boldsymbol{E}_{j+1},\boldsymbol{E}_{j})
such that

\sigma_{H}^{-m_{j}}(B_{j})\sigma_{H}^{m_{j}}(D_{j})+\sigma_{H}^{m_{j-1}}(D_{j-1})\sigma_{H}^{-m_{j-1}}(D_{j-1})=1,

for any
j
.

Proposition 0.20.7
.

Let
X
be a closed Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
two hermitean vector bundles and
\mathbb{F}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{0}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
be an
H
-elliptic element which is unitary up to
t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}
. Consider

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{F}\\
\mathbb{F}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{0}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),

as a self-adjoint adjointable operator on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
. Then
(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),\tilde{\mathbb{F}})
is an even
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
.

If
E=E_{1}=E_{2}
and
\mathbb{F}
additionally to above is self-adjoint up to
t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}
, then
(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E),\mathbb{F})
is an odd
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
.

We remark that
\mathbb{F}
is unitary/self-adjoint up to
t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}
precisely when
\mathrm{ev}_{t=0}(\mathbb{F})
is unitary/self-adjoint up to
C^{\infty}_{c}
, i.e.
\sigma_{H}(\mathrm{ev}_{t=1}(\mathbb{F}))
is a unitary/self-adjoint in
\Sigma^{0}_{H}
.

Proof.

We only prove the statement about even Kasparov modules, the odd statement is proven similarly. We need to prove that for any
a\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,\infty)\times X)
,
a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{*}-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}),a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{2}-1),[\tilde{\mathbb{F}},a]\in\mathbb{K}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}))
. It follows from our assumptions and Corollary
0.18.9
that
a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{*}-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}),a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{2}-1),[\tilde{\mathbb{F}},a]\in t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
. Since
C_{c}[0,\infty)t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})\subseteq C_{c}[0,\infty)\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{-1}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
, the operators
a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{*}-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}),a(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{2}-1),[\tilde{\mathbb{F}},a]
are all locally compact by Lemma
0.20.2
. Using
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linearity, compactness follows after multiplying by a
\chi\in C_{c}[0,\infty)
with
\chi a=a
.
∎

To make some constructions easier, we shall make use of higher order unbounded Kasparov modules. For more details, see
[
73
, Subsection 1.5]
. The next corollary is of
crucial importance for defining
KK
-classes
from
H
-elliptic operators.

Corollary 0.20.8
.

Let
X
be a closed Carnot manifold with a fixed volume density,
E_{1},E_{2}\to X
two hermitean vector bundles and
\mathbb{D}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1},E_{2})
,
m>0
, be an
H
-elliptic element. Consider

\tilde{\mathbb{D}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{D}\\
\mathbb{D}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m}_{H}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),

as a self-adjoint regular operator on
\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})
. Then
(C^{\infty}_{c}(X\times[0,\infty)),\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),\tilde{\mathbb{D}})
is an even higher order unbounded
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
of order
m
. In particular, for the element

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{D}}\in\mathrm{End}_{C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X)}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2})),

defined by

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{D}}:=\tilde{\mathbb{D}}(1+\tilde{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{-1/2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{D}(1+\mathbb{D}^{*}\mathbb{D})^{-1/2}\\
\mathbb{D}^{*}(1+\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}^{*})^{-1/2}&0\end{pmatrix},

the pair
(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E_{1}\oplus E_{2}),\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{D}})
is an even
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
.

If
E=E_{1}=E_{2}
and
\mathbb{D}
additionally to above is self-adjoint, then
(C^{\infty}_{c}(X\times[0,\infty)),\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E),\mathbb{D})
is an odd higher order unbounded
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
(of order
m
) and the pair
(\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E),\mathbb{D}(1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-1/2})
is an odd
C_{0}[0,\infty)
-linear Kasparov module for
(C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X),C^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{H}X))
.

Proof.

As in the previous result, the even statement is proven similarly to the odd so we restrict to the latter case. It is clear that
C^{\infty}_{c}(X\times[0,\infty))
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(\mathbb{D})
and by Proposition
0.20.4
,
\mathbb{D}
has compact resolvent. It remains to prove that
[\mathbb{D},a]
is
1/m
-bounded with respect to
\mathbb{D}
(in the sense of
[
73
, Definition 1.21]
). In other words, we need to prove that
[\mathbb{D},a](1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}
and
(1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}[\mathbb{D},a]
are bounded. Using complex interpolation for the domains of the complex powers of the self-adjoint regular operator
\mathbb{D}
, cf.
[
138
, Theorem 3]
, this follows from the fact that
[\mathbb{D},a]\in C_{c}^{\infty}[0,\infty)t\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m-1}_{H}(X;E)\subseteq C_{c}^{\infty}[0,\infty)\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{m-1}_{H}(X;E)
(see Corollary
0.18.9
) and therefore
[\mathbb{D},a](1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}
factors as bounded mappings

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)\xrightarrow{(1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{m-1}(X;E)\xrightarrow{[\mathbb{D},a]}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E),

and
(1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}[\mathbb{D},a]
factors as the bounded mappings

\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E)\xrightarrow{[\mathbb{D},a]}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}^{-(m-1)}(X;E)\xrightarrow{(1+\mathbb{D}^{2})^{-(m-1)/2m}}\boldsymbol{\mathpzc{E}}(X;E).\qquad\qed

0.20.1.
Symbols and flat orbits

For regular Carnot manifolds, the osculating Lie groupoid is a locally trivial bundle of Carnot-Lie groups and we can in this case give a more detailed description of the Heisenberg symbol calculus. We later on restrict to
\boldsymbol{F}
-regular Carnot manifolds, and study
H
-ellipticity in terms of the flat orbits building on the idea that the flat orbits form a Zariski open dense subset of the representation space.

Definition 0.20.9
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a simply connected Carnot-Lie group and
P^{m}_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(\mathsf{G},|\Lambda|)
the space of polynomial densities on
\mathsf{G}
homogeneous of degree
m
. Define the Frechet
*
-spaces

\Sigma^{m}_{\mathsf{G}}:=\{k\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathsf{G})\cap C^{\infty}(\mathsf{G}\setminus\{0\},|\Lambda|)/P^{m}_{\mathsf{G}}:\lambda_{*}k=\lambda^{m}k\},

and
